```bash
  # The ACU window has a single writer entry point: gpu_fault.admin.aurora_capacity. bootstrap, the legacy
  # deploy.sh, the stress fixtures and this manual share the same create parameters -- aurora-postgresql 16.8,
  # provisioned + db.serverless, storage encryption, deletion protection, IAM auth, 7-day backups, managed master password,
  # postgresql log export, a dedicated parameter group, and the two tags Application and gpu-fault:site-id.
  PYTHONPATH=src python3.12 -m gpu_fault.admin.aurora_capacity create \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-id "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    --engine-version "${AURORA_ENGINE_VERSION}" \
    --min-acu "${AURORA_MIN_ACU}" \
    --max-acu "${AURORA_MAX_ACU}" \
    --subnet-group "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    --security-group "${AURORA_SG_ID}" \
    --parameter-group "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-pg" \
    --site-id "<site-name>"
fi
```

To change Min/Max on an existing cluster, do not run `aws rds modify-db-cluster` by hand: go through `gpu-fault-admin config`
(which internally calls the same module's `reconcile`, including the convergence wait of "3 consecutive stable observations"; see
[Administrator Capacity Configuration](administrator-capacity-configuration.md)); a legacy environment without a site can use
`python3.12 -m gpu_fault.admin.aurora_capacity reconcile --region ... --cluster-id ...
--min-acu ... --max-acu ...`, which likewise returns only after writer/reader are both back to `available`.

The bootstrap of `gpu-fault-admin deploy` performs exactly this set of calls and reconciles an existing cluster on every deploy:
the dedicated parameter group `<cluster-name>-pg` (`log_lock_waits=1`, `log_min_duration_statement=1000`,
`pg_stat_statements.track=all`, `shared_preload_libraries=pg_stat_statements`) and the `postgresql`
log export are each completed if missing, taking effect online without a restart; only `shared_preload_libraries` needs to wait for one writer restart window,
with the steps in `管理员日常运维.md` (Administrator Operations) §4.6. The parameter group is written both to the bootstrap state and to the installation resource registry
(`aws/aurora/parameter-group`, `rds_cluster_parameter_group`, CREATED/DELETE), and
`uninstall` deletes it only after the Aurora cluster has been deleted.

```bash

if ! aws rds describe-db-instances \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-instance-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-writer" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  aws rds create-db-instance \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --db-instance-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-writer" \
    --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    --engine aurora-postgresql \
    --db-instance-class db.serverless \
    --availability-zone "${CPU_PRIVATE_AZS[0]}" \
    --promotion-tier 0
fi

# The reader must be created only after both the cluster and the writer are available: while the cluster or primary is still creating,
# RDS answers the replica's create-db-instance with InvalidDBClusterStateFault, and the instance that finishes first
# becomes the writer (regardless of naming), so the two cannot be created back to back.
aws rds wait db-instance-available \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-instance-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-writer"
aws rds wait db-cluster-available \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}"

if ! aws rds describe-db-instances \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-instance-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-reader" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  aws rds create-db-instance \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --db-instance-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-reader" \
    --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    --engine aurora-postgresql \
    --db-instance-class db.serverless \
    --availability-zone "${CPU_PRIVATE_AZS[1]}" \
    --promotion-tier 0
fi

aws rds wait db-instance-available \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-instance-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-reader"

aws rds describe-db-clusters \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
  --query 'DBClusters[0].{status:Status,engine:EngineVersion,writer:Endpoint,reader:ReaderEndpoint,capacity:ServerlessV2ScalingConfiguration,secret:MasterUserSecret.SecretArn,encrypted:StorageEncrypted,deletionProtection:DeletionProtection}' \
  --output table
```

Create the Kubernetes connection Secret. The procedure below does not show the password on standard output; the temporary URL file has mode
`0600`:

```bash
install -d -m 0700 /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-bootstrap
export AURORA_ENDPOINT="$(
  aws rds describe-db-clusters \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    --query 'DBClusters[0].Endpoint' --output text
)"
export AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN="$(
  aws rds describe-db-clusters \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    --query 'DBClusters[0].MasterUserSecret.SecretArn' --output text
)"

umask 077
aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --secret-id "${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN}" \
  --query SecretString --output text |
python3.12 -c '
import json, os, sys
from urllib.parse import quote
s = json.load(sys.stdin)
sys.stdout.write(
    "postgresql://"
    + quote(s["username"], safe="")
    + ":" + quote(s["password"], safe="")
    + "@" + os.environ["AURORA_ENDPOINT"]
    + ":5432/gpu_fault?sslmode=require"
)
' > /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-bootstrap/postgres-url

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" create namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create secret generic gpu-fault-aurora \
  --from-file=postgres-url=/tmp/gpu-fault-regional-bootstrap/postgres-url \
  --from-literal=master-secret-arn="${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN}" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -

rm -f /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-bootstrap/postgres-url
```

> **Must read: this Secret expires after 7 days, at which point all three control-plane replicas go
> `CrashLoopBackOff`.** The `--manage-master-user-password` passed to
> `create-db-cluster` lets RDS manage the master password, rotating it automatically with the default
> `AutomaticallyAfterDays=7`; the snippet above merely **snapshots** the password of that moment into a
> static `postgres-url`. After a rotation, Secrets Manager holds the new password while the K8s Secret
> still holds the old one (equal to `AWSPREVIOUS`), and the code has no rotation awareness at all:
> `PostgresStore.__init__` builds the connection pool at startup, and a failed connection raises directly -> the process exits
> -> `CrashLoopBackOff`. Because credentials are read only **at process start**, the failure does not erupt at the moment of rotation
> but only becomes visible at the first Pod restart after the rotation (a release, an eviction, a node rotation, a probe restart),
> so it presents as "it was fine yesterday, and today everything died on the first restart".
>
> The real incident record is in
> `docs/区域模式端到端验收测试用例.md`,
> `GF-REGIONAL-BOOT-016`, incidental finding 2.
>
> **Remediation: keep the rotation and deploy the credential refresh CronJob of the next sub-section.**
> Keeping the rotation means the master password keeps changing on the RDS cycle, at the cost of one more
> moving part you must maintain yourself; the other path is `aws secretsmanager cancel-rotate-secret` to turn
> rotation off completely, gaining no moving parts but a password that never changes. **This solution chooses the former.**
>
> Before acting, confirm the current rotation setting; do not assume it is 7 days:
>
> ```bash
> aws secretsmanager describe-secret \
>   --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
>   --secret-id "${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN}" \
>   --query '{rotation:RotationEnabled,rules:RotationRules,last:LastRotatedDate,next:NextRotationDate}' \
>   --output table
> ```

##### 2b. Deploy the Aurora Credential Refresh CronJob (Required Step When Keeping Rotation)

Manifest: `deploy/control-plane/regional/aurora-credential-refresh.yaml`
(SA + Role + RoleBinding + CronJob); logic:
`src/gpu_fault/aurora_credential_refresh.py`.

> If you deploy via `deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh`, this sub-section is **already automated**
> (`deploy_aurora_credential_refresh`, executed after the control plane is deployed:
> create the IRSA role -> apply the manifest -> run one verification Job), with no manual action needed.
> Below are the steps for manual deployment or retrofitting afterwards.

On every run it reads `AWSCURRENT` from Secrets Manager and compares it with the password in the Secret:

- Same -> do nothing, no database connection, no replica restart (a needless restart is itself a risk);
- Different -> **first really connect to the database once with the new password**, and write the Secret only when the check passes. By default it **no longer rolls**
  the Deployments: running Pods re-read the DSN through the mounted file (see below); only when
  `--restart-deployments` (or `GPU_FAULT_AURORA_REFRESH_RESTART_DEPLOYMENTS=true`) is passed
  does it stamp the Deployments with a timestamp annotation to trigger a rollout as the old version did.

The "verify first, then switch" step cannot be omitted: if the refresher cannot tell a good password from a bad one,
it has the power to replace a still-working Secret with a broken one, turning a rotation into an outage of its own making.

After replacing the Secret, **no** replica restart is needed (control-plane review 2026-09-08, CP-3 / H1): the three role Deployments project the
`gpu-fault-aurora` Secret as a whole into `/etc/gpu-fault/aurora` (read-only, without `subPath` -- a mount with
`subPath` does not follow Secret updates), and when the connection pool opens a new connection it re-reads the DSN via
`GPU_FAULT_STORE_URL_FILE=/etc/gpu-fault/aurora/postgres-url`; the `GPU_FAULT_STORE_URL`
env still keeps a copy as a startup fallback (settings/admission read it before the pool opens). Within roughly 1-2 minutes of kubelet sync after the Secret is written,
new connections use the new password; before that, connections rebuilt after being reclaimed by `max_idle` fail authentication, and the pool backs off
and retries without losing slots any more (`GpuFaultPostgresPoolConnectionErrors` will show this window). If you do choose
`--restart-deployments`, `maxUnavailable=1` plus the PDB `minAvailable=2` guarantee a majority of replicas
keep serving during the rollout. At the end of every run (success or final failure) the refresher writes
`{status, finished_at, error, rotated, restarted, reason}` into the same Secret's
`last-refresh-status.json` (patching only this one key; the DSN password in error is redacted); the control plane exports a metric from its
age and `GpuFaultAuroraCredentialRefreshStale` / `...Failing` alert on it -- a CronJob that
"silently does not work" is visible from now on.

**First create the IAM role and associate Pod Identity.** There are only two permissions, both restricted to concrete resources:
reading that one managed master password, and the KMS key used to decrypt it.

```bash
export REFRESH_ROLE_NAME=gpu-fault-aurora-credential-refresh
# Note that this reads MasterUserSecret.KmsKeyId on the RDS side.
# The top level of secretsmanager describe-secret has no KmsKeyId field;
# using it silently yields None, which becomes an invalid resource in the policy.
export AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_KMS_KEY_ARN="$(
  aws rds describe-db-clusters \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    --query 'DBClusters[0].MasterUserSecret.KmsKeyId' --output text
)"

cat > /tmp/refresh-trust.json <<'JSON'
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": { "Service": "pods.eks.amazonaws.com" },
      "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:TagSession"]
    }
  ]
}
JSON

aws iam create-role \
  --role-name "${REFRESH_ROLE_NAME}" \
  --assume-role-policy-document file:///tmp/refresh-trust.json

AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN="${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN}" \
AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_KMS_KEY_ARN="${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_KMS_KEY_ARN}" \
python3.12 - <<'PY' > /tmp/refresh-policy.json
import json, os
print(json.dumps({
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "ReadAuroraManagedMasterSecret",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": ["secretsmanager:GetSecretValue"],
            "Resource": os.environ["AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN"],
        },
        {
            "Sid": "DecryptAuroraManagedMasterSecret",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": ["kms:Decrypt"],
            "Resource": os.environ["AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_KMS_KEY_ARN"],
        },
    ],
}, indent=2))
PY

aws iam put-role-policy \
  --role-name "${REFRESH_ROLE_NAME}" \
  --policy-name ReadAuroraManagedMasterSecret \
  --policy-document file:///tmp/refresh-policy.json
```

First verify and reuse the wheel already tested in §4. Rebuilding here is forbidden; a missing module means the current release
artefacts are incomplete, and you must go back to §4 and rerun the complete release, updating the control plane, Executor, node
bundle and release metadata together; a second wheel must not be generated in this step:

```bash
export WHEEL="${WHEEL:-$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py wheel
)}"
test -s "${WHEEL}"
PYTHONPATH=src python3.12 -c \
  'import gpu_fault.aurora_credential_refresh'
python3.12 - "${WHEEL}" <<'PY'
import sys
import zipfile

with zipfile.ZipFile(sys.argv[1]) as wheel:
    assert "gpu_fault/aurora_credential_refresh.py" in wheel.namelist(), (
        "current release wheel lacks gpu_fault.aurora_credential_refresh; "
        "return to §4 and rebuild the complete release once"
    )
PY

WHEEL_SHA256="$(sha256sum "${WHEEL}" | awk '{print $1}')"
WHEEL_CM="gpu-fault-control-plane-wheel-0100-${WHEEL_SHA256:0:12}"
# The ConfigMap stores the xz-compressed wheel (key name <wheel>.xz): the ConfigMap limit is 1 MiB,
# which the control-plane wheel already exceeds; the runtime image ships the installed wheel, and no component installs it from the mount.
WHEEL_KEY="$(basename "${WHEEL}").xz"

if kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get configmap "${WHEEL_CM}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  CONFIG_MAP_SHA256="$(
    kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      get configmap "${WHEEL_CM}" -o json |
    jq -er --arg key "${WHEEL_KEY}" '.binaryData[$key]' |
    base64 -d | xz -d |
    sha256sum | awk '{print $1}'
  )"
  test "${CONFIG_MAP_SHA256}" = "${WHEEL_SHA256}"
else
  xz -9 -k -c "${WHEEL}" > "/tmp/${WHEEL_KEY}"
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    create configmap "${WHEEL_CM}" --from-file="${WHEEL_KEY}=/tmp/${WHEEL_KEY}"
  rm -f "/tmp/${WHEEL_KEY}"
fi

sed "s/gpu-fault-control-plane-wheel-0100/${WHEEL_CM}/g" \
  deploy/control-plane/regional/aurora-credential-refresh.yaml \
  > /tmp/aurora-credential-refresh.yaml
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply \
  -f /tmp/aurora-credential-refresh.yaml
rm -f /tmp/aurora-credential-refresh.yaml

REFRESH_ROLE_ARN="arn:aws:iam::$(
  aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text
):role/${REFRESH_ROLE_NAME}"
ASSOCIATION_ID="$(
  aws eks list-pod-identity-associations \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
    --service-account gpu-fault-aurora-credential-refresh \
    --query 'associations[0].associationId' \
    --output text
)"
if [[ "${ASSOCIATION_ID}" == "None" ]]; then
  aws eks create-pod-identity-association \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
    --service-account gpu-fault-aurora-credential-refresh \
    --role-arn "${REFRESH_ROLE_ARN}"
else
  aws eks update-pod-identity-association \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --association-id "${ASSOCIATION_ID}" \
    --role-arn "${REFRESH_ROLE_ARN}"
fi
```

The manifest must create the ServiceAccount first and the Pod Identity association afterwards; the order above cannot be swapped.
An existing association only has its role updated; a second association is not created.

> The trust policy and association method above are **Pod Identity**, which this manual's regionally separated deployment has always used.
> `deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh` uses **IRSA** (OIDC federation +
> the `eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn` annotation), consistent with the other roles of that script.
> Pick only one of the two paths; do not configure both.

The manifest does not set `AWS_REGION`: both Pod Identity and IRSA inject it into the Pod,
and hard-coding a region in a manifest that is otherwise region-agnostic is exactly how a copy-pasted deployment ends up reading
another account's keys. To override, set `GPU_FAULT_AWS_REGION`.

`gpu-fault-control-plane-wheel-0100` in the manifest is only a placeholder; the `sed` above must
replace it with the content-addressed ConfigMap of the current release. An existing ConfigMap is only compared by content SHA, not overwritten;
it is created from the same `${WHEEL}` only when absent. That way when CPU-4b meets it again it only re-checks the same SHA
and does not produce a second release artifact.

**Verify (do not wait until the next rotation cycle to discover it does not work).** Trigger it once manually.
When the password has not changed and the three database consumers already carry the same refresh token, the expected path is
the harmless "did nothing" path; if the previous rotation completed only part of the Deployment patches,
this run only rolls the missing roles:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create job aurora-credential-refresh-verify \
  --from=cronjob/gpu-fault-aurora-credential-refresh

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  wait --for=condition=complete job/aurora-credential-refresh-verify \
  --timeout=300s

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs job/aurora-credential-refresh-verify
```

When fully converged, expect `rotated=False restarted=False`. When the Secret of a first deployment has no
refresh token yet, the reason is `secret already carries the AWSCURRENT password`;
when a rotation has already completed, the reason is
`secret and database consumers already carry current credentials`.
`rotated=False restarted=True (deployment rollout resumed for
current credentials)` only appears under the `--restart-deployments` compatibility switch, meaning it completed a previously
unfinished rollout, which is also a correct recovery path; in that case confirm that only the `generation` of the missing Deployment changed;
in default mode a leftover rollout annotation is treated as nothing and does not trigger any rollout.

In a greenfield CPU-2b the control-plane Deployments do not exist yet, so only check the no-op log; when retrofitting an existing environment
that was already converged, also confirm that the `generation` of ingress, control-worker and the enabled
spool-worker are all unchanged. After confirming, delete this one-shot Job:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete job aurora-credential-refresh-verify
```

To verify end to end that "it handles a real rotation correctly", you can actively trigger a rotation
(this really replaces the production password; do it in a maintenance window):

```bash
aws secretsmanager rotate-secret \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --secret-id "${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN}"
```

Then run the Job above once manually; the expected log is
`rotated=True restarted=False (credentials updated; running replicas reload the mounted Secret)`.
The criterion is **zero rollout + file propagation**: the
`generation`, Pod names and `restartCount` of ingress, control-worker and the enabled spool-worker are all unchanged; use
`kubectl -n "${NAMESPACE}" exec <pod> -- sha256sum /etc/gpu-fault/aurora/postgres-url`
to check per role that the mounted file has been updated (its digest matches `postgres-url` in the Secret); after one
`GPU_FAULT_POSTGRES_POOL_MAX_IDLE_SECONDS` idle window `/healthz` stays 200 and the logs contain no
`password authentication failed`. Only when run with `--restart-deployments` are
`restarted=True` and a three-role rollout expected; in that case the Role's Deployment `resourceNames` must still exactly match
`GPU_FAULT_AURORA_RESTART_DEPLOYMENTS` (the RBAC consistency check is unchanged). To read the
conclusion of the last run:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-aurora \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.last-refresh-status\.json}' | base64 -d
```

**Troubleshooting essentials**: a silent failure of this CronJob has a cost -- it not working and it not existing
look identical until the next rotation. At minimum, monitor the failure records in `failedJobsHistoryLimit`:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get jobs -l app=gpu-fault-aurora-credential-refresh
```

**Schedule frequency and synchronous refresh before a release (two tightenings after the 2026-09-07 production incident).** The
`schedule` in the manifest is **hourly** `17 * * * *`, no longer twice a day. In the window between a rotation and the next
tick the Secret still holds the old password; running replicas are unaffected, but during that time **every newly created** control-plane
Pod exits with `FATAL: password authentication failed`. Twice a day used to mean this window could last
up to 12 hours; on 2026-09-07 12:13Z a release transaction landed exactly inside the window: the re-apply failed, the automatic rollback
restarted control-worker, the new Pods likewise could not authenticate, and the transaction finally stopped at `rollback-failed` -- from the release output
it looked no different from a genuinely broken release until someone read the Pod logs. The hourly schedule compresses the window to
1 hour, and `startingDeadlineSeconds` is shortened accordingly to 1800 so a late run does not collide with the next tick.

The second tightening is in the orchestrator (`src/gpu_fault_release/regional_aurora_credentials.py`):
`upgrade` (including resume), after applying the RDS CA bundle and **before** checking the remote command queue and capturing the
previous release, as well as `rollback` **before** making any compensation plan, synchronously runs
this CronJob once -- `kubectl create job --from=cronjob/gpu-fault-aurora-credential-refresh
gpu-fault-aurora-credential-refresh-<8-char random suffix>`, then
`kubectl wait --for=condition=complete --timeout=300s`, and deletes the Job on success. It is
**fail closed**: if the Job does not reach Complete within the timeout, the transaction immediately fails with
`Aurora credential refresh Job <namespace>/<job> did not complete within 300s`,
writes no release state, rolls no component, and leaves the Job in place for investigation:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs job/<Job name from the error message>
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  describe job/<Job name from the error message>
```

After fixing the cause (commonly a missing Pod Identity role, KMS permission or RDS CA bundle ConfigMap),
delete this Job and rerun the same deploy/rollback command. The wait duration is overridden by
`GPU_FAULT_RELEASE_AURORA_REFRESH_WAIT_SECONDS` (positive integer seconds, default 300); it applies to
the deployment-host environment running `gpu-fault-admin`, not to container env. On a site without the CronJob (a legacy environment that never installed
§2b) the step prints `aurora-credential-refresh skipped` and skips, behaving as
before. A greenfield bootstrap does not run this step: at that point the Secret was just written from `AWSCURRENT` by the same deployment,
and the CronJob has not been created yet.
##### CPU-2R. Aurora Credential Recovery for an Already Deployed Environment

This section is an after-the-fact recovery runbook, not part of the greenfield first-deployment steps. A first deployment continues with CPU-3; run this section only
when the Aurora password has already rotated and running replicas show authentication failures. Since the mounted credentials (§2b) went live,
"running replicas fail authentication" only happens in the window between rotation and refresh, and the recovery action is reduced to **running the refresh Job once**
-- Pods do not need a restart; once the mounted file syncs, new connections recover.

The symptom is all three replicas in `CrashLoopBackOff` at the same time, with `kubectl logs --previous` showing
`PostgresStore.__init__` -> `psycopg_pool.PoolTimeout: couldn't get a
connection after 2.00 sec`. Note that `PoolTimeout` is the **surface symptom**: the connection pool swallows the authentication failure
and only reports a timeout. To see the real cause, connect once directly with the DSN from the Secret and you will get
`FATAL: password authentication failed for user "gpu_fault_admin"`.

The same root cause has a second presentation: a `gpu-fault-admin deploy` or `rollback` stops at
`failed`/`rollback-failed`, the newly rolled control-plane Pods are in `CrashLoopBackOff`, yet the release itself
has nothing wrong. Since 2026-09-07, both deploy and rollback first synchronously run the refresh CronJob once and
fail closed (see the end of §2b), so this situation should only appear when the refresh step was skipped (the CronJob does not exist)
or the rotation happened in the tiny window right after the refresh and during the rollout. Do not touch the release in that case: after
diagnosing per this section, run the refresh Job once by hand (`kubectl create job --from=cronjob/...`, about 5 seconds, Pods
Ready in about 2 minutes), then rerun the same deploy command to let the transaction resume.

The criterion for confirming that rotation is the cause (rather than a network or cluster failure): the password in the Secret equals
`AWSPREVIOUS` and does not equal `AWSCURRENT`. The following compares only hashes and does not print passwords:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-aurora \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.postgres-url}' | base64 -d > /tmp/k8sdsn.txt
chmod 600 /tmp/k8sdsn.txt

AWS_REGION="${AWS_REGION}" \
AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN="${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN}" \
python3.12 - <<'PY'
import hashlib, json, os, subprocess
from urllib.parse import urlsplit, unquote


def stage(name: str) -> str:
    out = subprocess.run(
        [
            "aws", "secretsmanager", "get-secret-value",
            "--region", os.environ["AWS_REGION"],
            "--secret-id", os.environ["AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN"],
            "--version-stage", name,
            "--query", "SecretString", "--output", "text",
        ],
        capture_output=True, text=True, check=True,
    ).stdout.strip()
    return json.loads(out)["password"]


def digest(value: str) -> str:
    return hashlib.sha256(value.encode()).hexdigest()[:16]


with open("/tmp/k8sdsn.txt", encoding="utf-8") as handle:
    k8s = unquote(urlsplit(handle.read().strip()).password or "")

print("k8s secret  =", digest(k8s))
print("AWSCURRENT  =", digest(stage("AWSCURRENT")))
print("AWSPREVIOUS =", digest(stage("AWSPREVIOUS")))
PY
```

`k8s secret == AWSPREVIOUS` confirms the diagnosis. Recovery steps: first back up the existing Secret for rollback,
then rebuild the DSN from `AWSCURRENT`, `patch` it into the Secret, and `rollout restart`. The password travels
only through files and stdin throughout, **never in command-line arguments**:

<!-- boot017-out-of-band: after-the-fact recovery procedure operating on a running deployment; not part of the first-deployment order -->

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-aurora -o yaml > /tmp/gpu-fault-aurora.backup.yaml
chmod 600 /tmp/gpu-fault-aurora.backup.yaml

umask 077
AWS_REGION="${AWS_REGION}" \
AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN="${AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN}" \
AURORA_CLUSTER_ID="${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
python3.12 - <<'PY' > /tmp/aurora-patch.json
import base64, json, os, subprocess
from urllib.parse import quote

region = os.environ["AWS_REGION"]
secret = json.loads(subprocess.run(
    [
        "aws", "secretsmanager", "get-secret-value",
        "--region", region,
        "--secret-id", os.environ["AURORA_MASTER_SECRET_ARN"],
        "--version-stage", "AWSCURRENT",
        "--query", "SecretString", "--output", "text",
    ],
    capture_output=True, text=True, check=True,
).stdout.strip())
endpoint = subprocess.run(
    [
        "aws", "rds", "describe-db-clusters",
        "--region", region,
        "--db-cluster-identifier", os.environ["AURORA_CLUSTER_ID"],
        "--query", "DBClusters[0].Endpoint", "--output", "text",
    ],
    capture_output=True, text=True, check=True,
).stdout.strip()
url = (
    "postgresql://"
    + quote(secret["username"], safe="")
    + ":" + quote(secret["password"], safe="")
    + "@" + endpoint
    + ":5432/gpu_fault?sslmode=require"
)
print(json.dumps(
    {"data": {"postgres-url": base64.b64encode(url.encode()).decode()}}
))
PY

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  patch secret gpu-fault-aurora \
  --type=merge --patch-file=/tmp/aurora-patch.json

KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/restart-regional-control-plane.sh

shred -u /tmp/aurora-patch.json /tmp/k8sdsn.txt
```

Recovery criteria: three replicas `1/1 Running`, `restartCount=0`, `/healthz` returns 200.
Once confirmed, delete `/tmp/gpu-fault-aurora.backup.yaml` (it contains the old password).

#### CPU-3. Install the AWS Load Balancer Controller

First check whether the cluster already has a global AWS Load Balancer Controller managed by EKS/HyperPod:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" get deployment -A -o json |
jq '[.items[] |
  select((.spec.template.spec.containers[].image // "") |
    test("load-balancer|operator-alb"; "i")) |
  {
    namespace: .metadata.namespace,
    name: .metadata.name,
    managed_by: .metadata.labels["app.kubernetes.io/managed-by"],
    ready: .status.readyReplicas,
    replicas: .spec.replicas,
    image: .spec.template.spec.containers[0].image,
    args: .spec.template.spec.containers[0].args
  }]'
```

If a Controller with `managed_by=EKS` already exists, and its `--cluster-name`, `--aws-region` and
`--aws-vpc-id` exactly match the current CPU EKS with all replicas Ready, you must reuse it and skip the rest of this section's
standalone Helm/IAM installation. Two LBCs watching all namespaces at the same time must not run in the same cluster; they would simultaneously
create/modify the same SG, TargetGroupBinding, listener and NLB. Platform-managed CRDs must not be
force-upgraded with another chart version either.

Only when the check above finds no usable Controller do you run the following standalone installation procedure.

First confirm the EKS Pod Identity Agent is available; create the managed add-on when it is not:

```bash
if ! aws eks describe-addon \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
  --addon-name eks-pod-identity-agent >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  aws eks create-addon \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --addon-name eks-pod-identity-agent
fi

aws eks wait addon-active \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
  --addon-name eks-pod-identity-agent
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n kube-system \
  get daemonset eks-pod-identity-agent
```

Download the official IAM policy matching the installed version and create the Pod Identity role:

```bash
export AWS_ACCOUNT_ID="$(
  aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text
)"
export LBC_VERSION=v2.17.1
export LBC_POLICY_ARN="arn:aws:iam::${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:policy/${LBC_POLICY_NAME}"

curl -fsSLo /tmp/aws-load-balancer-controller-iam-policy.json \
  "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kubernetes-sigs/aws-load-balancer-controller/${LBC_VERSION}/docs/install/iam_policy.json"

if ! aws iam get-policy --policy-arn "${LBC_POLICY_ARN}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  aws iam create-policy \
    --policy-name "${LBC_POLICY_NAME}" \
    --policy-document file:///tmp/aws-load-balancer-controller-iam-policy.json
fi

cat > /tmp/lbc-pod-identity-trust.json <<'EOF'
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": {"Service": "pods.eks.amazonaws.com"},
    "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:TagSession"]
  }]
}
EOF

if ! aws iam get-role --role-name "${LBC_ROLE_NAME}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  aws iam create-role \
    --role-name "${LBC_ROLE_NAME}" \
    --assume-role-policy-document file:///tmp/lbc-pod-identity-trust.json
fi
aws iam attach-role-policy \
  --role-name "${LBC_ROLE_NAME}" \
  --policy-arn "${LBC_POLICY_ARN}"

export LBC_ROLE_ARN="$(
  aws iam get-role \
    --role-name "${LBC_ROLE_NAME}" \
    --query Role.Arn --output text
)"

export LBC_ASSOCIATION_ID="$(
  aws eks list-pod-identity-associations \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --namespace kube-system \
    --service-account aws-load-balancer-controller \
    --query 'associations[0].associationId' --output text
)"
if [[ -z "${LBC_ASSOCIATION_ID}" ||
  "${LBC_ASSOCIATION_ID}" == "None" ]]; then
  aws eks create-pod-identity-association \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --namespace kube-system \
    --service-account aws-load-balancer-controller \
    --role-arn "${LBC_ROLE_ARN}"
else
  aws eks update-pod-identity-association \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --association-id "${LBC_ASSOCIATION_ID}" \
    --role-arn "${LBC_ROLE_ARN}"
fi

helm repo add eks https://aws.github.io/eks-charts
helm repo update eks
helm upgrade --install aws-load-balancer-controller eks/aws-load-balancer-controller \
  --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  --namespace kube-system \
  --version 1.17.1 \
  --set clusterName="${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
  --set region="${AWS_REGION}" \
  --set vpcId="${CPU_VPC_ID}" \
  --set replicaCount=2 \
  --set serviceAccount.create=true \
  --set serviceAccount.name=aws-load-balancer-controller

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n kube-system \
  rollout status deployment/aws-load-balancer-controller --timeout=300s
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n kube-system \
  get pods -l app.kubernetes.io/name=aws-load-balancer-controller -o wide
```

It must be `2/2`, and the namespace and ServiceAccount name of the Pod Identity association must match the Helm
release.

#### CPU-4. Create the Control-Plane Secrets and Deploy the Three-Tier Roles

The regional overlay keeps
`GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_MANAGED_OBSERVER=true`, so the CPU API Pods need to read HyperPod state in this Region.
They need no GPU EKS kubeconfig and are granted no reboot/replace permission. Create a read-only SageMaker Pod Identity for the control-plane
ServiceAccount:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create serviceaccount gpu-fault-control-plane \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -

cat > /tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-pod-identity-trust.json <<'EOF'
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": {"Service": "pods.eks.amazonaws.com"},
    "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:TagSession"]
  }]
}
EOF

if ! aws iam get-role \
  --role-name "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_NAME}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  aws iam create-role \
    --role-name "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_NAME}" \
    --assume-role-policy-document \
      file:///tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-pod-identity-trust.json
else
  aws iam update-assume-role-policy \
    --role-name "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_NAME}" \
    --policy-document \
      file:///tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-pod-identity-trust.json
fi

cat > /tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-sagemaker-read.json <<EOF
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "ObserveRegionalHyperPodClusters",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": [
      "sagemaker:DescribeCluster",
      "sagemaker:ListClusterNodes",
      "sagemaker:DescribeClusterNode"
    ],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:sagemaker:${AWS_REGION}:${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:cluster/*"
  }]
}
EOF

aws iam put-role-policy \
  --role-name "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_NAME}" \
  --policy-name GPUFaultRegionalHyperPodRead \
  --policy-document \
    file:///tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-sagemaker-read.json

# Only sites with spec.notifications.channel: ses need the SES identity and ses:SendEmail policy below.
# New sites default to channel: sns: administrator notifications are published to the site SNS topic (sharing the same topic/subscription with AMP alerts),
# no SES identity/policy is needed, so skip this SES configuration.
export GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_SENDER='<verified-sender>'
export GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_RECIPIENTS='<administrator-addresses>'
export GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_EMAIL=true

cat > /tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-ses-send.json <<EOF
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Sid": "SendFromVerifiedGpuFaultIdentity",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": "ses:SendEmail",
    "Resource": "arn:aws:ses:${AWS_REGION}:${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:identity/${GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_SENDER}"
  }]
}
EOF

aws sesv2 get-email-identity \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --email-identity "${GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_SENDER}" \
  --query '{verification:VerificationStatus,enabled:VerifiedForSendingStatus}'

aws iam put-role-policy \
  --role-name "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_NAME}" \
  --policy-name SendGpuFaultEmail \
  --policy-document file:///tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-ses-send.json

export CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_ARN="$(
  aws iam get-role \
    --role-name "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_NAME}" \
    --query Role.Arn --output text
)"

export CONTROL_PLANE_ASSOCIATION_ID="$(
  aws eks list-pod-identity-associations \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
    --service-account gpu-fault-control-plane \
    --query 'associations[0].associationId' --output text
)"
if [[ -z "${CONTROL_PLANE_ASSOCIATION_ID}" ||
  "${CONTROL_PLANE_ASSOCIATION_ID}" == "None" ]]; then
  aws eks create-pod-identity-association \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
    --service-account gpu-fault-control-plane \
    --role-arn "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_ARN}"
else
  aws eks update-pod-identity-association \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --association-id "${CONTROL_PLANE_ASSOCIATION_ID}" \
    --role-arn "${CONTROL_PLANE_ROLE_ARN}"
fi
```

The regional control plane and the old single-cluster `deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh` use different IAM roles. The release engine (bootstrap) grants only the permissions of the
channel selected by `spec.notifications.channel`: for SNS, `sns:Publish` on the site topic; for
`channel: ses`, a sender-restricted `ses:SendEmail`. Setting only `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_EMAIL=true` does not fill in
the permissions; when the CPU EKS Pod Identity role lacks `ses:SendEmail`, notifications are still saved, but the delivery result is
`FAILED/AccessDeniedException`.

Create the email Secret (the sender/recipients fields below are needed only on `channel: ses` sites):

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create secret generic gpu-fault-email \
  --from-literal=email-sender="${GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_SENDER}" \
  --from-literal=email-recipients="${GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_RECIPIENTS}" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -
```

Do not use `kubectl set env` to turn on email sending. The Deployment does not exist yet at this point, so `set env` fails with
`NotFound`; even when run after deployment, the non-declarative field it writes is overwritten by the next
`kubectl apply`. Email is controlled by the declarative manifest
`deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-control-plane-patch.yaml`; the regional manifest turns on
both switches by default, and you cannot enable just one of them:

| Variable | Manifest default | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_EMAIL` | `"true"` | Allows notifications to be generated and delivered |
| `GPU_FAULT_NOTIFICATION_DISPATCHER_ENABLED` | `"true"` | Starts the outbox dispatcher |

Enabling only the former leaves every notification permanently in `PENDING` (no dispatcher drains the outbox);
enabling only the latter produces no notifications at all. After changing the patch, re-`apply` via the kustomize steps later in this section;
do not `set env` separately.

##### CPU-4a. Create the Control-Plane Credentials and Confirm the First Registry

Generate the control-plane keys only on the first deployment. When the Secret already exists it must not be regenerated, or the already registered GPU
clusters will be cut off:

```bash
if ! kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    create secret generic gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
    --from-literal=execution-token="$(openssl rand -hex 32)" \
    --from-literal=processor-replay-secret="$(openssl rand -hex 32)" \
    --from-literal=node-action-secret="$(openssl rand -hex 32)"
fi

# Prints only key names and digests, never secret values. There must be exactly three keys, and the digests must be pairwise different.
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active -o json |
python3 -c '
import base64, hashlib, json, sys
data = json.load(sys.stdin)["data"]
required = {
    "execution-token",
    "processor-replay-secret",
    "node-action-secret",
}
assert required <= set(data), required - set(data)
digests = {
    key: hashlib.sha256(base64.b64decode(data[key])).hexdigest()
    for key in sorted(required)
}
assert len(set(digests.values())) == len(required)
print("\n".join(f"{key} {value}" for key, value in digests.items()))
'
```

`gpu-fault-node-action-keys` is also a **Secret required before startup** for all three control-plane roles:
ingress, control-worker and spool-worker all mount it at
`/etc/gpu-fault/node-action-keys`. You cannot wait until the control plane has started to run REG-4,
otherwise all Pods stall at `FailedMount`. The context of the first GPU cluster has already been pinned by
REG-1 and REG-2; at this point create the GPU namespace first, and write the derived per-node keys
to both the CPU and GPU sides at the same time:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" create namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" apply -f -

FLEET_MASTER_FILE="$(mktemp)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.node-action-secret}' |
base64 -d > "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"
chmod 0600 "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"

GPU_FAULT_KUBECTL_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_ID="${CLUSTER_ID}" \
GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER="${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
GPU_FAULT_FLEET_MASTER_FILE="${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  deploy/node/provision-node-action-keys.sh
shred -u "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"

for target in cpu gpu; do
  if [[ "${target}" == cpu ]]; then
    kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      get secret gpu-fault-node-action-keys -o name
  else
    kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      get secret gpu-fault-node-action-keys -o name
  fi
done
```

**On the first deployment `gpu-fault-regional-clusters` must contain the first GPU cluster.** This guarantees an acceptable data plane exists
immediately after bootstrap; it is not a runtime restriction on an empty registry. After the last GPU cluster is deregistered via
`gpu-fault-admin remove-cluster`, an explicit empty array `[]` is a legitimate
unloaded control-plane state; a missing Secret/key, invalid JSON or a non-array still makes the API Pods fail to start. Therefore
the unified releaser first writes all clusters from `site.yaml` into the registry and then starts the CPU control plane; without release
state, or in a `bootstrap-*` recovery phase, an empty cluster set is refused before the first Kubernetes apply.
The manual check below is only for the break-glass procedure:

```bash
test -s "${SECURE_DIR:-/nonexistent}/clusters.json" ||
  {
    echo 'run REG-1 and REG-2 of §5.3 first to generate the first cluster entry' >&2
    exit 1
  }

REGISTRY_COUNT="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get secret gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
    -o jsonpath='{.data.clusters\.json}' |
  base64 -d | jq -er 'length'
)"
test "${REGISTRY_COUNT}" -ge 1
```

`jq 'length'` must be >= 1. The GPU data plane has not been deployed at this point; only an entry in the registry is needed;
the data-plane Secret, executor and node installation are done after the control plane is ready, in step 3 and later of the registration chapter.
In other words the registration chapter is split in two by the control-plane deployment:

1. Registration chapter steps 1 and 2 (generate the token, write the registry Secret), then run CPU-4a
   to synchronise the Node Action keys of the first cluster -- before the role-split;
2. This step: deploy the three-replica control plane, NLB, TLS;
3. Registration chapter step 3 and later (connection Secret, executor, node installation). Running the
   Node Action key script again in REG-4 is an idempotent re-check and supports adding GPU clusters later.

`deploy/control-plane/base/role-split-input.kustomization.yaml` is the input of the role-split renderer and
**must not be applied directly**: applying it directly only yields an old monolithic
Deployment that both receives requests and consumes the queue. The actual runtime manifests are generated by
`deploy/control-plane/tools/render-control-plane-role-split.sh` into
`deploy/control-plane/regional/generated/`. Before deploying, confirm that both the inputs and the generated output match this Region:

| File | Content | Fields to modify per site |
|---|---|---|
| `deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-control-plane-prerequisites.yaml` | `gpu-fault-system` Namespace, `gpu-fault-control-plane` ServiceAccount, ClusterIP Service `gpu-fault-api` (8080 -> `http`) | None (the NLB is created separately in step 5 of this chapter and points at the same set of Pods) |
| `deploy/control-plane/base/control-plane-deployment.yaml` | Deployment baseline shared by single-cluster and regional mode, wheel ConfigMap reference, artifact SHA annotation | None (the apply script replaces automatically; direct apply is forbidden) |
| `deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-control-plane-patch.yaml` | Regional-mode environment variables and `nodeSelector` | The Region stays `REPLACE_WITH_AWS_REGION` and is replaced at apply time by the checked configuration; do not hand-edit the generated output |
| `deploy/control-plane/regional/generated/*.yaml` | ingress, control-worker, spool-worker with 0 replicas by default, PDBs and per-role ConfigMaps | Do not hand-edit; the directory content must exactly match `manifest-list.txt` |

The ServiceAccount in prerequisites is the same object created by `create serviceaccount` earlier in this section;
repeated apply is idempotent and the Pod Identity association is not lost. The two
Region values in the source patch must stay placeholders; the unified orchestrator via `aws_region`, and the manual entry point via
`GPU_FAULT_AWS_REGION`, replace `AWS_REGION` /
`AWS_DEFAULT_REGION` together at apply time. With a missing or invalid Region the apply fails before any Kubernetes change.

##### CPU-4b. Create the Release Metadata and Initialise the Schema

Reuse the wheel already validated by `make check` in §4, regenerate the role-split manifests and use the content-addressed
ConfigMap:

```bash
export WHEEL="${WHEEL:-$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py wheel
)}"
export EXECUTOR_WHEEL="${EXECUTOR_WHEEL:-$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py executor-wheel
)}"
export NODE_WHEEL="${NODE_WHEEL:-$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py node-runtime-wheel
)}"
test -s "${WHEEL}"
test -s "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}"
test -s "${NODE_WHEEL}"
export ARTIFACT_SHA256="$(sha256sum "${WHEEL}" | awk '{print $1}')"
export EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256="$(
  sha256sum "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}" | awk '{print $1}'
)"
export NODE_ARTIFACT_SHA256="$(
  sha256sum "${NODE_WHEEL}" | awk '{print $1}'
)"
export EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="$(
  jq -er '.components.executor.module_digest' dist/current-release.json
)"
export NODE_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="$(
  jq -er '.components.node_runtime.module_digest' dist/current-release.json
)"
export WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP="gpu-fault-control-plane-wheel-0100-${ARTIFACT_SHA256:0:12}"

deploy/control-plane/tools/render-control-plane-role-split.sh
PYTHONPATH=src python3.12 -m gpu_fault.config_cli validate \
  deploy/control-plane/regional/generated

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply \
  -f deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-control-plane-prerequisites.yaml

if kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get configmap "${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  export CONFIG_MAP_SHA256="$(
    kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      get configmap "${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" -o json |
    jq -r --arg key "$(basename "${WHEEL}").xz" '.binaryData[$key]' |
    base64 -d | xz -d |
    sha256sum | awk '{print $1}'
  )"
  test "${CONFIG_MAP_SHA256}" = "${ARTIFACT_SHA256}"
else
  # Consistent with gpu-fault-admin: the wheel is stored xz-compressed in the ConfigMap (key name <wheel>.xz).
  xz -9 -k -c "${WHEEL}" > "/tmp/$(basename "${WHEEL}").xz"
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    create configmap "${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
    --from-file="$(basename "${WHEEL}").xz=/tmp/$(basename "${WHEEL}").xz"
  rm -f "/tmp/$(basename "${WHEEL}").xz"
fi
```

The release metadata values come from the Executor/Node Runtime artefacts, bundle/template, image digests and the final Node Agent configuration
of the same signed schema v3 release. The environment below matches the step 6 production installation command:
key version 2,
GPU reset, fabric reset, service quiesce and Fabric Manager restart enabled by default, and
the EFA remediation capability retained. Pins must not be reverse-selected from `/v1/fleet/agents`; that endpoint is only
used for cross-checking after node installation.

```bash
export REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${NODE_ARTIFACT_SHA256}"
export REQUIRED_AGENT_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="${NODE_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}"
export REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256}"
export REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="${EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}"
export REQUIRED_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION="$(
  PYTHONPATH=src python3.12 -c \
    'from gpu_fault.fleet import CURRENT_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION; print(CURRENT_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION)'
)"
export REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_PROTOCOL_VERSION="$(
  PYTHONPATH=src python3.12 -c \
    'from gpu_fault.regional_compatibility import CURRENT_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_PROTOCOL_VERSION; print(CURRENT_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_PROTOCOL_VERSION)'
)"
export NODE_ALLOWED_OPERATIONS='COLLECT_HUNG_TRIAGE,COLLECT_DIAGNOSTIC_BUNDLE,RUN_DCGM_DIAGNOSTIC,QUIESCE_GPU_SERVICES,VERIFY_NO_GPU_CLIENTS,TRIGGER_HEALTH_SNAPSHOT,RESET_GPU,RESET_ALL_GPUS_NVSWITCHES,RESTORE_GPU_SERVICES,RESTART_FABRIC_MANAGER,REMEDIATE_EFA_DRIVER,COLLECTOR_OUTBOX_MAINTENANCE'
: "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION:?load the approved content-addressed Profile from site/release}"
export REQUIRED_AGENT_CONFIG_DIGEST="$(
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION="${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" \
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_ACTION_KEY_VERSION=2 \
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_ALLOWED_OPERATIONS="${NODE_ALLOWED_OPERATIONS}" \
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_ALLOW_GPU_RESET=true \
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_ALLOW_FABRIC_RESET=true \
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_ALLOW_SERVICE_QUIESCE=true \
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_ALLOW_FABRIC_MANAGER_RESTART=true \
  GPU_FAULT_NODE_ALLOW_EFA_DRIVER_REMEDIATION=true \
  PYTHONPATH=src python3.12 -c \
    'from gpu_fault.node_agent import print_config_digest; print_config_digest()'
)"

test "${REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" = "${NODE_ARTIFACT_SHA256}"
test "${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" = \
  "${EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256}"
[[ "${REQUIRED_AGENT_CONFIG_DIGEST}" =~ ^[0-9a-f]{64}$ ]]
[[ "${REQUIRED_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION}" =~ ^[1-9][0-9]*$ ]]
[[ "${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_PROTOCOL_VERSION}" =~ ^[1-9][0-9]*$ ]]

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create configmap gpu-fault-release-metadata \
  --from-literal=required-agent-artifact-sha256="${REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" \
  --from-literal=compatible-agent-artifact-sha256s="" \
  --from-literal=required-agent-compatibility-digest="${REQUIRED_AGENT_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}" \
  --from-literal=compatible-agent-compatibility-digests="" \
  --from-literal=required-agent-protocol-version="${REQUIRED_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION}" \
  --from-literal=compatible-agent-protocol-versions="" \
  --from-literal=required-regional-executor-protocol-version="${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_PROTOCOL_VERSION}" \
  --from-literal=compatible-regional-executor-protocol-versions="" \
  --from-literal=required-regional-executor-artifact-sha256="${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" \
  --from-literal=compatible-regional-executor-artifact-sha256s="" \
  --from-literal=required-regional-executor-compatibility-digest="${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}" \
  --from-literal=compatible-regional-executor-compatibility-digests="" \
  --from-literal=required-agent-config-digest="${REQUIRED_AGENT_CONFIG_DIGEST}" \
  --from-literal=compatible-agent-config-digests="" \
  --from-literal=required-node-action-key-version=2 \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -

# The current manifests disable DDL at Pod startup; a first deployment must initialise the empty database first.
# The three-step index method: build indexes online first (CONCURRENTLY, no write lock), then run the idempotent DDL, finally the read-only preflight.
# The schema phase of `gpu-fault-admin deploy` runs these three Jobs in the same order via deploy/control-plane/tools/ensure-postgres-schema.sh;
# below are the equivalent manual commands.
# The script deletes each Job right after it completes and its logs are printed, leaving no completed Job objects behind.
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete job/gpu-fault-postgres-index-build --ignore-not-found
sed \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_WHEEL_CONFIGMAP/${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}/g" \
  -e "s/namespace: gpu-fault-system/namespace: ${NAMESPACE}/g" \
  -e "s#public.ecr.aws/docker/library/python:3.12-slim#${GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_IMAGE}#g" \
  deploy/migrations/postgres-index-build-job.yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  wait --for=condition=complete job/gpu-fault-postgres-index-build --timeout=60m
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" logs job/gpu-fault-postgres-index-build
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete job/gpu-fault-postgres-schema-ensure --ignore-not-found
sed \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_WHEEL_CONFIGMAP/${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}/g" \
  -e "s/namespace: gpu-fault-system/namespace: ${NAMESPACE}/g" \
  -e "s#public.ecr.aws/docker/library/python:3.12-slim#${GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_IMAGE}#g" \
  deploy/migrations/postgres-schema-ensure-job.yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  wait --for=condition=complete job/gpu-fault-postgres-schema-ensure --timeout=12m
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" logs job/gpu-fault-postgres-schema-ensure
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete job/gpu-fault-postgres-schema-preflight --ignore-not-found
sed \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_WHEEL_CONFIGMAP/${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}/g" \
  -e "s/namespace: gpu-fault-system/namespace: ${NAMESPACE}/g" \
  -e "s#public.ecr.aws/docker/library/python:3.12-slim#${GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_IMAGE}#g" \
  deploy/migrations/postgres-schema-preflight-job.yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  wait --for=condition=complete job/gpu-fault-postgres-schema-preflight --timeout=5m
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" logs job/gpu-fault-postgres-schema-preflight
```
##### CPU-4c. Deploy and Verify the Three-Tier Role Split

The deployment order cannot be swapped: first let the spool-worker object (0 replicas by default) and the control-worker become ready, and roll
ingress last. Otherwise ingress can return `202` while the queue has no running consumer.

```bash
KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_AWS_REGION="${AWS_REGION}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
GPU_FAULT_WHEEL_CONFIGMAP="${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
GPU_FAULT_WHEEL_SHA256="${ARTIFACT_SHA256}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_AGENT_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="${REQUIRED_AGENT_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_AGENT_CONFIG_DIGEST="${REQUIRED_AGENT_CONFIG_DIGEST}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION="${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION="${REQUIRED_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_PROTOCOL_VERSION="${REQUIRED_REGIONAL_EXECUTOR_PROTOCOL_VERSION}" \
GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_NODE_ACTION_KEY_VERSION=2 \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/apply-control-plane-role-split.sh

CPU_API_POD="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pods -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import boto3,sys; print(boto3.client("sts").get_caller_identity()["Arn"]); print(boto3.client("sagemaker", region_name=sys.argv[2]).describe_cluster(ClusterName=sys.argv[1])["ClusterStatus"])' \
  "${CPU_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER}" "${AWS_REGION}"
```

Before releasing you must simultaneously see ingress `3/3`, control-worker greater than 0 and all Ready,
and spool-worker `0` when spool is disabled by default; the role verifier at the end of the apply script
mechanically checks these conditions as well as each role's ports, connection pool and processor worker boundaries.
When the verifier resolves the container environment it follows kubelet's `optional: true` semantics: when the ConfigMap
`gpu-fault-failure-domain-map` does not exist, `GPU_FAULT_REMEDIATION_FAILURE_DOMAIN_MAP`
is treated as unset rather than as a verification failure; control-worker runs as usual, just without per-failure-domain remediation throttling.
That ConfigMap is rendered automatically by the apply step: the release engine generates the mapping from the node labels of every managed GPU cluster
(`spec.failureDomainLabels` in `site.yaml`, default instance group -> zone),
validates it with the worker's loader before applying, and writes the content digest into the control-worker Pod template annotation
`gpu-fault.io/failure-domain-map-sha256` (`GPU_FAULT_FAILURE_DOMAIN_MAP_SHA256`
passed to the apply script); the worker rolls only when the mapping changes; `join-cluster`/`remove-cluster` likewise
re-render it. Administrators need no manual action; see Administrator Operations §5.1 for details.

The release metadata must exist before any Deployment. Once it runs ahead of the nodes' current heartbeats,
NodeAction fails closed with `PIN_AHEAD_OF_FLEET`; this is the intended protection and must not be bypassed by lowering the gate.
Step 6 must install the Node Runtime wheel of the same release with the same configuration above; the GPU
Deployment must install the Executor wheel. Then use `/v1/fleet/agents` to check that artifact,
compatibility digest, config and protocol have all converged.

When this step fails, first stop accepting new requests, then stop the consumers, and keep the Secrets, Aurora and the content-addressed
ConfigMaps for troubleshooting:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  scale deployment/gpu-fault-api-ha --replicas=0
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  scale deployment/gpu-fault-control-worker --replicas=0
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  scale deployment/gpu-fault-telemetry-spool-worker --replicas=0
```

##### CPU-4d. Register the Regionally Shared Runtime Profile

Runtime Profiles are stored globally by `profile_version` in the regional control plane; the same version is shared by all
GPU clusters that reference it. The payload's `cluster_id` is the registration authorisation anchor and must be a stable
`CLUSTER_ID` in the registry, not a HyperPod provider name; it does not create a private copy for that cluster. When the same version
already exists with different content it must not be overwritten; a new version must be created.

First registration:

```bash
export RUNTIME_PROFILE_SOURCE="$(
  realpath config/runtime-profile.regional-hyperpod-safe.example.yaml
)"
export RUNTIME_PROFILE_REGISTRATION_CLUSTER_ID="${CLUSTER_ID}"

PROFILE_JSON="$(
  python3.12 - \
    "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_SOURCE}" \
    "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_REGISTRATION_CLUSTER_ID}" \
    "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" <<'PY'
import json
import pathlib
import sys
import yaml

payload = yaml.safe_load(
    pathlib.Path(sys.argv[1]).read_text(encoding="utf-8")
)
payload["cluster_id"] = sys.argv[2]
payload["profile_version"] = sys.argv[3]
print(json.dumps(payload, separators=(",", ":")))
PY
)"
PROFILE_B64="$(printf '%s' "${PROFILE_JSON}" | base64 -w0)"

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- env \
  PROFILE_B64="${PROFILE_B64}" \
  PROFILE_VERSION="${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" \
  python -c '
import base64
import json
import os
import urllib.request

payload = base64.b64decode(os.environ["PROFILE_B64"])
request = urllib.request.Request(
    "http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/runtime-profiles",
    data=payload,
    method="POST",
    headers={
        "Content-Type": "application/json",
        "X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token":
            os.environ["GPU_FAULT_EXECUTION_TOKEN"],
    },
)
result = json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=15))
assert result["profile_version"] == os.environ["PROFILE_VERSION"]
assert result["warnings"] == []
print(json.dumps(result, indent=2))
'
unset PROFILE_B64 PROFILE_JSON
```

The returned `warnings` must be empty. The unified orchestrator performs the same check using the configuration file's
`runtime_profile.source/version/registration_cluster_id`: it registers when missing,
stays idempotent when it exists and matches, and stops and requires a new version when it exists but the content has drifted.

##### CPU-4P. Wheel Reclamation After the Release Completes

This section is run only after all of CPU-4c has passed; it is not part of the first-start dependency order.

**Reclaim the accumulated wheel ConfigMaps.** The content-addressed naming above
(`gpu-fault-control-plane-wheel-<tag>-<sha12>`) creates a new object of about
440 KiB on every code change, and **originally there was no reclamation mechanism at all** -- measured on 2026-08-01, the CPU control plane
had 31 piled up and the GPU cluster 98. Run a reclamation once after the rollout completes:

```bash
# Dry run by default: only prints the plan.
KUBECONFIG_PATH="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/prune-wheel-configmaps.sh

# Really delete once the plan has been confirmed correct.
KUBECONFIG_PATH="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  GPU_FAULT_PRUNE_APPLY=true \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/prune-wheel-configmaps.sh
```

On the GPU side replace `KUBECONFIG_PATH` with `KUBE_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}"`.
`deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh` already calls it automatically after creating the ConfigMap
(`GPU_FAULT_PRUNE_WHEELS=false` skips it), so that path needs no manual execution.

The script's retention rules -- **deleting one wrong object breaks rollback, so none of the three can be omitted**:

- **Anything still referenced is always kept**, including `ReplicaSet`s (otherwise the Pods rolled out by `kubectl rollout undo`
  cannot mount the wheel) and **Deployments with 0 replicas**
  (`gpu-fault-cluster-executor` is 0 replicas on the CPU side).
- **The most recent `GPU_FAULT_PRUNE_KEEP_RECENT` (default 3) are kept unconditionally**,
  guaranteeing a rollback target at any time.
- **Only the auto-generated naming `<tag>-<12 hex chars>` is processed.** Hand-pinned experimental wheels
  (such as `gpu-fault-control-plane-wheel-reboot-email-reason-0723`,
  of which the GPU side has 62) are **never in the candidate set**.
- `GPU_FAULT_PRUNE_PROTECT=<name>[,<name>]` can explicitly protect a freshly built wheel
  not yet referenced by any workload; that is how `deploy.sh` protects the artefact of the current build.

Check replicas, node distribution, PDBs and regional mode:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment/gpu-fault-api-ha -o wide
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get pods -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha -o wide
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get pdb gpu-fault-api-ha

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment gpu-fault-api-ha -o json |
jq '{
  replicas: .spec.replicas,
  nodeSelector: .spec.template.spec.nodeSelector,
  terminationGracePeriodSeconds: .spec.template.spec.terminationGracePeriodSeconds,
  env: [.spec.template.spec.containers[0].env[] |
    select(.name == "GPU_FAULT_DEPLOYMENT_MODE"
      or .name == "GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_MODE"
      or .name == "GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_KUBERNETES_ADAPTER"
      or .name == "GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_NODE_ACTION_ADAPTER"
      or .name == "GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER"
      or .name == "GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_TRAINING_HEALTH_MONITOR")]
}'

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get pdb gpu-fault-api-ha -o jsonpath='{.spec.minAvailable}{"\n"}'
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment gpu-fault-control-worker \
  -o jsonpath='{.status.readyReplicas}{"/"}{.spec.replicas}{"\n"}'
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get pdb gpu-fault-control-worker-pdb \
  -o jsonpath='{.spec.maxUnavailable}{"\n"}'
```

Expected values:

- ingress Deployment `3` replicas, Pods spread across the CPU nodes; control-worker
  Deployment `6` replicas and all Ready; spool-worker `0` by default.
- ingress PDB `minAvailable=2`; control-worker PDB `maxUnavailable=1`.
- `GPU_FAULT_DEPLOYMENT_MODE=regional`.
- ingress only receives requests and does not start a processor; control-worker uses
  `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_MODE=active-active` to consume the shared Aurora queue, competing dynamically for work via database lane
  leases, with no need to pre-assign clusters or partitions.
- Each active consumer process uses 24 processor workers by default. XID/SXID, workflow and management changes are serialised by
  `cluster_id`; high-frequency `gpu-metrics`/`host-telemetry` are parallelised by lane keyed on
  `cluster_id + node_id + endpoint`, and use a lower queue priority. Management requests without a cluster
  ID share one serial group.
- The control-worker process runs the workflow dispatcher and XID correlation, relying on the workflow lease
  and the correlation lease respectively to prevent duplicate decisions. training/spare/identity/cleanup use mutually independent
  periodic task leases; there is no global service leader. The lane lease and the request lease use
  the same token/epoch fencing; attempt aggregation continues to be guaranteed by Aurora advisory transaction locks.
- ingress uses `requests.cpu=4`, `limits.cpu=8`; control-worker uses
  `requests.cpu=3`, `limits.cpu=4`.
- The central
  `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_KUBERNETES_ADAPTER/GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_NODE_ACTION_ADAPTER/GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER`
  are all `false`, preventing accidental operations on the CPU EKS.
- `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_TRAINING_HEALTH_MONITOR=false`.

Within the same cluster lane, `/v1/workload-observations`, XID and SXID freeze the
`cluster_id/job_id/attempt_id` correlation key at enqueue time. At claim time only observations with the same key as a not-yet-claimed XID/SXID
are temporarily promoted ahead of the fault request, so that attempt allocation lands in
Aurora first and the XID/SXID is aggregated afterwards. Historical observations of a different attempt or lacking a complete key keep ordinary
priority and cannot suppress the current fault. This rule does not preempt already leased requests.

The processor admission counter uses three independent bucket classes:

```text
priority 0   × 16 shards
priority 50  × 16 shards
priority 100 × 16 shards
```

Different priorities no longer update the same counter row, so routine/evidence transactions cannot hold
the row lock needed by priority 0. The 40 PostgreSQL/Store I/O slots of ingress are split, with spool disabled, into
general 28, evidence 4, fault 8; with spool enabled the spool admission slots must additionally be carved out of general,
and the role verifier checks that the total does not exceed the pool max.

##### CPU-4U. Counter Shards Upgrade for an Already Deployed Environment

This section is only for upgrading from an old version; a greenfield database gets the final structure directly from schema ensure. The upgrade must use
a two-phase switch; old Pods must not be run mixed with the `partitioned` counter mode directly:

1. First run `postgres-schema-ensure-job.yaml`. In `dual` mode the new trigger maintains both
   the legacy total counter and the three-bucket shards; at this point old and new code both continue to use the legacy
   counter as the source of truth.
2. Complete the rollout of all ingress, control-worker and enabled spool-worker Pods, and confirm the
   module digest matches.
3. Confirm the processor queue's `PENDING/LEASED` are both 0.
4. Substitute the current wheel ConfigMap into
   `postgres-counter-shards-finalize-job.yaml` and run it. Under the advisory lock and the
   queue table lock the Job re-seeds the shards and then atomically switches to `partitioned`.
5. Confirm `mode=partitioned`, `ready=true` with
   `gpu-fault-store-migrate --processor-counter-shard-status`.

Rollback is in the reverse order: you must first run
`postgres-counter-shards-rollback-job.yaml` while queue=0, restoring and re-seeding the legacy counter,
and only after confirming `mode=dual` may you roll back to an old wheel that does not understand priority shards. Both Jobs have
a hard cap of 720 seconds; on failure the rollout must not continue.

The execution side is not infinitely strict about priority either: once this process has claimed or is executing priority 0, the GPU/Host
evidence pools each shrink to 1 worker, and evidence already running is not interrupted. Priority 50
keeps progressing; within the same GPU/Host pool, priority 100 that has waited more than 30 seconds is claimed at effective priority 49,
avoiding permanent starvation by continuous evidence. This aging only changes the claim order, not the persisted
priority, and does not preempt already leased requests.

The `gpu_fault_processor_queue.correlation_key` column is idempotently added by the standalone schema ensure Job
(`_add_column_if_missing`, `gpu-fault-postgres-schema-ensure`). Production business Pods keep
`GPU_FAULT_POSTGRES_AUTO_SCHEMA_INIT=false`, executing no DDL at startup and only validating the schema;
run the ensure Job before upgrading.
The upgrade must complete the rollout of all API Pods; `NULL` keys of old records are handled at their original priority. Before go-live, run
`scripts/e2e/regional/manifests/postgres-processor-queue-smoke.yaml` against an isolated schema and confirm the output
`postgres processor queue smoke passed`.

Check the capacity metrics and alerts. `/metrics` exposes the regional queue depth, oldest request latency, per-cluster backlog,
processed counts, processing duration histogram, processor admission rejections, and Store I/O
in-flight, capacity and rejections:

```bash
CPU_API_POD="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pods -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  port-forward "pod/${CPU_API_POD}" 18080:8080
# Run in another terminal:
curl -fsS http://127.0.0.1:18080/metrics |
  grep '^gpu_fault_processor_'
```

After enabling email on a `channel: ses` site, run an SES smoke from the API Pod and obtain a non-empty MessageId;
an SNS site must confirm the subscribed mailbox actually received the drill notification. Checking only environment variables
or the number of notification objects does not count as passing:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c '
import os
import boto3

sender = os.environ["GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_SENDER"]
recipients = [
    item.strip()
    for item in os.environ["GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_RECIPIENTS"].split(",")
    if item.strip()
]
result = boto3.client("sesv2", region_name=os.environ["AWS_REGION"]).send_email(
    FromEmailAddress=sender,
    Destination={"ToAddresses": recipients},
    Content={"Simple": {
        "Subject": {"Data": "GPU fault email smoke", "Charset": "UTF-8"},
        "Body": {"Text": {
            "Data": "Regional control-plane SES delivery smoke.",
            "Charset": "UTF-8",
        }},
    }},
)
print(result["MessageId"])
'
```

Path A (administrator notifications) and path B (ADOT/AMP/SNS metric alerts) are both enabled by default. Path A is the fault notification outlet owned by the control plane,
sending according to `spec.notifications.channel`: new sites default to SNS
(`SnsNotifier`, publishing to the same site topic as the AMP alerts), and `channel: ses` uses
`SesEmailNotifier`; path B provides operational alerts such as queue, capacity and unclaimed remote commands; the two complement each other.

The standalone ADOT Collector (`gpu-fault-system/gpu-fault-adot`) implements **path B** only:
scrape `/metrics` of `gpu-fault-api-ha` -> remote-write to AMP -> AMP evaluates rules
-> Alertmanager -> SNS. It takes no part in fault remediation transactions, but belongs to the production observability baseline:
  the first sub-route of the Alertmanager definition sends `cluster_id=~"perf-cap-.*"` (the synthetic clusters of capacity stress tests) to the `gpu-fault-drill-sink` receiver, which has no delivery configuration: these alerts remain queryable in AMP but do not send email via SNS; the capacity runner checks that the online definition contains this route before registering synthetic clusters and fails closed if it is missing.

- The metrics interaction is one-way pull. ADOT connects directly to Pod IPs via Pod discovery and does not depend on the `gpu-fault-api` Service.
  The control plane does not push metrics to ADOT and there is no push logic in the code; the topic environment variable used for SNS notifications belongs to
  a different sending path and is not a metrics coupling.
- **Administrator notifications (path A) do not depend on ADOT**: the control-plane code publishes directly
  (`src/gpu_fault/notifications/` -> `AdvisoryNotificationService`
  -> `SnsNotifier` or `SesEmailNotifier` chosen by `GPU_FAULT_NOTIFICATION_CHANNEL`),
  without going through the collector, AMP or Alertmanager; an SNS site merely shares the same topic with the metric alerts.
- `deploy/observability/adot-control-plane.yaml` defaults to `replicas: 1`,
  and the installation script waits for it to be Ready by default.
**The scrape path for the data plane's own metrics (`gpu-fault-adot-dataplane`, one per GPU cluster).** The control-plane collector only scrapes
Pods of the CPU cluster; the `/metrics` of the Completion Watcher (9109), the node-installer reconciler (9110) and the cluster executor (9111)
are scraped by the collector rendered from `deploy/dataplane/adot-dataplane.yaml`: via Pod discovery it keeps
Pods in `gpu-fault-system` carrying the `prometheus.io/scrape: "true"` annotation and a named `metrics` port, keeps only the four families
`gpu_fault_completion_.+ | gpu_fault_node_installer_.+ | gpu_fault_cluster_executor_.+ | gpu_fault_node_resource_.+`,
attaches the three static labels `control_plane_cluster` / `region` / `gpu_cluster`, and writes with sigv4 + IRSA to **the same**
AMP workspace. The release engine applies it just like the DCGM exporter (server dry-run -> apply -> `rollout status`):
`bootstrap`, `join-cluster` (onboarding a second GPU cluster) and the `OBSERVABILITY` node in the upgrade plan all
apply the collector on every GPU cluster, and the candidate preflight dry-runs it; `remove-cluster` scales `gpu-fault-adot-dataplane` to 0 together with the other data-plane Deployments
(the `gpu-scaled-down` step of the bootstrap cleanup does the same), leaving no collector still writing to AMP; rollback restores **each GPU cluster's collector objects from the pre-release snapshot**: the snapshot is captured before the transaction opens through each
cluster's own kube context (`observability.dataplane_adot.clusters[<cluster>]`, containing the declared objects and an "absent" marker;
it iterates over the candidate configuration, so clusters newly added to the configuration in the candidate are also in the snapshot, recorded as absent). The data-plane half is a **separate rollback phase** `dataplane_observability_restore` (checkpoints
`rollback-dataplane-observability-restoring` / `-restored`), placed **after** the control-plane restore (`rollback-cpu-restored`) and
before `rollback-restored` -- the control plane comes back first, telemetry second; during restore it applies per cluster from the snapshot and deletes objects absent from the snapshot;
clusters whose apply output is entirely `unchanged` are not restarted (other clusters Recreate once, roughly a 30-60 s scrape gap); clusters without a collector in the snapshot
(including those newly added in the candidate) have the objects installed by the candidate deleted, recorded as `removed`; only clusters added to site.yaml after the transaction opened take the `scaled-to-zero` path,
and clusters removed from site.yaml after the transaction opened make the phase refuse before touching any object. An unreachable cluster or a `rollout status` timeout **does not abort** the other clusters:
it records `failed: <reason>` and continues, and at the end of the phase raises a single error "data-plane observability rollback failed on N of M steps: … -- the rest was
restored; resume the rollback to retry the failed ones"; the rollback is truthfully recorded as failed, and after the fix `--resume` reruns the phase. Afterwards the per-cluster
alert rule namespace `gpu-fault-dataplane-expected` is restored or deleted (waiting for it to stabilise first; after a restore it waits for ACTIVE, after a delete for ResourceNotFound, before recording `restored` / `deleted`).
