The rollback of the control-plane AMP definitions (static rule namespace, Alertmanager definition) uses the same waits: each definition waits to stabilise before the put, and after the put waits until AMP
reports ACTIVE before the next step (300 s budget each); when AMP rejects a definition, the rollback fails before touching the collectors with an error like
`AMP rejected the AMP Alertmanager definition after put-alert-manager-definition: status UPDATE_FAILED: <statusReason>`,
and after the fix `--resume`.
In the rollback record, `path` under `rollback_timing.phases.dataplane_observability_restore.details` for this phase reads `snapshot`
(plus `clusters.<id>`, `expected_rules`, and `error` on failure; `observability_restore.details` keeps only the control-plane collector's
`restored|restored-unchanged` and one line `dataplane_adot: deferred to dataplane_observability_restore`); only when the transaction state predates this version and has no such snapshot does it fall back to
`previous-image` (re-rendering the candidate manifests with the previous ADOT image, and truthfully recording that path). `bootstrap`, `join-cluster` and
`remove-cluster` each rerun the AMP installation at the end to re-render the per-cluster rules: a newly onboarded cluster with a role immediately has its own absent rule, and the rules of a deregistered cluster
disappear accordingly; before `remove-cluster`, first remove it from site.yaml and `deploy` (the OBSERVABILITY node scales the collector to 0), otherwise
there is a 15-minute gap between its collector and its rules during which the alert fires. Prerequisites,
**one set per GPU cluster**:

1. An IAM role, **created automatically** by the bootstrap of the first `gpu-fault-admin deploy` and by `join-cluster` (task `adot_writer_role:<hyperpod>`,
   running after `executor_role:<hyperpod>` and `monitoring_resources`), named `gpu-fault-<site>-<hyperpod>-adot-writer`
   (truncated with an 8-character digest by the same rule as the executor role when over 64 characters); the inline policy `GPUFaultDataplaneAmpWriter` allows only
   `aps:RemoteWrite` to `arn:aws:aps:<region>:<account>:workspace/<AMP_WORKSPACE_ID>`; the trust policy hangs on that GPU EKS's
   OIDC provider (sharing the same provider as the executor role; `sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity`,
   `<issuer>:aud = sts.amazonaws.com`, `<issuer>:sub = system:serviceaccount:gpu-fault-system:gpu-fault-adot-dataplane`).
   It is created only when the site has `health.amp_workspace_id` configured (no workspace means no collector and no role). It is registered as
   `aws/iam/adot-writer/<cluster>/role` in the installation resource inventory; `remove-cluster` and uninstall delete it together with that cluster's other resources,
   and a failed join-cluster rollback deletes it together with the executor role; every bootstrap/deploy re-checks the role with a read-only probe (trust/policy drift
   is corrected). The deployment host needs no new IAM actions -- it uses the same set of calls as creating the executor role.
2. `health.amp_workspace_id` is configured (already required by the control-plane collector).
3. The role ARN on that cluster's entry: `spec.clusters[].adotIrsaRoleArn` is filled in automatically by bootstrap/join-cluster with the freshly created role;
   if site.yaml already has this key written explicitly (an operator-built role), **the explicit value wins**, and regenerating the site does not overwrite it (the automatically created role
   then sits idle and is cleaned up at uninstall). Still **do not** write a placeholder -- a leftover `REPLACE_WITH_…` value makes the render fail closed.
   `config/site.example.yaml` has an example and explanation; in the low-level regional-release JSON the counterpart is
   `clusters[].adot_irsa_role_arn` (`deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-release.example.json`). This field does not enter
   `registry_config_digest` (a `clusters` difference does not flip it), but **does enter the `observability_adot` digest**: the digest is now computed jointly from
   `adot-control-plane.yaml`, `adot-dataplane.yaml`, `health.amp_workspace_id`, each cluster's role ARN and the rendered
   per-cluster alert rule text (`dataplane_expected_rules`),
   so adding the role, adding the workspace or changing the collector manifest is not a NOOP but a `CONTROL_PLANE_ONLY` release: it reruns the AMP installation,
   then visits every GPU cluster with the `OBSERVABILITY` node, applying the collector or printing a skip. The first time a release containing this change is deployed, every site whose state
   already has `observability_adot_sha256` sees `observability_adot` change once (the formula changed), with the consequence being the round trip described above.

When any of these is missing, the release does **not** deploy that cluster's collector: it prints one line on stderr
`<cluster>: data-plane ADOT collector not applied (<reason>); the Completion Watcher alerts stay inert for this cluster`,
`dataplane_observability[<cluster>]` in the plan is an empty list, and nothing is touched. Be informed of the consequences:

- `GpuFaultCompletionActiveStateUnavailable` / `GpuFaultCompletionOutboxAppendFailures` really fire only on clusters where the collector is installed;
- `GpuFaultTelemetryRemoteWriteFailing` / `GpuFaultTelemetryRemoteWriteQueueSaturated` do not select by job, so they also cover
  dropped points and the queue of the data-plane collector (the raw series carry `gpu_cluster`); `GpuFaultAdotSelfMetricsMissing` looks only at the control plane's
  `gpu-fault-adot-self`; the data plane's own job is named `gpu-fault-adot-dataplane-self`, deliberately different; the absence of the data-plane collector is reported by
  `GpuFaultDataplaneCollectorMissing`, in two halves: the static rule (`amp-rules.yaml`, verbatim identical to the repository file) only reports "all targets of some
  `gpu_cluster` have `up == 0` for 15 minutes"; "that cluster's collector has not a single series" is reported by the
  `absent(up{job="gpu-fault-dataplane", gpu_cluster="<cluster>"} == 1)` rule **rendered per cluster** by the release -- one per cluster configured with `adotIrsaRoleArn`,
  written into the separate AMP rule namespace `gpu-fault-dataplane-expected`, and that namespace is deleted when no cluster has a role configured. So a site without
  a role is quiet (no permanent "deployed but no role configured" warning), and with multiple clusters, one dead and one alive, the dead one can still be reported; removing a cluster's role
  is a release, the OBSERVABILITY node scales that cluster's collector to 0, and at the next render its rule disappears. The two Completion Watcher rules are also
  changed to group by `gpu_cluster`;
- The first release carrying this manifest moves the cleanup inventory digests of `rendered_manifests` and the node component, so the node component reruns once
  -- the same behaviour as every previous manifest addition.

Process-side variables for the two new endpoints (both are env of the data-plane containers and must match the manifest's `metrics` containerPort / `prometheus.io/port`;
set 0 to disable; a port bind failure only logs one ERROR line, the main loop runs as usual, and the collector reads `up == 0`):

| Environment variable | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `GPU_FAULT_NODE_INSTALLER_METRICS_PORT` | 9110 | The reconciler's `/metrics` + `/healthz`, family `gpu_fault_node_installer_*` (12 series, counters count events); `/healthz` asks the same question as the exec liveness: whether the heartbeat file is fresh within 300 s |
| `GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_METRICS_PORT` | 9111 | The executor's `/metrics` + `/healthz`, family `gpu_fault_cluster_executor_*` (27 series, same source as the readiness breadcrumb); `/healthz` reads the same loop-alive file |

How to read them: `gpu_fault_node_installer_reconcile_passes_total` flat across several reconcile periods, or
`time() - gpu_fault_node_installer_last_pass_completed_timestamp` growing continuously = the loop is not doing work; `nodes_pending > 0`
with `budget_in_flight == 0` unchanged for a long time = no installation is being scheduled. Executor side: `gpu_fault_cluster_executor_claim_loop_alive == 0`
or `last_loop_iteration_timestamp` older than 300 s = the claim loop has stopped (the same signal as the exec liveness); loop alive but
`last_claim_timestamp` aging = the control plane is unreachable, not a broken executor.

**Consequence of explicitly scaling to 0 replicas (you must be informed): all path B alerts are disabled without exception** --
queue depth, Store I/O, processing duration p95, and
`GpuFaultRemoteCommandUnclaimed`. In that case the scenario "the recovery action was never executed"
**has no "nobody claimed the command" alert**. Secondary detection of stuck training was originally planned to be produced by
`TrainingHealthMonitor` (`GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_TRAINING_HEALTH_MONITOR`) as
`training_hang` CRITICAL findings going through the incident flow.
**That feature is not yet fully implemented and tested as a deliverable capability and currently cannot serve as the production alerting fallback.**
Therefore path B cannot be scaled to 0 replicas; it remains responsible for attributing "training stuck" directly to "command not claimed"
(see `GF-REGIONAL-BOOT-015` incidental finding 2).
Only when another monitored Prometheus/AMP scrape chain has already taken over these rules may you explicitly set
`GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_ADOT=false`; the script never silently scales down a running collector.

The CPU control plane does not require Prometheus Operator by default. The script below creates all the AWS-side resources
needed by path B (IAM role, Pod Identity, AMP rules, Alertmanager, SNS topic),
and keeps the collector at one Ready replica:

```bash
export AWS_REGION='<operator-selected-aws-region>'
export CPU_EKS_CLUSTER='<cpu-eks-cluster>'
export CPU_KUBECONFIG=/tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane.kubeconfig
export AMP_WORKSPACE_ID='<amp-workspace-id>'
export GPU_FAULT_ADOT_IMAGE='<approved-image-visible-in-target-region>'

# Optional; when set, a pending-confirmation SNS email subscription is created.
export GPU_FAULT_ALERT_EMAIL='<operations-email>'

AWS_REGION="${AWS_REGION}" \
CPU_EKS_CLUSTER="${CPU_EKS_CLUSTER}" \
CPU_KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
AMP_WORKSPACE_ID="${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID}" \
GPU_FAULT_ALERT_EMAIL="${GPU_FAULT_ALERT_EMAIL:-}" \
GPU_FAULT_ADOT_IMAGE="${GPU_FAULT_ADOT_IMAGE}" \
deploy/observability/install-amp-monitoring.sh
```

The script has two further optional parameters passed only by the release engine: `--dataplane-expected-rules <file>` (writes the rendered per-cluster
`GpuFaultDataplaneCollectorMissing` rules into the AMP namespace `gpu-fault-dataplane-expected`, on the same
put/create/wait-for-ACTIVE path as the static namespace) and `--no-dataplane-expected-rules` (no cluster has a role configured; idempotently deletes that namespace and waits until AMP reports ResourceNotFound,
so that an immediately following rollback put does not hit a namespace in DELETING). When run manually
**without parameters** as above, the script does not touch that namespace, so a manual rerun does not lose the rules rendered by some deploy. The rollback phase
`dataplane_observability_restore` restores that namespace with the same waits: after put/create it waits until AMP reports ACTIVE, after delete until
ResourceNotFound, before writing `expected_rules: restored/deleted`; when AMP rejects a definition that step is recorded as
`failed: ReleaseError: … status UPDATE_FAILED: <statusReason>`, and resume reruns this idempotent phase.

By default the script requires the SNS topic to have at least one confirmed subscription. Only in the short window when the email subscription is first created and awaiting
manual confirmation may you temporarily set
`GPU_FAULT_REQUIRE_CONFIRMED_SNS_SUBSCRIPTION=false`; after confirmation the gate must be rerun.

The script performs the following idempotent operations:

1. Creates an IAM role with `aps:RemoteWrite` restricted to the target AMP workspace.
2. Establishes an EKS Pod Identity association for `gpu-fault-system/gpu-fault-adot`.
3. Deploys the standalone ADOT Collector manifest and ensures `replicas: 1`;
   does not modify the collector managed by the HyperPod Observability operator.
4. Creates a dedicated SNS topic and allows only the AMP Alertmanager of the same account and workspace to publish messages.
5. Writes the AMP rule namespace and the Alertmanager definition.

Check the AWS-side configuration:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  -n gpu-fault-system get deployment gpu-fault-adot \
  -o jsonpath='{.spec.replicas}{"\n"}'   # must be >= 1
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  -n gpu-fault-system logs deployment/gpu-fault-adot --tail=100

aws amp list-rule-groups-namespaces \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --workspace-id "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID}"
aws amp describe-alert-manager-definition \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --workspace-id "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID}"
aws sns list-subscriptions-by-topic \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --topic-arn "arn:aws:sns:${AWS_REGION}:$(aws sts get-caller-identity \
    --query Account --output text):gpu-fault-control-plane-alerts-${AWS_REGION}"
```

**Amazon Managed Grafana dashboards** are imported automatically by `gpu-fault-admin deploy` after the AMP installation converges;
they are not part of the alerting chain. deploy resolves a workspace in the Region of the CPU cluster ARN (there is no separate Region option):
first `--grafana-workspace-id` (a typo fails the deployment, because this is operator input), then a workspace tagged
`gpu-fault:site-id`, then the only ACTIVE workspace in that Region (which gets the site tag,
for example a workspace created by the HyperPod observability component -- we only reuse it and never delete it); several untagged
workspaces raise an error listing all ids, never guessing; when there is none at all, a new one is created for the customer (`AWS_SSO`, i.e. IAM Identity
Center authentication, `CUSTOMER_MANAGED`, a self-built read-only `aps:Query*` role, tagged `gpu-fault:grafana-created-by`
and written into the installation resource registry as CREATED). There is no switch: dashboards are not the alerting chain and there is no reason to turn them off.
Then, with a one-shot service account token (15 minutes, deleted after use, never entering state or logs), it creates the Prometheus data source
`gpu-fault-amp` (SigV4 `default`, pointing at this site's AMP workspace), verifies it can query, creates the folder
`GPU Fault Recovery` (uid `gpu-fault-recovery`) and imports
`deploy/observability/dashboards/*.json` with `overwrite`; when the JSON changes, the next deploy re-imports automatically. On workspace resolution
ambiguity, creation refused by the Region (for example no IAM Identity Center instance) or a Grafana API refusal, it records
`FAILED`, prints a WARNING (stating the manual fallback: create an Identity Center-authenticated workspace in the console and rerun with
`--grafana-workspace-id`, or in the Grafana UI use Dashboards -> New -> Import and paste the JSON files above one by one,
choosing `GPU Fault AMP (<region>)` as the data source) and continues the deployment; the next deploy retries. The result is written to
`spec.health.grafanaWorkspaceId` in `site.yaml` and to the bootstrap state `resources.monitoring_install.grafana`;
uninstall deletes only the workspace we created (tagged `gpu-fault:grafana-created-by`) and our service account.
A human opening the dashboards still needs Identity Center user authorisation, which deploy does automatically after the import: using the site administrator email
(the `--admin-email` of the first deployment, afterwards read from site.yaml), it queries `aws sso-admin list-instances` in the CPU cluster Region
to obtain the single Identity Center instance and its identity store, then uses `aws identitystore get-user-id` to find that user by
`emails.value` (falling back to `userName`), first checks the roles already held with `aws grafana list-permissions`,
and only when ADMIN is missing runs `update-permissions` to grant **ADMIN** (the administrator can then add
VIEWER for on-call colleagues in the Grafana UI); the result is written to `resources.monitoring_install.grafana.admin_grant`
(`status` is `granted` / `already` / `not-derivable`), and at the end deploy prints
`Grafana ADMIN granted to <email> (Identity Center user <id>)`. When the user cannot be derived (no
Identity Center instance visible in that Region, several instances visible, no user whose email or username equals the administrator email, `identitystore:GetUserId`
denied) the deployment is **not blocked**: it still prints the one line `aws grafana update-permissions …` for that workspace and a sentence about what is
missing (for example `no Identity Center user has email …; create/assign one in IAM Identity Center, then
re-run deploy or run the command above`); after fixing the user, simply rerun deploy -- the read-only probe retries the derivation on every deployment,
and only when the user exists but lacks ADMIN does it rerun the grant task. You can also pass `--grafana-viewer <sso-user-id>` directly and
deploy grants VIEWER on your behalf (this is operator input; a wrong id fails the deployment), taking effect together with the automatic ADMIN grant.
For this the deployment host needs `sso:ListInstances`, `identitystore:GetUserId`, `ec2:DescribeRegions`,
`grafana:ListPermissions`, in addition to the existing `grafana:UpdatePermissions`. The IAM Identity Center instance ARN
(`arn:aws:sso:::instance/ssoins-…`) contains no Region, and `sso-admin list-instances` only answers in its home Region,
so deploy **auto-discovers** the home Region: it queries the CPU cluster Region first, and if nothing is found queries each account-enabled Region listed by `ec2 describe-regions`
one by one (Regions that refuse the call are skipped); the first Region with an instance is the home Region, `identitystore
get-user-id` queries there, while the Grafana-related calls stay in the CPU cluster Region; the discovery result is written to `admin_grant.identity_center_region`
and persisted as `spec.health.identityCenterRegion` in site.yaml, so the next deploy asks that Region directly without scanning
(production 2026-09-09: the workspace was in us-west-2, Identity Center in us-east-1, and before the scan nobody had been granted anything).
The operator can also pre-set `spec.health.identityCenterRegion` (for example `eu-west-1`): then only that Region is asked and no scan occurs,
and the reason for not finding an instance states that it is the Region pointed to by that setting; an invalid Region string fails site validation. When no Region
finds anything, the reason is `no IAM Identity Center instance is visible from <region> or the N other enabled
regions scanned; enable IAM Identity Center for the account, or set spec.health.identityCenterRegion …`.

If the script above was run without `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_ADOT=true`,
and you now decide to enable path B, scale up explicitly here (the Deployment has been created by the script at this point):

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n gpu-fault-system \
  scale deployment/gpu-fault-adot --replicas=1
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n gpu-fault-system \
  rollout status deployment/gpu-fault-adot --timeout=300s
```

The email subscription must be confirmed by the recipient clicking the confirmation link sent by AWS. When no email is configured, the rules and SNS topic
are still deployed normally, but the alerts have no final receiver. The rules cover missing metrics, regional queue 70%/90%, oldest request
over 30 seconds, single cluster 70%, admission rejections occurring, Store I/O over 90% or rejections occurring,
processing duration p95 over 5 seconds, plus remote commands unclaimed for over 600 seconds and executor
internal errors. If thresholds override the default capacity, the absolute values in
`deploy/observability/amp-rules.yaml` must be adjusted in step.

The ARN bootstrap does not resend the confirmation email on every deploy: the Topic carries a generation tag; before the first
`Subscribe` call it writes `REQUESTING` into the bootstrap state, then saves the returned Subscription ARN,
endpoint and the 48-hour pending window. Within the same generation, while pending or when the query temporarily fails, it refuses to
Subscribe again; only when the Topic is deleted and recreated does the generation change, allowing one new necessary confirmation. If the same
Topic/mailbox already has two confirmed subscriptions, or confirmed and pending coexist, bootstrap stops. The administrator should
keep one confirmed subscription, explicitly delete the rest and rerun; do not "try until it works" by repeated deploys.

**First distinguish the two notification paths; this section covers only one of them.**

- **Path A: administrator notifications (via SNS or SES according to `spec.notifications.channel`), not through Prometheus/AMP.**
  **After** a recovery action completes, the control-plane code sends directly; for example a completed node restart goes
  `src/gpu_fault/adapters/hyperpod/notifications.py::HyperPodNotificationMixin._notify_node_restarted`
  `_notify_node_restarted` ->
  `src/gpu_fault/notifications/restart_guard.py::RestartGuardEmailBuilder.build_node_restarted`
  -> `AdvisoryNotificationService.send` -> `SnsNotifier` (default, publishing to the site topic) or
  `SesEmailNotifier` (`channel: ses`); the switches are
  `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_EMAIL` and `GPU_FAULT_NOTIFICATION_DISPATCHER_ENABLED`.
  **This path needs neither ADOT nor AMP**; an SNS site still needs its site topic, and a `channel: ses` site does not need SNS either.
  Nothing in this section concerns it.
- **Path B: operational metric alerts (`/metrics` -> ADOT -> AMP -> Alertmanager -> SNS),
  enabled by default in production** (`gpu-fault-adot` must have at least 1 Ready replica).
  Used only for continuous/stock conditions such as queue depth, Store I/O, processing duration p95 and unclaimed remote commands
  -- these have no "action completed" moment to hang a send point on.
  **The "never loaded" and "only entry point" statements below apply only to path B.**

**(Path B) Adding a metric alert must change two files at once, otherwise nothing has been done.** This control plane
does not use Prometheus Operator; `deploy/control-plane/regional/processor-alerts.yaml`
(`PrometheusRule`) is **never loaded** here; a rule written only there is dead configuration.
And even with a correct rule, the metric must still reach AMP: the ADOT collector's
`metric_relabel_configs` keep whitelist is the **only entry point**; metric families not on the whitelist
are dropped on the scrape side, and the rule stays `absent` forever.
The real-machine re-check of `GF-REGIONAL-BOOT-015` on 2026-08-01 hit both layers at once:
the `gpu_fault_remote_command_*` alerts were only in the `PrometheusRule`, and the metric was blocked by the keep
whitelist, so it could not fire in the real environment. Run this gate after every alert addition:

```bash
python3.12 scripts/verify-regional-alerting.py
```

Only `UNREACHABLE_ALERT_DEFECTS= 0` counts as passing.

Check what is actually loaded in AMP and Alertmanager (rather than only looking at the repository files):

```bash
: "${AWS_REGION:?the Region where AMP lives must be set}"
: "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID:?the AMP workspace ID must be set}"
: "${AMP_RULE_NAMESPACE:=gpu-fault-control-plane-capacity}"

ALERT_VERIFY_DIR="$(mktemp -d)"
aws amp describe-rule-groups-namespace \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --workspace-id "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID}" \
  --name "${AMP_RULE_NAMESPACE}" \
  --query 'ruleGroupsNamespace.data' --output text |
  base64 -d > "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/amp-rules.yaml"
aws amp describe-alert-manager-definition \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --workspace-id "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID}" \
  --query 'alertManagerDefinition.data' --output text |
  base64 -d > "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/alertmanager.yaml"

python3.12 scripts/verify-regional-alerting.py \
  --live-rules "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/amp-rules.yaml" \
  --live-alertmanager "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/alertmanager.yaml"
rm -rf "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}"
```

Passing requires all of: `UNREACHABLE_ALERT_DEFECTS= 0`, `AMP_RULE_GROUPS` contains at least
`gpu-fault-collector-health`, `gpu-fault-control-plane-capacity`,
`gpu-fault-remote-command`, `gpu-fault-policy-coverage` and
`gpu-fault-orchestration-invariants`, and `ALERTMANAGER_SNS_RECEIVERS` is non-empty.

When you need to confirm that an alert really fires, evaluate directly against AMP with SigV4. Beware of two pitfalls:
the control-plane Pod Identity role has no `aps:QueryMetrics` (least privilege, correct),
so administrator credentials must be used; and when the expr contains `>`, GET returns 403 because of a SigV4 canonical query
string encoding difference, so POST + `x-www-form-urlencoded` must be used:

```bash
python3.12 - <<'PY'
import json, os, urllib.parse, urllib.request
import boto3
from botocore.auth import SigV4Auth
from botocore.awsrequest import AWSRequest

region = os.environ["AWS_REGION"]
workspace = os.environ["AMP_WORKSPACE_ID"]
url = (
    f"https://aps-workspaces.{region}.amazonaws.com"
    f"/workspaces/{workspace}/api/v1/query"
)
expr = (
    "max by (cluster_id) "
    "(gpu_fault_remote_command_oldest_unclaimed_seconds) > 600"
)
body = urllib.parse.urlencode({"query": expr})
headers = {"Content-Type": "application/x-www-form-urlencoded"}
signed = AWSRequest(method="POST", url=url, data=body, headers=headers)
SigV4Auth(
    boto3.Session().get_credentials(), "aps", region
).add_auth(signed)
request = urllib.request.Request(
    url, data=body.encode(), headers=dict(signed.headers), method="POST"
)
with urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=30) as response:
    print(json.dumps(json.load(response)["data"]["result"], indent=2))
PY
```

To list the currently firing alerts use `GET /api/v1/alerts` (no `>`, so GET works).

Only when the cluster already uses Prometheus Operator do you **additionally** deploy the compatible
`PrometheusRule`. It is a mirror configuration for sites that bring their own Prometheus,
not the alert source of this control plane:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  get crd prometheusrules.monitoring.coreos.com
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  apply -f deploy/control-plane/regional/processor-alerts.yaml
```
#### CPU-5 to CPU-8. Automated NLB, DNS and TLS Gates

In the current production deployment this phase is executed by `gpu-fault-admin deploy` and the regional rollout orchestrator. A non-empty
Service hostname must no longer be treated as NLB readiness, and the CNAME must not be published before the targets are healthy. The strict order
is as follows; failure of any step stops the subsequent steps:

1. Confirm the private Route53 hosted zone exists and the control-plane hostname is inside that zone.
2. Confirm the ACM certificate is `ISSUED`, not yet expired, with a SAN covering the control-plane hostname.
3. Create or update `Service/gpu-fault-api-nlb`.
4. Wait for the AWS NLB `state=active`, and confirm the single TLS 443 listener uses the target certificate.
5. Resolve the raw `*.elb.<region>.amazonaws.com` NLB DNS from the deployment execution environment.
6. Wait until every NLB target group has at least as many targets as
   `Deployment/gpu-fault-api-ha.spec.replicas`, all of them
   `healthy`.
7. `UPSERT` the private Route53 CNAME so the stable control-plane hostname points at the raw NLB DNS.
8. Wait for the Route53 change waiter to finish, then read the change again; the status must explicitly be `INSYNC`.
9. Create a one-shot check Pod in every GPU EKS; that Pod must be able to resolve the stable CNAME, complete TLS hostname/certificate-chain verification
   with the private CA inside the connection Secret, and obtain `/healthz` HTTP 200.
10. Only after the check Pod succeeds may the Cluster Executor be started for the first time or re-joined.

The implementation entry points are
`src/gpu_fault_release/regional_dns.py` and
`src/gpu_fault_release/rollout.py`. After a failure simply rerun the same
`gpu-fault-admin deploy`; the orchestrator idempotently reuses the resources already created but never bypasses the gates above.

The TCP bootstrap commands of CPU-5 to CPU-8 below are only for recognising and migrating legacy environments; they are not steps of a new
deployment. The old flow's certificate is bound to the raw NLB DNS and cannot be mixed with the current "stable private CNAME + pre-issued certificate" path.

#### Legacy Reference: CPU-5. TCP Bootstrap of the Public NLB

Prepare public subnets in at least two different AZs. They must be filled in explicitly, and their route tables checked for
`0.0.0.0/0 -> igw-*`:

```bash
# Current control-plane values of the sample Region; a new Region/VPC must replace them.
export PUBLIC_SUBNETS='<cpu-subnet-a>,<gpu-subnet-b>,<gpu-subnet-a>,<cpu-subnet-b>'

aws ec2 describe-subnets \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --subnet-ids ${PUBLIC_SUBNETS//,/ } \
  --query 'Subnets[].{id:SubnetId,az:AvailabilityZone,cidr:CidrBlock}' \
  --output table
```

Create the NLB Security Group. Do not open `0.0.0.0/0` in the first phase; first create an SG with no inbound rules,
and in the next step add only the NAT EIPs of each GPU VPC:

```bash
export NLB_SG_CREATED=false
export NLB_SG_ID="$(
  aws ec2 describe-security-groups \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --filters \
      "Name=vpc-id,Values=${CPU_VPC_ID}" \
      "Name=group-name,Values=${NLB_NAME}" \
    --query 'SecurityGroups[0].GroupId' --output text
)"
if [[ "${NLB_SG_ID}" == "None" ]]; then
  export NLB_SG_CREATED=true
  export NLB_SG_ID="$(
    aws ec2 create-security-group \
      --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
      --vpc-id "${CPU_VPC_ID}" \
      --group-name "${NLB_NAME}" \
      --description "Public NLB for GPU fault regional control plane" \
      --query GroupId --output text
  )"
fi
```

The certificate SAN depends on the NLB AWS DNS, and the TLS listener depends on the certificate, so the first pass deletes only the three SSL
annotations and creates a TCP 443 listener:

```bash
sed \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_NLB_NAME/${NLB_NAME}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_PUBLIC_SUBNETS/${PUBLIC_SUBNETS}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_NLB_SECURITY_GROUP/${NLB_SG_ID}/" \
  -e '/aws-load-balancer-ssl-cert:/d' \
  -e '/aws-load-balancer-ssl-ports:/d' \
  -e '/aws-load-balancer-ssl-negotiation-policy:/d' \
  deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-control-plane-nlb.yaml \
  > /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-nlb-bootstrap.yaml

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  apply -f /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-nlb-bootstrap.yaml

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  wait service/gpu-fault-api-nlb \
  --for=jsonpath='{.status.loadBalancer.ingress[0].hostname}' \
  --timeout=600s

export NLB_DNS="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get service gpu-fault-api-nlb \
    -o jsonpath='{.status.loadBalancer.ingress[0].hostname}'
)"
test -n "${NLB_DNS}"
printf 'NLB_DNS=%s\n' "${NLB_DNS}"
```

In the old flow too, a non-empty Service hostname only meant AWS had assigned a name, not that the NLB was ready. You must
continue checking the AWS NLB state, raw DNS and target health; in the current production flow the automated gates above
do this. To revert this step before CPU-6 starts:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete service gpu-fault-api-nlb --ignore-not-found
if [[ "${NLB_SG_CREATED}" == true ]]; then
  aws ec2 delete-security-group \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" --group-id "${NLB_SG_ID}"
fi
```

#### Legacy Reference: CPU-6. Issue a Private Certificate for the NLB AWS DNS

The private CA key and the unencrypted server private key are generated only in a temporary directory with mode `0700`. The NLB DNS usually
exceeds the 64-byte limit of the X.509 CN, so the short CN is only an identifier and the full DNS must go into the SAN:

```bash
export PKI_DIR=/tmp/gpu-fault-regional-pki
install -d -m 0700 "${PKI_DIR}"
umask 077

openssl genrsa -out "${PKI_DIR}/ca.key" 4096
openssl req -x509 -new -sha256 \
  -key "${PKI_DIR}/ca.key" \
  -days 3650 \
  -subj '/O=GPU Fault Regional Control Plane/CN=GPU Fault Private Root CA' \
  -addext 'basicConstraints=critical,CA:TRUE,pathlen:0' \
  -addext 'keyUsage=critical,keyCertSign,cRLSign' \
  -out "${PKI_DIR}/ca.crt"

openssl genrsa -out "${PKI_DIR}/server.key" 2048
openssl req -new -sha256 \
  -key "${PKI_DIR}/server.key" \
  -subj '/O=GPU Fault Regional Control Plane/CN=gpu-fault-regional' \
  -out "${PKI_DIR}/server.csr"

printf '%s\n' \
  'basicConstraints=critical,CA:FALSE' \
  'keyUsage=critical,digitalSignature,keyEncipherment' \
  'extendedKeyUsage=serverAuth' \
  "subjectAltName=DNS:${NLB_DNS}" \
  > "${PKI_DIR}/server.ext"

openssl x509 -req -sha256 \
  -in "${PKI_DIR}/server.csr" \
  -CA "${PKI_DIR}/ca.crt" \
  -CAkey "${PKI_DIR}/ca.key" \
  -CAcreateserial \
  -days 825 \
  -extfile "${PKI_DIR}/server.ext" \
  -out "${PKI_DIR}/server.crt"

openssl verify \
  -CAfile "${PKI_DIR}/ca.crt" \
  -verify_hostname "${NLB_DNS}" \
  "${PKI_DIR}/server.crt"
```

Import into ACM and store the PKI in Secrets Manager. Private keys must not be written into Git, ConfigMaps or ordinary logs:

```bash
export TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN="$(
  aws acm import-certificate \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --certificate "fileb://${PKI_DIR}/server.crt" \
    --private-key "fileb://${PKI_DIR}/server.key" \
    --certificate-chain "fileb://${PKI_DIR}/ca.crt" \
    --query CertificateArn --output text
)"

jq -n \
  --rawfile ca_certificate "${PKI_DIR}/ca.crt" \
  --rawfile ca_private_key "${PKI_DIR}/ca.key" \
  --rawfile server_certificate "${PKI_DIR}/server.crt" \
  --rawfile server_private_key "${PKI_DIR}/server.key" \
  --arg nlb_dns "${NLB_DNS}" \
  --arg certificate_arn "${TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN}" \
  '{
    ca_certificate: $ca_certificate,
    ca_private_key: $ca_private_key,
    server_certificate: $server_certificate,
    server_private_key: $server_private_key,
    nlb_dns: $nlb_dns,
    certificate_arn: $certificate_arn
  }' > "${PKI_DIR}/secret.json"

if aws secretsmanager describe-secret \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --secret-id "${PKI_SECRET_ID}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  aws secretsmanager put-secret-value \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --secret-id "${PKI_SECRET_ID}" \
    --secret-string "file://${PKI_DIR}/secret.json"
else
  aws secretsmanager create-secret \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --name "${PKI_SECRET_ID}" \
    --description "Private PKI for GPU fault regional NLB" \
    --secret-string "file://${PKI_DIR}/secret.json"
fi

aws acm describe-certificate \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --certificate-arn "${TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN}" \
  --query 'Certificate.{status:Status,notAfter:NotAfter,sans:SubjectAlternativeNames}' \
  --output table
```

The success criterion is an ACM certificate that is `ISSUED`, with a SAN exactly equal to `${NLB_DNS}`, and Secrets
Manager able to read the object just written. To revert this step while CPU-8 has not yet referenced the certificate:

```bash
aws acm delete-certificate \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --certificate-arn "${TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN}"
shred -u \
  "${PKI_DIR}/ca.key" \
  "${PKI_DIR}/server.key" \
  "${PKI_DIR}/secret.json"
```

#### Legacy Reference: CPU-7. Whitelist the GPU NAT EIPs

Every GPU VPC must reach the public NLB through the fixed EIP of a NAT Gateway. For every GPU cluster to be registered,
confirm the VPC and the actual outbound NAT Gateway; do not mistake private CIDRs, node private IPs or temporary public IPs
for the source:

```bash
# Current GPU cluster example of the sample Region; replace and run this sub-section separately for each cluster to be registered.
export GPU_EKS_NAME='<gpu-eks-cluster>'
export GPU_VPC_ID="$(
  aws eks describe-cluster \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --name "${GPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --query 'cluster.resourcesVpcConfig.vpcId' --output text
)"

aws ec2 describe-nat-gateways \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --filter \
    "Name=vpc-id,Values=${GPU_VPC_ID}" \
    "Name=state,Values=available" \
  --query 'NatGateways[].{id:NatGatewayId,subnet:SubnetId,eips:NatGatewayAddresses[].PublicIp}' \
  --output table

mapfile -t GPU_NAT_EIPS < <(
  aws ec2 describe-nat-gateways \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --filter \
      "Name=vpc-id,Values=${GPU_VPC_ID}" \
      "Name=state,Values=available" \
    --query 'NatGateways[].NatGatewayAddresses[].PublicIp' \
    --output text |
  tr '\t' '\n' | sort -u
)

for eip in "${GPU_NAT_EIPS[@]}"; do
  if ! sg_error="$(
    aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress \
      --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
      --group-id "${NLB_SG_ID}" \
      --protocol tcp --port 443 \
      --cidr "${eip}/32" 2>&1
  )"; then
    if [[ "${sg_error}" != *InvalidPermission.Duplicate* ]]; then
      printf '%s\n' "${sg_error}" >&2
      exit 1
    fi
  fi
done

aws ec2 describe-security-group-rules \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --filters "Name=group-id,Values=${NLB_SG_ID}" \
  --query 'SecurityGroupRules[?IsEgress==`false`].{protocol:IpProtocol,from:FromPort,to:ToPort,cidr:CidrIpv4}' \
  --output table
```

With several GPU VPCs, run it one by one and keep every EIP `/32`. The NLB SG must never contain TCP 443
`0.0.0.0/0`.

The success criterion is exactly one TCP 443 `/32` rule for every expected EIP, and no wider IPv4/IPv6
source. To revoke this cluster's rules before CPU-8 starts:

```bash
for eip in "${GPU_NAT_EIPS[@]}"; do
  aws ec2 revoke-security-group-ingress \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --group-id "${NLB_SG_ID}" \
    --protocol tcp --port 443 --cidr "${eip}/32"
done
```

#### Legacy Reference: CPU-8. Switch the NLB to a TLS Listener

Render and re-apply the same Service with the final certificate ARN:

```bash
sed \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_NLB_NAME/${NLB_NAME}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_PUBLIC_SUBNETS/${PUBLIC_SUBNETS}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_NLB_SECURITY_GROUP/${NLB_SG_ID}/" \
  -e "s#REPLACE_WITH_TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN#${TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN}#" \
  deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-control-plane-nlb.yaml \
  > /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-nlb-tls.yaml

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  apply -f /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-nlb-tls.yaml

export NLB_ARN="$(
  aws elbv2 describe-load-balancers \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --names "${NLB_NAME}" \
    --query 'LoadBalancers[0].LoadBalancerArn' --output text
)"

until aws elbv2 describe-listeners \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --load-balancer-arn "${NLB_ARN}" \
  --query 'Listeners[?Port==`443` && Protocol==`TLS`].ListenerArn' \
  --output text | grep -q '^arn:'; do
  sleep 10
done

aws elbv2 describe-listeners \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --load-balancer-arn "${NLB_ARN}" \
  --query 'Listeners[].{port:Port,protocol:Protocol,policy:SslPolicy,certificates:Certificates}' \
  --output table
```

The success criterion is a single 443 listener with protocol `TLS`, whose certificate ARN matches
`${TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN}`. When CPU-9 fails and you need to return to the bootstrap state, re-apply
the TCP manifest saved in CPU-5:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  apply -f /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-nlb-bootstrap.yaml

until aws elbv2 describe-listeners \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --load-balancer-arn "${NLB_ARN}" \
  --query 'Listeners[?Port==`443` && Protocol==`TCP`].ListenerArn' \
  --output text | grep -q '^arn:'; do
  sleep 10
done
```

Before cleaning up the local private keys, fetch the CA public certificate from Secrets Manager once and compare:

```bash
aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --secret-id "${PKI_SECRET_ID}" \
  --query SecretString --output text |
jq -r .ca_certificate > /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-bootstrap/control-plane-ca.crt

cmp "${PKI_DIR}/ca.crt" \
  /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-bootstrap/control-plane-ca.crt
rm -rf "${PKI_DIR}"
```

#### CPU-9. Verify Pod IP Targets, TLS and Processor Active-Active Consumption

Confirm the NLB target type is `ip` and all three targets are healthy:

```bash
export TARGET_GROUP_ARN="$(
  aws elbv2 describe-target-groups \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --load-balancer-arn "${NLB_ARN}" \
    --query 'TargetGroups[0].TargetGroupArn' --output text
)"

aws elbv2 describe-target-groups \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --target-group-arns "${TARGET_GROUP_ARN}" \
  --query 'TargetGroups[0].{targetType:TargetType,protocol:Protocol,port:Port,healthProtocol:HealthCheckProtocol,healthPath:HealthCheckPath}' \
  --output table

aws elbv2 describe-target-health \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --target-group-arn "${TARGET_GROUP_ARN}" \
  --query 'TargetHealthDescriptions[].{target:Target.Id,port:Target.Port,state:TargetHealth.State,reason:TargetHealth.Reason}' \
  --output table

test "$(
  aws elbv2 describe-target-health \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --target-group-arn "${TARGET_GROUP_ARN}" \
    --query 'length(TargetHealthDescriptions[?TargetHealth.State==`healthy`])' \
    --output text
)" -eq 3

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get pods -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
  -o custom-columns='POD:.metadata.name,POD_IP:.status.podIP,NODE:.spec.nodeName,HOST_NETWORK:.spec.hostNetwork'
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" get nodes \
  -o custom-columns='NODE:.metadata.name,NODE_IP:.status.addresses[?(@.type=="InternalIP")].address'
```

You must see the three CPU Pod IPs registered as targets, `HOST_NETWORK` empty or `false`, and Pod IPs different from Node IPs.
The VPC CNI assigns routable VPC IPs to Pods, and the NLB does not go through the ClusterIP.

Query `/healthz` and `/metrics` Pod by Pod. All three NLB targets must be
`service_role=ingress` with `gpu_fault_processor_active_consumer=0`; at least one
control-worker must be `service_role=worker` with
`gpu_fault_processor_active_consumer=1`. There is no global processor/service leader:

```bash
for pod in $(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pods -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha -o name
); do
  printf '%s ' "${pod}"
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    exec "${pod}" -- python -c \
    'import json,urllib.request; h=json.load(urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8080/healthz")); m=urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8080/metrics").read().decode(); print(json.dumps({"service_role":h.get("service_role"),"processor_epoch":h.get("processor_epoch"),"active_consumer":next(line.split()[1] for line in m.splitlines() if line.startswith("gpu_fault_processor_active_consumer "))}))'
done

INGRESS_ROLE_COUNT="$(
  for pod in $(
    kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      get pods -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha -o name
  ); do
    kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      exec "${pod}" -- python -c \
      'import json,urllib.request; print(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8080/healthz"))["service_role"])'
  done | grep -c '^ingress$'
)"
test "${INGRESS_ROLE_COUNT}" -eq 3

WORKER_POD="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pods -l app=gpu-fault-control-worker \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${WORKER_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import json,urllib.request; h=json.load(urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8081/healthz")); m=urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8081/metrics").read().decode(); assert h["service_role"]=="worker"; assert next(line.split()[1] for line in m.splitlines() if line.startswith("gpu_fault_processor_active_consumer "))=="1"; print("worker consumer ready")'
```

Strict GPU VPC to NLB TLS verification is only possible after completing the later §5.3 "Static registration of regional GPU clusters" and creating
`gpu-fault-regional-connection/ca.crt`. `curl -k` is forbidden. After registration run at least:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete pod gpu-fault-nlb-probe --ignore-not-found
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" run \
  gpu-fault-nlb-probe \
  --image=curlimages/curl:8.14.1 \
  --restart=Never \
  --overrides="$(
    jq -n --arg url "${CONTROL_PLANE_URL}" '{
      spec: {
        containers: [{
          name: "probe",
          image: "curlimages/curl:8.14.1",
          command: ["sh", "-c"],
          args: ["i=0; while [ $i -lt 300 ]; do curl --fail --silent --show-error --cacert /pki/ca.crt \"$CONTROL_PLANE_URL/healthz\" >/dev/null || exit 1; i=$((i+1)); sleep 1; done"],
          env: [{name: "CONTROL_PLANE_URL", value: $url}],
          volumeMounts: [{name: "pki", mountPath: "/pki", readOnly: true}]
        }],
        volumes: [{
          name: "pki",
          secret: {
            secretName: "gpu-fault-regional-connection",
            items: [{key: "ca.crt", path: "ca.crt"}]
          }
        }]
      }
    }'
  )"
```

Run the next block immediately after the `kubectl run` above returns; do not wait for the probe to finish. During the 300 probes,
randomly delete one ingress Pod and verify zero API failures, and that the NLB target of the deleted Pod is replaced by the new Pod.
The control-worker consumes the queue independently and does not stop because an ingress Pod is replaced:

```bash
ACTIVE_POD="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pods -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"
test -n "${ACTIVE_POD}"

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete pod "${ACTIVE_POD}"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status deployment/gpu-fault-api-ha --timeout=300s

until [[ "$(
  aws elbv2 describe-target-health \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --target-group-arn "${TARGET_GROUP_ARN}" \
    --query 'length(TargetHealthDescriptions[?TargetHealth.State==`healthy`])' \
    --output text
)" -eq 3 ]]; do
  sleep 5
done

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  wait pod/gpu-fault-nlb-probe --for=jsonpath='{.status.phase}'=Succeeded \
  --timeout=420s
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs gpu-fault-nlb-probe
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete pod gpu-fault-nlb-probe
```

This is the real network and failover acceptance; checking only Deployment Ready, unit tests or mocks is not equivalent.
After verification, delete the locally rendered files that contain no long-term credentials:

```bash
rm -f \
  /tmp/aws-load-balancer-controller-iam-policy.json \
  /tmp/lbc-pod-identity-trust.json \
  /tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-pod-identity-trust.json \
  /tmp/gpu-fault-control-plane-sagemaker-read.json \
  /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-control-plane.yaml \
  /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-nlb-bootstrap.yaml \
  /tmp/gpu-fault-regional-nlb-tls.yaml
```

<a id="5a2-从集群内控制面迁移到区域控制面"></a>
### 5.2 Migrating from an In-Cluster Control Plane to the Regional Control Plane

This section targets environments that already run the single-cluster all-in-one control plane inside the GPU cluster and want to move to the regional control plane.
A greenfield deployment does not include this part. The prerequisite is that the three-replica regional control plane has been deployed per the previous chapter and has passed
the Pod IP target, TLS and Processor active-active consumption verification.

The order cannot be swapped: every step provides a revertible observation window for the next.

1. **The control plane starts observe-only.** After the regional control plane is deployed, keep the workflow executor
   in observation state and open no destructive operation. At this point the old in-cluster control plane is still working;
   the two coexist but only the old control plane executes actions.
2. **Onboard the data plane of the first GPU cluster.** Complete registration,
   credentials, private CA and Executor deployment per §5.3, and point that cluster's Collector, Completion Watcher and
   Node Agent at the regional control plane's TLS endpoint. When migrating existing nodes you must update
   the URL, per-cluster token, Node Action signing key and CA trust configuration together, then restart the services.
3. **Verify the scope is correct.** Confirm that events, topology and training terminal states are written only into the correct `cluster_id` scope,
   not into other clusters or the control plane itself. This step is the foundation of all subsequent isolation guarantees;
   do not continue if it fails.
4. **Open capabilities step by step.** Follow the phase A -> B -> C -> D order of §10 enabling destructive capabilities in phases,
   without skipping levels; those criteria are not repeated in this section.
5. **Verify reset/reboot/replace in a maintenance window.** Only afterwards onboard the second GPU cluster,
   repeating steps 2-4 for the second cluster.
6. **Stop and delete the control plane in the original GPU cluster.** Delete only the old control-plane resources,
   **not the data-plane components** -- the Collector, Watcher, Node Agent and Executor stay in the
   GPU cluster, now serving the regional control plane. Scaling to 0 alone is not enough: a 0-replica template can still be scaled up by mistake,
   and the legacy `gpu-fault-control-plane-active` would leave the administrator execution token in
   the data plane. After confirming the regional executor/collector are Ready, run:

   ```bash
   kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" delete \
     deployment gpu-fault-api-ha gpu-fault-api-canary \
     --ignore-not-found
   kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" delete \
     poddisruptionbudget gpu-fault-api-ha \
     service gpu-fault-api-canary \
     secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
     --ignore-not-found

   # The data plane must not hold the administrator execution token.
   kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
     get secret -o json |
   jq '[.items[] | select((.data // {}) | has("execution-token")) |
        .metadata.name] | length'
   ```

   The last command must print `0`.

The go-live gates are in [High-Level Design](high-level-design.md) §10, and the item-by-item verification steps in
[Regional Mode End-to-End Acceptance Test Cases](regional-e2e-acceptance-test-cases.md).
Production-grade multi-cluster E2E must not be declared complete until all gates pass.

<a id="5a3-区域级-gpu-集群静态注册"></a>
### 5.3 Static Registration of Regional GPU Clusters

> **On the normal path REG-1 to REG-8 are completed by the administrator CLI.** The first deployment uses `gpu-fault-admin
> deploy`; adding a cluster uses `gpu-fault-admin join-cluster --state-dir ...
> --gpu-cluster-arn ...`. The item-by-item commands in this section are only for audit, migration and break-glass.

This section registers one GPU HyperPod EKS to a standalone CPU control plane in the same Region. There is currently no self-service
registration API; the administrator must update the control-plane registry and distribute the same set of credentials to the GPU cluster.

#### REG-1. Pin the Control-Plane and Target GPU Cluster Contexts

The variables below describe one registration operation: one fixed regional CPU control plane plus the one GPU
cluster currently being registered. With several GPU clusters in the Region, the CPU kubeconfig stays the same, and each GPU cluster gets
its own `CLUSTER_ID`, `GPU_EKS_CONTEXT`, EKS ARN, token and namespace allowlist;
run this section for each one and accept each separately. The system actually uses `1 + N` Kubernetes contexts, not just two.

All commands must explicitly specify the target cluster of this operation:

```bash
: "${REGIONAL_ENV_FILE:=/secure/gpu-fault/regional-env.sh}"
set -a
. "${REGIONAL_ENV_FILE}"
set +a
regional_require \
  AWS_REGION CPU_KUBECONFIG GPU_EKS_CONTEXT NAMESPACE \
  GPU_EKS_NAME GPU_EKS_ARN CLUSTER_ID HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME \
  HYPERPOD_CONFIRM_CLUSTER_NAME ALLOWED_NAMESPACES SECURE_DIR

regional_validate_region
regional_assert_eks_arn_region GPU_EKS_ARN
test "${HYPERPOD_CONFIRM_CLUSTER_NAME}" = "${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}"

ACTUAL_GPU_EKS_ARN="$(
  aws eks describe-cluster \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --name "${GPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --query 'cluster.arn' --output text
)"
test "${ACTUAL_GPU_EKS_ARN}" = "${GPU_EKS_ARN}"
GPU_HYPERPOD_EKS_ARN="$(
  aws sagemaker describe-cluster \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
    --query 'Orchestrator.Eks.ClusterArn' --output text
)"
GPU_NODE_RECOVERY="$(
  aws sagemaker describe-cluster \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
    --query 'NodeRecovery' --output text
)"
test "${GPU_HYPERPOD_EKS_ARN}" = "${GPU_EKS_ARN}"
test "${GPU_NODE_RECOVERY}" = "None"

install -d -m 0700 "${SECURE_DIR}"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" get --raw=/readyz
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" get --raw=/readyz
```

For example, the same Region can maintain the following inventory:

| cluster_id | Kubernetes context | Independent credential |
|---|---|---|
| `gpu-training-a` | `arn:aws:eks:...:cluster/gpu-a` | token A |
| `gpu-training-b` | `arn:aws:eks:...:cluster/gpu-b` | token B |
| `gpu-training-c` | `arn:aws:eks:...:cluster/gpu-c` | token C |

Do not let several GPU clusters share a `cluster_id` or token, and do not rely on the current default context within a single command.
#### REG-2. Generate the Per-Cluster Token and Update the Control-Plane Registry

The `gpu-fault-regional-clusters/clusters.json` Secret serves as the registry seed only at the first bootstrap,
and afterwards as a disaster-recovery copy: once the control plane is running, the Aurora persistent registry head/revision is the true source; modifying the Secret and
rolling Pods does **not** change the runtime membership. Later joins, removals and rotations use the bound `join-cluster`,
`remove-cluster` and `rotate-token`, which publish a revision via `/v1/regional/registry` and verify the fleet ACK;
the manual write-up below is only for the first cluster seed before the control plane is deployed, or for a DR rebuild after identity and database state have been verified.

**The execution order for the first cluster differs from later clusters.** The regional-mode startup guard refuses an empty registry, so this step for the first
cluster must be completed before the control plane is deployed; at that point `deployment/gpu-fault-api-ha` does not exist yet,
the restart script finds no Deployment and returns safely, and the registry is loaded from the Secret seed when the control plane first starts.
The 2nd and later clusters are onboarded by `join-cluster` via the registry API; rolling the CPU to import the Secret cannot
replace a persistent revision and ACK; writing the Secret at that point only keeps the disaster-recovery copy consistent with the registry, and rolling the running
roles does not import it.

The token must be independent per cluster and must not contain a newline. On repeated runs reuse the token already generated, to avoid updating only the control-plane
side and leaving the GPU data plane temporarily unauthenticated:

```bash
umask 077
if [[ ! -s "${SECURE_DIR}/cluster-token" ]]; then
  TOKEN="$(openssl rand -hex 32)"
  printf '%s' "${TOKEN}" > "${SECURE_DIR}/cluster-token"
  unset TOKEN
fi

# On the first registration the Secret does not exist; start with an empty array.
# Later registrations must read the existing array first and must not overwrite other GPU clusters.
if kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  -n "${NAMESPACE}" get secret gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
  >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
    -n "${NAMESPACE}" get secret gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
    -o jsonpath='{.data.clusters\.json}' |
    base64 -d > "${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.current.json"
else
  printf '[]\n' > "${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.current.json"
fi

# Remove the old entry with the same cluster_id, then append this entry; other clusters stay unchanged.
jq \
  --arg cluster_id "${CLUSTER_ID}" \
  --arg region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --arg hyperpod "${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
  --arg eks_arn "${GPU_EKS_ARN}" \
  --arg namespaces "${ALLOWED_NAMESPACES}" \
  --rawfile token "${SECURE_DIR}/cluster-token" \
  'map(select(.cluster_id != $cluster_id)) + [{
    cluster_id: $cluster_id,
    region: $region,
    hyperpod_cluster_name: $hyperpod,
    eks_cluster_arn: $eks_arn,
    token: $token,
    allowed_namespaces: ($namespaces | split(","))
  }]' \
  "${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.current.json" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.json"

# Write to the CPU EKS. --dry-run + apply supports both first creation and later updates.
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  -n "${NAMESPACE}" create secret generic \
  gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
  --from-file=clusters.json="${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.json" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -

# The Secret is injected via GPU_FAULT_REGIONAL_CLUSTERS_JSON and serves only as the first seed; once an Aurora
# registry durable head exists, rolling does not reload the Secret. Roll all non-zero-replica roles in the order spool-worker, control-worker,
# ingress; for the first cluster, run before the Deployments are created, it is a safe no-op.
KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/restart-regional-control-plane.sh

# Does not print the token; only confirms the target entry exists and the token length qualifies.
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  -n "${NAMESPACE}" get secret gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.clusters\.json}' |
base64 -d |
jq --arg cluster_id "${CLUSTER_ID}" \
  '[.[] | select(.cluster_id == $cluster_id) |
    (.token | length >= 32) as $token_ok |
    del(.token) + {token_configured: $token_ok}]'
```

The verification output must contain exactly one target `cluster_id`. An empty array means the written ID and the queried ID
differ; several entries mean the registry file was already corrupted before this upsert, and the GPU data plane must not be deployed
further.

#### REG-3. Prepare the Node Action Keys and the Private CA

The CPU control plane keeps the fleet master; the GPU Node Agent and Cluster Executor receive only
node-specific keys derived by `(cluster_id, node_id)`. First stage the master on a trusted deployment host;
it **must not** be written into a GPU Kubernetes Secret:

```bash
if [[ -z "${CONTROL_PLANE_URL:-}" ||
  "${CONTROL_PLANE_URL}" == REPLACE_* ]]; then
  export CONTROL_PLANE_URL="https://$(
    kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
      -n "${NAMESPACE}" get service gpu-fault-api-nlb \
      -o jsonpath='{.status.loadBalancer.ingress[0].hostname}'
  )"
fi
regional_require CONTROL_PLANE_URL

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  -n "${NAMESPACE}" get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.node-action-secret}' |
  base64 -d > "${SECURE_DIR}/node-action-secret"
chmod 0600 "${SECURE_DIR}/node-action-secret"
```

Save the public certificate of the CA that issued the NLB certificate as:

```text
${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-ca.crt
```

If the CA has been stored in Secrets Manager per this solution, you can export only the public certificate:

```bash
export NLB_PKI_SECRET_ID=gpu-fault/regional-control-plane/nlb-private-pki
aws secretsmanager get-secret-value \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --secret-id "${NLB_PKI_SECRET_ID}" \
  --query SecretString --output text |
  jq -r .ca_certificate > "${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-ca.crt"
chmod 0644 "${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-ca.crt"
```

Verify the CA and the server hostname with the following commands; `-k` is forbidden:

```bash
openssl x509 -in "${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-ca.crt" -noout \
  -subject -issuer -dates
curl --fail --cacert "${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-ca.crt" \
  "${CONTROL_PLANE_URL}/healthz"
```

If the NLB Security Group only allows the GPU NAT EIPs, the `curl` above should be run from inside the GPU VPC.

#### REG-4. Create the Connection Secret in the GPU EKS

```bash
: "${ALLOWED_NAMESPACES:?the training namespaces allowed for operation must be configured explicitly}"
: "${HYPERPOD_CONFIRM_CLUSTER_NAME:?the target HyperPod cluster name must be confirmed by a human}"
printf '%s' "${CONTROL_PLANE_URL}" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-url"
printf '%s' "${CLUSTER_ID}" > "${SECURE_DIR}/cluster-id"
printf '%s' "${ALLOWED_NAMESPACES}" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/allowed-namespaces"
printf '%s' "${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/hyperpod-cluster-name"
printf '%s' "${HYPERPOD_CONFIRM_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/hyperpod-confirm-cluster-name"

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create secret generic gpu-fault-regional-connection \
  --from-file=control-plane-url="${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-url" \
  --from-file=cluster-id="${SECURE_DIR}/cluster-id" \
  --from-file=cluster-token="${SECURE_DIR}/cluster-token" \
  --from-file=allowed-namespaces="${SECURE_DIR}/allowed-namespaces" \
  --from-file=hyperpod-cluster-name="${SECURE_DIR}/hyperpod-cluster-name" \
  --from-file=hyperpod-confirm-cluster-name="${SECURE_DIR}/hyperpod-confirm-cluster-name" \
  --from-file=ca.crt="${SECURE_DIR}/control-plane-ca.crt" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" apply -f -

FLEET_MASTER_FILE="$(mktemp)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.node-action-secret}' |
base64 -d > "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"
chmod 0600 "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"

GPU_FAULT_KUBECTL_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_ID="${CLUSTER_ID}" \
GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER="${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
GPU_FAULT_FLEET_MASTER_FILE="${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  deploy/node/provision-node-action-keys.sh

# Both the CPU and GPU sides now hold only node-specific keys; destroy the local master immediately.
shred -u "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"
```

#### REG-5. Sync the Executor Wheel and Deploy the Cluster Action Executor

**The Executor is deployed only on the GPU cluster side (current design, confirmed 2026-08-01).** It needs to operate directly on
data-plane nodes and training workloads, so it belongs to the GPU EKS and connects back to the CPU control plane through the NLB
to claim commands; there should be no running executor on the CPU control-plane side.

If you see `deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor` in the CPU control-plane cluster,
it is a leftover from early deployment experiments: it **must stay at 0 replicas**. Keeping the 0-replica object is safe
(it claims no commands), but **do not** scale it to 1 -- that would make the control-plane cluster claim, under its own identity,
commands that should be executed on the GPU side. The wheel reclamation script above keeps the wheel it references according to
the reference relationship, precisely for such 0-replica objects.

The CPU API, GPU Executor and node Agent must come from the same release, but each installs its own component:

The manual shared-image example below is only for compatibility break-glass on confirmed schema-v3. schema-v4 must use
the signed Manifest's separate CPU/Executor images and the Node offline dependency identity, rendered via the unified deploy;
`GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_IMAGE` cannot replace the Executor pin, nor can only the wheel ConfigMap be swapped.

```bash
export EXECUTOR_WHEEL="$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py executor-wheel
)"
test -s "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}"
export ARTIFACT_SHA256="$(
  sha256sum "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}" | awk '{print $1}'
)"
export EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="$(
  jq -er '.components.executor.module_digest' dist/current-release.json
)"
[[ "${ARTIFACT_SHA256}" =~ ^[0-9a-f]{64}$ ]]
export WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP="gpu-fault-executor-wheel-0100-${ARTIFACT_SHA256:0:12}"

if kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get configmap "${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  # When the key name ends in .xz, decompress before verifying (the release engine stores the wheel xz-compressed).
  EXISTING_WHEEL_KEY="$(
    kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      get configmap "${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" -o json |
    jq -r '.binaryData | keys[0]'
  )"
  EXISTING_WHEEL_SHA="$(
    kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
      get configmap "${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" -o json |
    jq -r --arg key "${EXISTING_WHEEL_KEY}" '.binaryData[$key]' |
    base64 -d |
    { if [[ "${EXISTING_WHEEL_KEY}" == *.xz ]]; then xz -d; else cat; fi; } |
    sha256sum | awk '{print $1}'
  )"
  test "${EXISTING_WHEEL_SHA}" = "${ARTIFACT_SHA256}"
else
  # Consistent with the release engine: store the xz-compressed wheel under the key <wheel>.whl.xz. This ConfigMap is only
  # a pin/identity artefact; the Executor runs from the signed image (the image has the executor built in, PATH points at
  # /opt/gpu-fault/executor/bin) and does not pip install from /artifact inside the Pod.
  xz -9e -c "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}" > "/tmp/$(basename "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}").xz"
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    create configmap "${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
    --from-file="$(basename "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}").xz=/tmp/$(basename "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}").xz"
fi

# The GPU side also accumulates wheel ConfigMaps (98 measured on 2026-08-01); reclaim after the rollout completes;
# protect the one just built -- it is not yet referenced by any workload.
KUBE_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  GPU_FAULT_PRUNE_PROTECT="${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/prune-wheel-configmaps.sh

# The executor's IRSA role ARN. The manifest holds the placeholder
# REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN, which **must** be replaced: without this annotation the Pod has
# no AWS credentials at all, and an executor with GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER=true
# fails and exits at startup with missing_aws_credentials (this is deliberate --
# before 2026-08 it started up perfectly fine, until hours later a card really broke and
# REPLACE_NODE died on NoCredentialsError).
# Regional mode must use a data-plane-specific role and must not reuse the single-cluster
# gpu-fault-control-plane role. The latter usually contains ses:SendEmail; reusing it would hand notification permission
# down to the GPU EKS. The trust of the dedicated role only allows
# system:serviceaccount:gpu-fault-system:gpu-fault-cluster-executor.
# The SageMaker Allow whitelist may only contain:
# DescribeCluster, ListClusterNodes, DescribeClusterNode,
# BatchRebootClusterNodes. ses:*, BatchReplaceClusterNodes,
# BatchDeleteClusterNodes and sagemaker:* are forbidden; workload log S3 PutObject/
# AbortMultipartUpload may be granted separately per target prefix.
export EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN="arn:aws:iam::<account-id>:role/<executor-role>"

# The ARN contains "/", so this substitution uses "#" as the delimiter.
sed \
  -e "s/gpu-fault-executor-wheel-0100/${WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}/g" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256/${ARTIFACT_SHA256}/g" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST/${EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}/g" \
  -e "s#public.ecr.aws/docker/library/python:3.12-slim#${GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_IMAGE}#g" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_AWS_REGION/${AWS_REGION}/g" \
  -e "s#REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN#${EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN}#" \
  deploy/dataplane/cluster-action-executor.yaml \
  > /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml
grep -n 'role-arn\|REPLACE_WITH' /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  apply -f /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  --timeout=180s

# Data-plane self-check: the SA has a real role annotation, the web identity token is really injected into the Pod,
# the wheel ConfigMap matches the control plane's required Executor pin, all three ENABLE_* are true,
# and the Secret keys needed by the enabled adapters
# are all present. If any item fails, the exit code is non-zero and the reason is printed.
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  GPU_FAULT_KUBE_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  python3 deploy/dataplane/tools/verify_dataplane_executor.py

# During bootstrap/join/upgrade the regional lifecycle orchestrator also reads all inline/attached policies of this role
# and performs the same whitelist check; over-privilege fails before any Deployment change.
deploy/control-plane/regional/rollout-regional-release.sh status \
  --config /secure/regional-release.json
```

When the ConfigMap already exists, first check the SHA-256 of the wheel inside it; a same-named content-addressed ConfigMap must not be overwritten.

**Adding the IRSA annotation on an already running cluster must be followed immediately by a rollout restart.** The EKS
Pod Identity webhook injects the `aws-iam-token` projected volume and
`AWS_ROLE_ARN`/`AWS_WEB_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE` only **at Pod creation**; annotating the ServiceAccount does not
change existing Pods, which keep running in the "no credentials" state:

<!-- boot017-out-of-band: retrofitting the annotation on an existing cluster; not part of the first-deployment order -->

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" annotate sa \
  gpu-fault-cluster-executor --overwrite \
  "eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn=${EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN}"
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout restart deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor
```

`deploy/dataplane/tools/verify_dataplane_executor.py` checks exactly this: it compares the Pod's `AWS_ROLE_ARN` with
the SA's current annotation and runs `test -s` on that token file inside the container, so both "annotated but not restarted"
and "never annotated" are judged as failures.

**The executor's readiness is a real claim-capability probe, not `/healthz`.**
The probe runs `gpu-fault-cluster-executor-readiness`; the container's declared `PATH` prefers
`/opt/gpu-fault/executor/bin`, falling back to the original global entry point when an old image is rolled back. The probe POSTs with the cluster token to
the control plane's `/v1/regional/executors/readiness`, so Ready simultaneously proves: the TLS chain
works, the cluster token is valid, the control plane knows this cluster_id, the
`execution_owners` announced by this executor cover the owners of the backlog commands, the oldest unclaimed command has not exceeded
`GPU_FAULT_EXECUTOR_READINESS_MAX_UNCLAIMED_SECONDS`, and the last successful claim has not exceeded
`GPU_FAULT_EXECUTOR_READINESS_MAX_CLAIM_AGE_SECONDS`. If any item fails it returns
503 and the Pod turns NotReady.

The old anonymous `/healthz` probe returned 200 just the same for an executor with a wrong token, wrong CA or completely mismatched owners,
which is exactly how "commands stay PENDING forever while the Pods are all green" came about.
When troubleshooting, you can run the same command by hand to see the reason:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" exec \
  deploy/gpu-fault-cluster-executor -- gpu-fault-cluster-executor-readiness
```

Note that this entry point ships with the wheel: **on a cluster whose wheel ConfigMap has not been re-rolled the command does not exist**,
and the probe fails continuously.

#### REG-6. Install the Collector and Node Agent on Every GPU Node

This section is the node delivery step of the regional data plane. Automatic installation by the Installer Job + Reconciler is recommended;
environments that cannot use privileged Jobs run the same installer per §5.5.

Securely distribute the node installer package of the same release, the CA, the control-plane URL, the cluster ID, the cluster token,
the node-specific Node Action Secret, the Kubernetes Node name and the Node UID to every GPU
node, saving them as `/secure/control-plane-url`, `/secure/cluster-id`,
`/secure/cluster-token`, `/secure/node-action-secret`, `/secure/node-name` and
`/secure/node-uid`, and save the Node InternalIP as `/secure/node-private-ip`. First pin the identity on the
management host:

```bash
export NODE_NAME='<Kubernetes Node name being installed>'
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" get node "${NODE_NAME}" \
  -o jsonpath='{.metadata.name}{"\n"}{.metadata.uid}{"\n"}{.status.addresses[?(@.type=="InternalIP")].address}{"\n"}'
```

Save the three lines on the node as `/secure/node-name`, `/secure/node-uid` and
`/secure/node-private-ip` respectively. The installer package must be extracted first; no repository checkout is assumed on the node.
Then run on every node:

```bash
sudo install -d -m 0755 /opt/gpu-fault-installer
sudo tar -xzf /secure/gpu-fault-node-installer-0.10.0.tar.gz \
  -C /opt/gpu-fault-installer
cd /opt/gpu-fault-installer/gpu-fault-node-installer-0.10.0

sudo ./deploy/node/install-gpu-fault-collector.sh \
  --control-plane-url "$(cat /secure/control-plane-url)" \
  --ca-certificate /secure/control-plane-ca.crt \
  --cluster-id "$(cat /secure/cluster-id)" \
  --runtime-profile-version "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION:?}" \
  --token-file /secure/cluster-token \
  --wheel ./dist/gpu_fault_node_runtime-0.10.0-py3-none-any.whl \
  --wheel-sha256 "$(cat /secure/node-runtime-wheel.sha256)" \
  --metrics-mode dcgm \
  --dcgm-exporter existing \
  --enable-node-agent \
  --node-action-secret-file /secure/node-action-secret \
  --node-action-key-version 2 \
  --allow-gpu-reset \
  --allow-fabric-reset \
  --allow-service-quiesce \
  --allow-fabric-manager-restart \
  --node-id "$(cat /secure/node-name)" \
  --node-agent-advertise-url \
    "https://$(cat /secure/node-private-ip):9099" \
  --node-instance-id "$(cat /secure/node-uid)"
```

Security constraints: do not put the cluster token directly on the command line with `--token` (argv is visible to every process on the same machine);
use `--token-file` instead, pointing at a 0600 absolute-path file (`--token` and `--token-file`
are mutually exclusive). `--wheel-sha256` is now **mandatory**: pass the 64-hex-digit
wheel SHA-256 from the pinned release record (from the release artefacts, not from the installer package itself). The installer checks that both the wheel file to be installed and
the release manifest match that value, aborting fail-closed on a mismatch; the dependency closure is likewise installed only from the hash lock with
`pip install --require-hashes`, never resolving unpinned packages online. That lock
(`requirements/node-runtime.lock`) is the complete closure of the `collectors` extra, generated by
`uv export --frozen --no-dev --no-emit-project --extra collectors`; **do not** use
`--no-emit-package` to strip any package: `prometheus-client` is imported only inside a function of the DCGM collector,
invisible to a top-level scan; on 2026-09-09 a lock that stripped it was installed on the first canary node,
`gpu-fault-metrics-collector` hit ModuleNotFoundError on every scrape, the node sent not a single metric,
the installation validation's "metrics delivered" step kept failing, and the release stalled in the data-plane pause. The test
`test_node_runtime_lock_pins_every_third_party_import_of_the_node_closure`
now checks the lock import by import against the node-runtime wheel closure.

Do not pass `--enable-node-log-collector` or
`--enable-nvidia-smi-metrics-collector`. After installation run:

```bash
sudo /opt/gpu-fault/verify
systemctl is-active \
  gpu-fault-kernel-collector \
  gpu-fault-fabric-manager-collector \
  gpu-fault-metrics-collector \
  gpu-fault-host-collector \
  gpu-fault-node-agent
if systemctl is-enabled --quiet gpu-fault-log-collector; then
  echo "gpu-fault-log-collector must remain disabled" >&2
  exit 1
fi
```

New nodes must run the same installation flow automatically via a HyperPod lifecycle script or a controlled installation Job; do not
rely on manual retrofitting.

Every new GPU node ends up with the following host systemd services installed and started:

| Service | Purpose | Default state |
|---|---|---|
| `gpu-fault-kernel-collector` | Continuously reads `/dev/kmsg` and reports NVIDIA XIDs | Enabled |
| `gpu-fault-fabric-manager-collector` | Reads the Fabric Manager journal/log and reports SXIDs | Enabled |
| `gpu-fault-metrics-collector` | Scrapes the DCGM Exporter and performs node-side edge filtering | Enabled |
| `gpu-fault-host-collector` | Collects CPU, memory, disk, network, RDMA/EFA, GPU/EFA inventory | Enabled |
| `gpu-fault-node-agent` | Heartbeat, diagnostics and controlled node actions | Enabled |
| `gpu-fault-gpu-persistence` | oneshot: `nvidia-smi -pm 1` turns on GPU persistence mode (`After=nvidia-persistenced.service`) | Enabled |
| `gpu-fault-certificate-check.timer` | Periodically checks the remaining validity of the control-plane CA/leaf certificates: fires the same-named `.service` 5 minutes after boot and every 12 hours thereafter, with up to 10 minutes of random delay | Enabled when a control-plane CA (`--ca-certificate`) is configured |
| `gpu-fault-dcgm-exporter` | Installer-managed DCGM Exporter container | Installed and enabled only with `--dcgm-exporter docker` |
| `gpu-fault-log-collector` | NodeLogCollector | Disabled by default |

The DCGM Exporter reuses an existing instance or is created by the deployment script depending on the deployment mode; it is not
`gpu-fault-metrics-collector` itself. The Completion Watcher and Cluster Action Executor are
per-cluster Deployments, not installed once per GPU node either. The Nvidia SMI fallback is
an optional runtime mode of `gpu-fault-metrics-collector`, not a separate systemd service, and is currently
disabled by default.

Persistent deployments must enable `gpu-fault-node-installer-reconciler`. Every 5 seconds (`GPU_FAULT_RECONCILE_SECONDS`, manifest value 5) it queries only Ready
Nodes carrying the `sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name=${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}` label; the stable `CLUSTER_ID` is used only for events, the registry and per-node key derivation and cannot replace
the provider cluster name. When a Node
lacks the current installer version/config digest/artifact SHA, it creates an
Installer Job bound to that Node.
Inside the Job the same `install-gpu-fault-collector.sh` and `/opt/gpu-fault/verify` still run.
On success the Reconciler records the version, config digest, artifact SHA,
Node UID and `Succeeded` in Node annotations; the same node is not installed twice. An artifact change must trigger a reinstall even when the config digest
is unchanged. A failed Job is kept for 5 minutes, then deleted and recreated; a node replacement also reinstalls because the Node UID changes.
The Reconciler serves `/metrics` (family `gpu_fault_node_installer_*`) and `/healthz` on `GPU_FAULT_NODE_INSTALLER_METRICS_PORT` (default 9110),
scraped by each GPU cluster's `gpu-fault-adot-dataplane`;
for variables, how to read them and prerequisites see "The scrape path for the data plane's own metrics" in the path B section.

When using `deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh deploy`, the Reconciler is deployed automatically after the first per-node installation succeeds.
In regional control-plane mode, use the existing production installer bundle and separately upload the wheel containing
the Reconciler:

```bash
export GPU_EKS_CONTEXT='arn:aws:eks:us-west-2:123456789012:cluster/<gpu-eks-cluster>'
export CLUSTER_ID='<stable-regional-cluster-id>'
export NAMESPACE=gpu-fault-system
export INSTALLER_CONFIG_MAP=gpu-fault-node-installer-0100

EXECUTOR_WHEEL="${EXECUTOR_WHEEL:-$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py executor-wheel
)}"
test -s "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}"
```
