```bash
WHEEL_SHA="$(sha256sum "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}" | cut -d' ' -f1)"
RECONCILER_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP="gpu-fault-executor-wheel-0100-${WHEEL_SHA:0:12}"

if ! kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get configmap "${RECONCILER_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    create configmap "${RECONCILER_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
    --from-file="${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}"
fi

INSTALLER_DIGEST="$(
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get configmap "${INSTALLER_CONFIG_MAP}" -o json |
  jq -r '.binaryData | to_entries[0].value' |
  base64 -d | sha256sum | cut -d' ' -f1
)"
INSTALLER_ARTIFACT_SHA="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get configmap gpu-fault-release-metadata \
    -o jsonpath='{.data.required-agent-artifact-sha256}'
)"

FLEET_MASTER_FILE="$(mktemp)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.node-action-secret}' |
base64 -d > "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"
chmod 0600 "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"

GPU_FAULT_KUBECTL_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_ID="${CLUSTER_ID}" \
GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER="${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_PROFILE="${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" \
GPU_FAULT_FLEET_MASTER_FILE="${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_CONFIG_MAP="${INSTALLER_CONFIG_MAP}" \
GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_CONFIG_DIGEST="${INSTALLER_DIGEST}" \
GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${INSTALLER_ARTIFACT_SHA}" \
GPU_FAULT_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP="${RECONCILER_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
GPU_FAULT_EXECUTOR_WHEEL_FILENAME="$(basename "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}")" \
  deploy/node/deploy-node-installer-reconciler.sh
shred -u "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"
```

The Reconciler wheel and the production installer bundle must be kept separate: the former only runs the Kubernetes
controller, the latter determines the Agent/collector artifact SHA installed on the host. Do not replace the production installer bundle in order to upgrade
the Reconciler, or you may trigger the CPU control plane's Agent artifact
compatibility refusal.

If the goal is indeed to upgrade the host collector/Agent, use the Node Runtime wheel and production bundle generated by the same
artifact-check of §4, and update the CPU control plane's compatibility policy in step. The builder itself is
deterministic, but even here you must not repackage outside the release manifest. The following commands do not overwrite old content-addressed
ConfigMaps:

```bash
NODE_WHEEL="$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py node-runtime-wheel
)"
test -s "${NODE_WHEEL}"
NODE_WHEEL_SHA="$(sha256sum "${NODE_WHEEL}" | cut -d' ' -f1)"
NODE_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="$(
  jq -er '.components.node_runtime.module_digest' dist/current-release.json
)"

NODE_BUNDLE="$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py bundle
)"
NODE_BUNDLE_SHA="$(sha256sum "${NODE_BUNDLE}" | cut -d' ' -f1)"
NODE_BUNDLE_CONFIG_MAP="gpu-fault-node-installer-0100-${NODE_BUNDLE_SHA:0:12}"

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create configmap "${NODE_BUNDLE_CONFIG_MAP}" \
  --from-file=gpu-fault-node-installer-0.10.0.tar.gz="${NODE_BUNDLE}"

FLEET_MASTER_FILE="$(mktemp)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.node-action-secret}' |
base64 -d > "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"
chmod 0600 "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"

GPU_FAULT_KUBECTL_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_ID="${CLUSTER_ID}" \
GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER="${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_PROFILE="${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" \
GPU_FAULT_FLEET_MASTER_FILE="${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_CONFIG_MAP="${NODE_BUNDLE_CONFIG_MAP}" \
GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_CONFIG_DIGEST="${NODE_BUNDLE_SHA}" \
GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${NODE_WHEEL_SHA}" \
GPU_FAULT_NODE_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="${NODE_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}" \
GPU_FAULT_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP="${RECONCILER_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
GPU_FAULT_EXECUTOR_WHEEL_FILENAME="$(basename "${EXECUTOR_WHEEL}")" \
  deploy/node/deploy-node-installer-reconciler.sh
shred -u "${FLEET_MASTER_FILE}"
```

Use `kubectl create` for the large bundle ConfigMap; do not create it via
`kubectl create --dry-run -o yaml | kubectl apply -f -`; the
`last-applied-configuration` annotation the latter adds may exceed the Kubernetes 256 KiB limit.

Regional authentication uses the token digest (`token_sha256`) and membership state of the Aurora persistent registry as the source of truth; the CPU
`gpu-fault-regional-clusters` Secret keeps the bootstrap seed and the raw credentials needed for disaster recovery. The following is only
a reviewed break-glass sync; formal rotation uses REG-9's `rotate-token`, and you must not bypass the
revision by changing only the Secret. When syncing to the GPU Secret use
`jq -jr`: `-r` outputs the raw token, `-j` suppresses the trailing newline. Plain
`jq -r`, which appends a newline, cannot be used:

```bash
TOKEN_B64="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get secret gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
    -o jsonpath='{.data.clusters\.json}' |
  base64 -d |
  jq -jr --arg cluster "${CLUSTER_ID}" \
    '.[] | select(.cluster_id==$cluster) | .token' |
  base64 -w0
)"
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  patch secret gpu-fault-regional-connection --type=merge \
  -p "{\"data\":{\"cluster-token\":\"${TOKEN_B64}\"}}"
unset TOKEN_B64
```

The Agent pin cannot switch atomically from the old value straight to the new one: the control plane and all nodes cannot possibly finish
rolling at the same instant. The standard `deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh` first pauses the processor worker, writes the old
artifact/config/protocol into the three `compatible-*` fields, then starts the new control plane and installs the
Agents; only after the heartbeats of all current nodes have converged exactly to the new
`artifact_sha256`, `config_digest` and protocol does the script clear the compatibility fields, roll
ingress and resume the worker.

When using `apply-control-plane-role-split.sh` by hand you must also perform the same two-phase flow. The first
run automatically adds the existing required pin to the compatibility window; after all Agents have converged, run it again with
`GPU_FAULT_FINALIZE_AGENT_PIN=true` to clear the window. Do not switch to the strict pin before installing the
Agents, and do not clear the compatibility fields while the nodes have not fully converged.

Neither phase restarts control-plane Pods for the pin. The Deployment's pin environment variables reference
`gpu-fault-release-metadata` via `configMapKeyRef`, and the control-plane process (`gpu_fault.fleet_pins.FleetPinRuntime`)
additionally hot-reloads the pins by polling the same ConfigMap through the Kubernetes API; the read-only permission is granted to
ServiceAccount `gpu-fault-control-plane` by
`deploy/control-plane/regional/control-plane-release-metadata-rbac.yaml`, applied idempotently after bootstrap and before every control-plane apply.
The script records the digest of `.data` (`jq -cjS`, byte-for-byte identical to the runtime algorithm) in the Deployment's
`gpu-fault.io/pin-config-sha256` metadata annotation, then
`deploy/control-plane/tools/wait_control_plane_pins.py` waits until every Pod of the selected roles reports the same digest in
`fleet_pins.content_sha256` of `/healthz` (timeout
`GPU_FAULT_FLEET_PIN_CONVERGENCE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`, default 180 seconds, values below 30 seconds refused); a timeout means
the apply fails and the transaction rolls back as usual. A verbatim rollback (with `GPU_FAULT_ROLE_SPLIT_CONTAINER_ENV_FILE` set) forces
a restart of all roles, and the Pods start directly from the ConfigMap snapshot, so that wait is skipped. The `fleet_pins` field of `/v1/version` and
`/healthz` gives the source, digest, generation and most recent
read error of the pin snapshot the process currently serves; pin read failures are only recorded there and do not affect the readiness decision.

The workflow executor and the GPU Cluster Executor also check the Fleet readiness of the NodeAction targets later in the workflow
before the first destructive step. On pin drift the workflow stays retryable, and
`MARK_UNSCHEDULABLE`, `QUARANTINE` or workload mutation must not be executed; this gate is the last protection against release
mistakes and does not replace the two-phase release.

The Cluster Executor's claim/readiness must also carry
`executor_protocol_version`. The control plane accepts the previous version only within an explicit compatibility window; once the window closes,
an old executor receives 503 before the remote command lease and therefore does not claim a new command
it cannot parse. The control plane must first complete its rollout with "old + new protocol", then roll the GPU Cluster Executor,
and finally clear `compatible-regional-executor-protocol-versions`. The Completion Watcher and
collectors only use the versioned telemetry/observation write interfaces and do not claim fault mutation commands; within the same
protocol, optional fields may be added, while removing/re-defining fields requires a new API version and must not depend on rollout order.

Verify that all current nodes have converged:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status deployment/gpu-fault-node-installer-reconciler
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs deployment/gpu-fault-node-installer-reconciler --tail=100
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" get nodes \
  -l sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name="${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
  -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,VERSION:.metadata.annotations.gpu-fault\.io/installer-version,STATE:.metadata.annotations.gpu-fault\.io/installer-state,DIGEST:.metadata.annotations.gpu-fault\.io/installer-config-digest'
```
#### REG-7. Verify from the GPU Data Plane

```bash
EXECUTOR_POD="$(
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pod -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${EXECUTOR_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import json,os,ssl,urllib.request; context=ssl.create_default_context(cafile=os.environ["GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_CA_FILE"]); print(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(os.environ["GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_URL"] + "/healthz", context=context, timeout=10)))'

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor --since=5m |
  grep -E 'ERROR|Traceback|401|403|CERTIFICATE_VERIFY_FAILED' &&
  exit 1 || true
```

You must also confirm on the CPU control plane:

- Every GPU node has `NVIDIA_KERNEL`, `GPU_METRICS` and `HOST_TELEMETRY` status;
- Every Node Agent is `ACTIVE`, with an artifact SHA matching the release value;
- The Fabric Manager collector service is active; do not fabricate events as acceptance when there is no real SXID;
- The executor claim API keeps returning 200.

Query commands:

```bash
CPU_API_POD="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pod -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import json,os,sys,urllib.parse,urllib.request; base="http://127.0.0.1:8080"; cluster=urllib.parse.quote(sys.argv[1], safe=""); request=urllib.request.Request(base + "/v1/collector-status/" + cluster, headers={"X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token":os.environ["GPU_FAULT_EXECUTION_TOKEN"]}); print(json.dumps(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=15)), indent=2))' \
  "${CLUSTER_ID}"

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import json,os,sys,urllib.parse,urllib.request; base="http://127.0.0.1:8080"; cluster=urllib.parse.quote(sys.argv[1], safe=""); request=urllib.request.Request(base + "/v1/fleet/agents?cluster_id=" + cluster, headers={"X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token":os.environ["GPU_FAULT_EXECUTION_TOKEN"]}); print(json.dumps(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=15)), indent=2))' \
  "${CLUSTER_ID}"
```

After verification delete the locally rendered file:

```bash
rm -f /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml
```

#### REG-8. Regional Deployment Acceptance Checklist

This checklist applies only to the regionally separated deployment and is not interchangeable with the Appendix A.6 single-cluster checklist: the single-cluster checklist uses
`http://<control-plane>` directly on 8080, whereas the regional control plane is exposed only through the TLS NLB on 443, and
`/v1/fleet/agents` must carry the cluster scope in regional mode (a call without `cluster_id` is blocked with 403 by the
default-deny middleware, or returns only the caller's own cluster data).

First prepare the acceptance variables. All operator-side calls need the execution token:

```bash
export EXECUTION_TOKEN="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
    -o jsonpath='{.data.execution-token}' | base64 -d
)"
```

| # | Check item | Command | Pass criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The NLB is TLS and does not accept plaintext | The GPU data-plane TLS probe below | TLS returns `200`, the plaintext connection fails |
| 2 | The certificate chain is valid, without `-k` | The GPU data-plane TLS probe below | Succeeds with the private CA; certificate verification fails when no CA is supplied |
| 3 | Unauthenticated requests are denied by default | The GPU data-plane TLS probe below | `403` |
| 4 | Registry entries are complete and do not leak tokens | The code block below | One entry per registered cluster, `token_sha256_present=true`, `token_sha256_length=64`, and the response contains no `token_sha256` or `retiring_token_sha256` field; outside a rotation `retiring_token_sha256_present=false` |
| 5 | All three replicas Ready | `kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" get deployment/gpu-fault-api-ha` | `3/3`, with no restart in the last 10 minutes |
| 6 | Per-cluster Agent readiness (scoped) | The code block below | All nodes of the target cluster `ready=true` |
| 7 | Executor **functionally** ready | The code block below | `claimed_total` increases monotonically or `last_successful_claim_at` is within 2 poll periods; Pod Ready by itself does not count |
| 8 | No command stuck unclaimed | The code block below | `oldest_unclaimed_age_seconds` is `null` or < 600 |
| 9 | No new executor internal errors | `gpu_fault_remote_command_executor_internal_errors_total`, `gpu_fault_remote_command_executor_internal_error_last_seen_timestamp_seconds` | The former is the number of currently retained records and decreases with cleanup; the latter must not advance after this release, and a new value within the last 900 seconds triggers a critical alert |
| 10 | The AMP/SNS alerting chain is loaded | The code block below | Live AMP contains the five required groups, Alertmanager has a valid SNS receiver, and `UNREACHABLE_ALERT_DEFECTS= 0` |
| 11 | The executor's HyperPod second confirmation comes from its own configuration | The code block below | With the HyperPod adapter enabled, `GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER` is non-empty and equals the `hyperpod_cluster_name` of the same `cluster_id` in the registry; when disabled, the variable being empty suffices |

Items 1-3 must be run from a source allowed by the NLB Security Group. If the front-end SG only allows the GPU cluster's
NAT EIPs, a timeout from the management host connecting directly is the correct result and must not be taken as an NLB failure. Verify using the already deployed Executor
Pod:

```bash
EXECUTOR_POD="$(
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pod -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec -i "${EXECUTOR_POD}" -- python - <<'PY'
import os
import ssl
import urllib.error
import urllib.request

base = os.environ["GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_URL"].rstrip("/")
context = ssl.create_default_context(
    cafile=os.environ["GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_CA_FILE"]
)
with urllib.request.urlopen(
    base + "/healthz", context=context, timeout=10
) as response:
    assert response.status == 200
    print("tls_health_http_code=", response.status)

try:
    urllib.request.urlopen(base + "/healthz", timeout=10)
except Exception as error:
    print("without_private_ca=failed_as_expected", type(error).__name__)
else:
    raise SystemExit("TLS unexpectedly succeeded without private CA")

plain = "http://" + base.removeprefix("https://")
try:
    urllib.request.urlopen(plain + "/healthz", timeout=5)
except Exception as error:
    print("plaintext=failed_as_expected", type(error).__name__)
else:
    raise SystemExit("plaintext unexpectedly succeeded")

try:
    urllib.request.urlopen(
        base + "/v1/regional/clusters",
        context=context,
        timeout=10,
    )
except urllib.error.HTTPError as error:
    assert error.code == 403
    print("unauthenticated_registry_http_code=", error.code)
else:
    raise SystemExit("unauthenticated registry unexpectedly succeeded")
PY
```

Only when the management host's address has also been explicitly added to the NLB SG may you run the equivalent check with local `curl --cacert` instead.

Commands for items 4, 6, 8 and 9 (all go over loopback from inside the CPU API Pod, to keep the execution token
out of the NLB access logs):

```bash
CPU_API_POD="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pod -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"

# 4. registry entries
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- env TOKEN="${EXECUTION_TOKEN}" python -c \
  'import json,os,urllib.request; r=urllib.request.Request("http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/regional/clusters", headers={"X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token": os.environ["TOKEN"]}); print(json.dumps(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(r, timeout=10)), indent=2))'

# 6. cluster-scoped readiness
NODE_IDS_JSON="$(
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" get nodes \
    -l "sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name=${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" -o json |
  jq -c '[.items[].metadata.name] | sort'
)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- env \
  TOKEN="${EXECUTION_TOKEN}" \
  CLUSTER_ID="${CLUSTER_ID}" \
  NODE_IDS_JSON="${NODE_IDS_JSON}" \
  python -c \
  'import json,os,urllib.request; body=json.dumps({"cluster_id":os.environ["CLUSTER_ID"],"node_ids":json.loads(os.environ["NODE_IDS_JSON"])}).encode(); r=urllib.request.Request("http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/fleet/readiness", data=body, method="POST", headers={"Content-Type":"application/json","X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token":os.environ["TOKEN"]}); print(json.dumps(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(r, timeout=15)), indent=2))'

# 8, 9. remote command metrics
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import urllib.request; print("".join(l for l in urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8080/metrics", timeout=10).read().decode().splitlines(True) if "remote_command" in l))'
```

Item 10 must check the
`ADOT -> AMP -> Alertmanager -> SNS` actually used in regional production, not the
`PrometheusRule` that is never loaded here:

```bash
: "${AWS_REGION:?the Region where AMP lives must be set}"
: "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID:?the AMP workspace ID must be set}"
: "${AMP_RULE_NAMESPACE:=gpu-fault-control-plane-capacity}"

ALERT_VERIFY_DIR="$(mktemp -d)"
aws amp describe-rule-groups-namespace \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --workspace-id "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID}" \
  --name "${AMP_RULE_NAMESPACE}" \
  --query 'ruleGroupsNamespace.data' --output text |
  base64 -d > "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/amp-rules.yaml"
aws amp describe-alert-manager-definition \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --workspace-id "${AMP_WORKSPACE_ID}" \
  --query 'alertManagerDefinition.data' --output text |
  base64 -d > "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/alertmanager.yaml"

python3.12 scripts/verify-regional-alerting.py \
  --live-rules "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/amp-rules.yaml" \
  --live-alertmanager "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}/alertmanager.yaml"
rm -rf "${ALERT_VERIFY_DIR}"
```

Item 7, executor functional readiness. Pod Ready only proves it can reach `/healthz`, not that claims succeed
(a token mismatch or unadvertised owners both leave it idling indefinitely):

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor --since=3m |
  grep -cE 'claim|claimed' || true

# Take two consecutive readings; the interval should exceed GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_POLL_SECONDS
for i in 1 2; do
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    logs -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor --tail=1
  sleep 10
done
```

You must also confirm that the owners advertised by the executor cover all OWN/DELEGATE owners in the runtime profile,
otherwise the remote commands of the corresponding steps stay `PENDING` forever with no error log at all:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  -o jsonpath='{range .spec.template.spec.containers[0].env[*]}{.name}={.value}{"\n"}{end}' |
  grep -E 'ENABLE_(NODE_ACTION|HYPERPOD)_ADAPTER'
```

`gpu-fault-hyperpod-adapter` is advertised only when `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER=true`; the default manifest has `false`;
before using
`config/runtime-profile.regional-hyperpod-safe.example.yaml` (where `nodeReboot` and
`nodeReplace` are OWN), the adapter must first be enabled per chapter 10 "Phase D". `nodeReplace`
in that profile performs a `HEALTHY_WARM_SPARE_ONLY` local switch and must still keep
`GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REPLACE=false`; it must not call the provider replace API.

Item 11. The second confirmation of HyperPod changes is provided by the executor's own environment; the
`cluster_id` carried in the command takes no part in the confirmation (otherwise the command would confirm itself and the gate would effectively not exist). The two values must match, otherwise
every reboot/replace step FAILs at the adapter's confirmation gate:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  -o jsonpath='{range .spec.template.spec.containers[0].env[*]}{.name}{"\n"}{end}' |
  grep -x GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-regional-connection \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.hyperpod-cluster-name}' | base64 -d; echo

# Compare with the control-plane registry (in the output of item 4 the
# hyperpod_cluster_name of the same cluster_id must be exactly identical)
```

#### REG-8.1 Unified Regional Lifecycle Orchestrator

A regionally separated deployment must no longer be assembled by administrators hand-stitching two sets of CPU/GPU `kubectl` commands. The unified entry point is:

```bash
deploy/control-plane/regional/rollout-regional-release.sh
```

It uses one JSON configuration describing the operator-chosen `aws_region`, CPU kubeconfig, CPU EKS ARN,
CPU HyperPod name, current release, regionally shared Runtime Profile and the current set of GPU clusters.
`runtime_profile` must explicitly provide source, version and a stable
`registration_cluster_id`; that anchor does not change as GPU clusters join or are decommissioned. Every GPU entry must explicitly contain the same Region, EKS ARN and
HyperPod name; there is no `us-west-2` or other Region default.
That `version` is the single version input for the CPU fleet pin, the Kubernetes Resource Collector,
the node Installer Jobs generated by the Reconciler and the training workload annotation;
you must not modify only one of these objects.
Template:

```text
deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-release.example.json
```

The configuration file, Runtime Profile source, cluster token, CA and fleet master must all live in controlled
directories with appropriate permissions; files containing deployment identity and credentials use `0600`. The script never writes tokens or the master
into release state or ordinary logs.

##### REG-8.1.1 Management Boundary

This sub-section describes the low-level `rollout-regional-release.sh`: `bootstrap` forces the first regional application
deployment, and `deploy` automatically chooses bootstrap or upgrade according to the release state. The low-level rollout
does not create AWS infrastructure, so before the first run the following must already exist:

- The CPU EKS and every GPU EKS;
- `gpu-fault-aurora`,
  `gpu-fault-control-plane-active` and `gpu-fault-node-action-keys` in the CPU namespace;
- A usable Aurora/PostgreSQL URL, ACM certificate, NLB subnets/SG;
- Each GPU cluster's EKS context, HyperPod name, executor IRSA role, and the IRSA role of the data-plane ADOT collector
  (`clusters[].adot_irsa_role_arn`; when missing, that cluster skips the collector, see the path B section);
- A separate token per cluster, and the control-plane private CA.

The script is responsible for the Kubernetes prerequisites owned by this solution, the registry, schema Jobs, NLB Service,
CPU role-split, Runtime Profile registration, GPU executor/watcher/collector/reconciler and
node installation.

The normal first administrator entry point is `gpu-fault-admin deploy --cpu-cluster-arn ...` with the GPU ARNs supplied repeatedly;
that command builds the signed release, creates or reuses AWS resources, generates the site and deploys. An existing site only needs
`gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir <state-dir>`: the CPU/GPU ARNs and administrator email are read from the site.

##### REG-8.1.2 Read-Only Checks

```bash
CONFIG=/secure/gpu-fault-regional-release.json

deploy/control-plane/regional/rollout-regional-release.sh \
  plan --plan-mode deploy --config "${CONFIG}"

deploy/control-plane/regional/rollout-regional-release.sh \
  status --config "${CONFIG}"
```

`status` reports at once the configured Runtime Profile source/version/SHA, the CPU release metadata
with the three component digests, the executor/reconciler wheels of all GPU clusters, and the classification of the next deploy,
without modifying resources. If the online release has completed and rollback has been verified, but the management site still points at the failed candidate,
the high-level `gpu-fault-admin status` constructs a temporary read-only baseline from the previous immutable Manifest, Profile and capacity configuration;
it neither modifies the site nor misreports the healthy rolled-back online version as candidate drift.

##### REG-8.1.3 First Deployment

```bash
deploy/control-plane/regional/rollout-regional-release.sh \
  bootstrap --config "${CONFIG}"
```

The execution order of `bootstrap` is fixed:

1. Validate the actual EKS ARN, Region and HyperPod Orchestrator of the CPU/GPU kube contexts,
   and confirm every GPU HyperPod is `NodeRecovery=None`;
2. Apply the CPU prerequisites and write a non-empty regional registry containing only the first baseline GPU cluster;
3. Upload the content-addressed wheels/bundle and run the schema ensure Job;
4. Deploy the CPU ingress/worker, and register or check the declared regional Runtime Profile;
5. Deploy and verify the NLB/TLS endpoint;
6. Once the endpoint is ready, create the connection Secret for the first baseline GPU cluster and deploy
   the Executor, Watcher, Resource Collector and Reconciler;
7. Wait for the baseline cluster's node annotations to converge to the target artifact and write a checkpoint; a process interruption can be resumed,
   and a controlled failure still runs the first-deployment cleanup below; the remaining GPU ARNs enter a separate batch join only after the baseline verify;
8. Run the CPU role-split and GPU executor verifiers.

All GPU-specific mutations in steps 2 to 7 target only the first baseline cluster, including the kubeconfig context,
namespace, token, Executor IAM, Node Action key, NLB NAT EIP and Private Hosted Zone
VPC association. Clusters waiting to join must not receive network permissions in advance. VPC associations are deduplicated by `Region + VPC ID`;
when several GPU clusters share a VPC it is associated only once.

A first deployment has no "previous stable version". On failure it automatically fails closed: this solution's CPU/GPU Deployments
are scaled to 0, while EKS/VPC/Aurora, Secrets and the content-addressed artefacts are kept for inspection or `resume`; shared
infrastructure is never deleted by mistake.

##### REG-8.1.4 Upgrade, Resume and Rollback

All three are the same administrator command; the low-level orchestrator is no longer called directly:

```bash
# Upgrade: release the candidate pointed to by dist/current-release.json to the site
gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault-bootstrap

# Resume: after a transaction interruption (process killed, cluster temporarily unreachable, PAUSED) simply rerun as is;
# deploy reads the phase of the release state and decides on its own whether to resume, finish the commit cleanup or continue rolling back
gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault-bootstrap

# Rollback: return to the previous committed release
gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault-bootstrap --rollback
```

The semantics of `--rollback` are a single step: the target is always `previous` in the release state (the release that was running online
before the current release was committed); there is no release id parameter and no confirmation literal is required.
The command first reads the online release state and refuses outright in the following cases, explaining the reason and not touching the site:

- The state has no `previous` (right after the first bootstrap, or a NOOP release): there is no target to fall back to;
- A transaction is still in progress (any intermediate upgrade/rollback phase, `complete` but not committed, or the commit
  cleanup unfinished): first rerun `deploy --state-dir X` to let it resume/finish, then roll back. A `complete` but
  uncommitted transaction (verify/stability failed after deployment and no rollback) does not have to be finished by the same candidate: when you deploy
  a new candidate directly, deploy first commits the online transaction as the baseline under its own identity, then opens a new transaction;
- There is a PostgreSQL schema change between the previous release and the current release: the engine refuses cross-schema
  rollback anyway, and the command reports the engine's own words (including the snapshot id recorded by `--accept-schema-change`).

`deploy --rollback` also passes through the in-flight reinstall gate (explained after REG-8.1.4 below): the engine's `rollback` mode reads the control-plane
store once before opening the transaction, and refuses with exit code 3 when a reinstall step is in flight, the store has no Pod to answer, or there is a kubectl-layer fault; to override, add
`--allow-inflight-installs` on the **same command** (`gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir X --rollback --allow-inflight-installs`); the command overlays it as
`GPU_FAULT_RELEASE_ALLOW_INFLIGHT_INSTALLS=1` onto the environment of the engine rollback and the subsequent `sync-state`, the same variable as the upgrade path,
with no manual `export` needed any more. `--rollback` recognises only this one consent: when `--accept-schema-change` /
`--accept-schema-change-without-snapshot` / `--supersede-failed-transaction` appear together with `--rollback`, the command immediately refuses with
`deploy --rollback takes no other deploy option` without touching the site -- cross-schema rollback is refused by the engine according to the acceptance **recorded** in the release state,
acceptance on the command line has no effect on rollback, and the rollback of a committed release has no failed transaction to supersede; a leftover
`export GPU_FAULT_RELEASE_ACCEPT_SCHEMA_CHANGE=…` in the shell does not affect the `--rollback` decision (the engine's rollback mode does not read it).

If the previous `deploy` aborted after the Aurora refresher prerequisite repair was already `READY` but before the application transaction started (for example the preflight's
workflow gate refused), the release state retains an `aurora_prerequisite_repair`
journal bound to that candidate, while the online refresher CronJob already runs that candidate's image. No manual cleanup is needed: the next `deploy` (any candidate)
first restores the original refresher in the preflight and then takes over the journal; to only undo the repair without deploying, run
`deploy --rollback` on a committed, quiescent site; it restores the original refresher, deletes the journal and stops with
`Aurora prerequisite repair restored; rerun rollback to roll back the application`,
**without** rolling back the application (do not run a second `--rollback`; that one would roll back the application).

After a rollback completes, the state enters `rolled-back`, `previous` is the version now running online, and the state no longer
records earlier committed releases, so **a second consecutive `--rollback` is refused**. To go back to an earlier
version, deploy that old commit through the ordinary release flow (point `dist/current-release.json` at the old
release and then `deploy`), not by rolling back again.

The Secret backups used for rollback (the `*-rollback-<release_id>` copies of the CPU `gpu-fault-email` and of each GPU cluster's `gpu-fault-regional-connection`)
are kept at release commit and cleaned up only when the **next** transaction commits;
so the rollback of a committed release can find its own backups, and when they are missing it still fails closed with
`release Secret backup is missing`.

After the engine finishes, the command also aligns the management side just like an automatic rollback: it changes `site.yaml` back to the previous release's
manifest/profile/images and runs `sync-state`, so that `gpu-fault-admin status` judges health by the rolled-back online version.
If the engine rollback succeeds but the alignment fails, the command returns 1 and reports
`ALIGNMENT_FAILED` with the reason in the JSON result; if the engine fails, the engine's exit code is returned.

Before an upgrade, remote commands must satisfy `PENDING/LEASED/WAITING=0`, **and** no
`REMEDIATE_DRIVER` / `UPDATE_SOFTWARE_FIRMWARE` / `REMEDIATE_EFA_DRIVER` step may be in flight anywhere in the Region. "In flight" is decided by one read of the control-plane store by the probe
`inflight_installs`: it scans only workflows in the **executable** states `PENDING` / `SAFETY_PENDING` / `RUNNING`
(BLOCKED is not scanned, on the grounds that **a dispatched BLOCKED row never executes again**: the dispatcher's internal error funnel does indeed turn a RUNNING workflow, together with its
WAITING reinstall execution, into BLOCKED, but the executor returns the existing result for BLOCKED directly, and the operations lever only closes it to SUPERSEDED and refuses rows with execution
records; only two writers turn BLOCKED back into executable -- the aggregation merge `families/node_lifecycle._state` (touching only rows whose `not_before` is still in
the future, with no executor, i.e. never claimed by the dispatcher) and REPLACE_IN_PLACE under the `workflow_merge.never_executed_operator_block` decision
(touching only BLOCKED(NEEDS_OPERATOR) rows with no execution record and no owner, and switching to the candidate's new id),
pinned in both directions by `tests/orchestration/test_replacement_merge_reopens_only_undispatched.py`); within them every unfinished, non-superseded reinstall step is recorded as `PENDING` when it has no execution record yet and `WAITING` when its latest execution is WAITING
(a latest SUCCEEDED/FAILED does not count). Therefore reinstall steps not yet started at **any depth** of the plan, as well as plans still in SAFETY_PENDING,
block every deploy / rollback in the Region for the whole lifetime of that step (until it completes, fails or is superseded). The scan is bounded at 1000 rows;
with more than 1000 executable workflows it refuses with `could not bound the scan` rather than treating 0 as clean. The rationale is that R4's stable command id
is one-way: a control plane of another shape (the previous version a rollback lands on, or the old version replaced mid-upgrade) would submit the installation still running on the node a second time.

The gate is open on both `upgrade` (new transaction, resume and supersede all enter here) and `rollback`, positioned **after** the Aurora credential refresh
(a rotation-window failure is reported first); the re-entrant cleanup already checkpointed at `rollback-cpu-restored` skips it (the previous control plane is already the dispatcher). The read is attempted in turn on
**every** Running Pod of each control-plane role (a stuck rollout leaves the old ReplicaSet's Pods coexisting with CrashLooping new Pods,
and the old Pods are the dispatcher), CPU ingress role first; the probe source is fed via stdin, and the `+` echo in the log is fixed as
`kubectl exec -i <pod> -- sh -c 'python - 2>&1; echo "__GPU_FAULT_PROBE_EXIT=$?"'`; the trailing `__GPU_FAULT_PROBE_EXIT=<code>`
is proof the probe ran, and the probe's own failures are reported as `{"probe_error": "<Type>: <msg>"}`; a single read times out at 120 s. When a reinstall step is in flight it refuses and
names the workflow id, operation, step index, node and step status, advising to wait for it to finish (one driver/firmware installation takes at most 30 minutes). Gate failures fall into two classes:
**the probe could not run** (`StoreUnreachable`: no Running control-plane Pod at all, or every attempted Pod has no ready container and exec fails at the
kubectl layer -- a CrashLooping control plane has this shape) and **evidence defects** (non-zero exit after the probe started -- not trusted even if it printed valid JSON at the same time --
no JSON output, self-reported error, no return within 120 s, wrong shape, scan exceeding the 1000-row bound). There is also a class of **kubectl-layer faults**: exec fails at the kubectl layer on a Pod that has a
ready container (kubelet down, deployment host lacking `pods/exec` RBAC, image without `sh`), or
`get pod` itself fails (API server unreachable, kubeconfig expired) -- that is "a live dispatcher that cannot be asked" rather than "no dispatcher"; all modes
refuse, the wording names kubectl/API server/kubeconfig rather than the store, and the consent variable can override it (this is a verifiable transport/permission fault); on override it
records `inflight-installs-unchecked` with the reason. Evidence defects are refused
at every entry point and in every mode, and even the consent variable cannot override them -- they mean the dispatcher is blocked or the probe is broken; first drain the queue, adjust the window or fix the probe. When the probe could not
run, the **manual** entry points refuse likewise, and the override lever differs by entry point -- `gpu-fault-admin deploy --allow-inflight-installs` (just a flag of `deploy`;
`status` does not accept it; passed to the engine as `GPU_FAULT_RELEASE_ALLOW_INFLIGHT_INSTALLS=1`; like the other two consents, **the flag
overrides a leftover export** -- without the flag a residual variable is passed through unchanged but does not count as this run's consent), `deploy --rollback --allow-inflight-installs`
hands the same variable to the engine's rollback mode, and `rollout-regional-release.sh rollback` has no flag and sets the same variable directly in the environment. Consent covers only three situations: the explicitly listed in-flight reinstalls (stderr records
`inflight-installs-overridden steps=…`), the probe not running (records `inflight-installs-unchecked`) and kubectl-layer faults
(`KubectlFailure`, records `inflight-installs-unchecked reason=exec failed on a ready Pod, consent given` or
`reason=kubectl could not list the control-plane Pods, consent given`), then continues. **Automatic**
rollback (the engine-internal upgrade-failure rollback, and the engine rollback invoked by the driver with `--automatic` after a verification failure; that flag is engine-internal,
absent from `--help`, and rejected in non-`rollback` modes) has one additional rule: only when the probe could not run does it continue with
`inflight-installs-unchecked action=rollback reason=…` -- recovery of the control plane must not be abandoned just because the control plane is down -- while a reinstall really in flight
or defective evidence (including a probe that started in a Running Pod and then failed) is refused all the same.

The gate's decision is persistent: before `rollback-started` (and the first checkpoint on the upgrade side) the engine writes
`inflight_installs: {checked, verdict: clear|unchecked|overridden, reason, steps, inflight_count, scanned, checked_at, error?}`
into the transaction state; the driver's `rollback` record also carries `inflight_installs` -- PASSED/FAILED copied from the engine state, a refusal recorded as
`verdict: refused` (no steps; the workflows and nodes are in the rollback log), and `unrecorded` meaning the engine predates the field. Re-entry after `rollback-cpu-restored`
skips the gate, and the state keeps the first round's decision; tell them apart by `checked_at`.

When an automatic rollback is refused, the engine logs `automatic-rollback-refused phase=failed reason=…`, and the transaction stops at `failed`
(**not** `rollback-failed`, with no `rollback_failure` field -- nothing was restored); the error string chains the original upgrade error and offers two ways out:
rerun the same `gpu-fault-admin deploy` after the installation finishes, i.e. resume, or run the engine rollback with the consent variable above; `--supersede-failed-transaction`
applies equally to this `failed`. The engine signals this kind of refusal with exit code **3** (other `ReleaseError`s are 2), and the driver accordingly records the rollback as
`status: REFUSED_INFLIGHT_INSTALLS` (with reason and the original error) and re-raises the original verification error, rather than "the rollback also failed". The four change kinds remain
summaries; the real actions come from the component DAG:

| Classification | Execution scope |
|---|---|
| `NOOP` | No upload, no migration, no rollout; only the read-only verifier runs |
| `CONTROL_PLANE_ONLY` | Uploads only the Control Plane wheel and rolls the CPU roles |
| `DATA_PLANE_COMPATIBLE` | Uploads only the changed Executor/Node artefacts and rolls the affected data plane |
| `FULL` | Cross-plane contract change; still executes only the dependent nodes in the DAG |

Runtime Profile classification uses the canonical policy digest after removing `cluster_id/profile_version` and sorting
`claims/observed`. Switching the template to an immutable snapshot or reordering YAML fields is not a
policy change; the raw source/template SHA serves only as audit evidence in the release state.
A real policy change first has the high-level deploy generate a plan awaiting approval; `site_identity` in the plan shows the site name,
Region and CPU EKS ARN directly, and `site_identity_sha256` checks that readable identity. The approval of `deploy --approve-profile-plan` is additionally bound to
the registration anchor, the source/target versions, the policy digest, the template/snapshot SHA and the live source SHA at approval time, and forcibly compares
the reviewed `plan_sha256` passed by the administrator. The low-level
engine must not create or replace that approval record on its own.

When a compatibility window is needed, the old stable Agent/Executor stays required and the candidate joins compatible. A shared release
upgrades one GPU cluster at a time in site order by default; only `upgradeMaxParallelClusters` (default 1, maximum 8)
turns cross-cluster progression into bounded parallelism, while automatic rollback stays serial; a single cluster runs only the changed Endpoint, DCGM, Executor,
Watcher, Collector, Reconciler or Agent nodes. `upgradeMaxUnavailable` defaults to 0 (auto, taking the scale-based cap) with a maximum of 32,
and the first upgrade wave is fixed at 1; an UNKNOWN topology forces 1, known failure domains adapt by node count to 4, 8, 16 or 32 and are
distributed evenly, and an explicit 1..32 takes the smaller of the configured value and the cap. `rollbackMaxUnavailable=2` with a maximum of 4, and the first rollback wave is fixed at 1; fewer than 6 nodes or
UNKNOWN gives 1, 6 to 31 nodes at most 2, at least 32 nodes are required for 4, and with multiple failure domains at most 1 per domain per wave.

After the CPU candidate phase completes, the orchestrator waits at most 75 seconds for Agents to refresh their heartbeats against the candidate control plane. Each Fleet
wave waits at most 60 seconds before claiming, requiring Agents outside the wave to retain at least a 40-second lease; after claiming it checks a 30-second margin as
the minimum lease remainder recorded by the first probe minus elapsed time, and re-probes only when the margin is insufficient or the probe reported no remainder.
It stops immediately when a remote command or destructive workflow exists; a momentarily insufficient lease is a bounded wait rather than
a false failure after a single check.

A release across schema versions is the exception: the new wheel requires an exactly equal schema version, and the old wheel produced by rollback refuses to
start on the new database, so both automatic rollback and explicit `rollback` are refused. Such a release adds
`--accept-schema-change` to the same deploy: the engine first snapshots Aurora (`gpu-fault-pre-v<version>-<release id>`),
then runs the schema Job; this one transaction is handled fail-forward and `site.yaml` is untouched; after a failure restore the database snapshot and then roll back,
or fix and rerun the same command. Steps are in `管理员日常运维.md` (Administrator Operations) §4.7.

The old image restored by a rollback only accepts the `GPU_FAULT_*` names it knows: the application fails fast at startup on unknown names
(the apply script attributes that log line to `unknown GPU_FAULT environment variable`), while the engine only holds
the Deployment template of the current checkout, and `env`/`envFrom` entries newly added in the new template would make the old image refuse
to start. Therefore, besides the role ConfigMap data, the `previous` snapshot also saves verbatim, per Deployment/container, the `env` and `envFrom` lists of every container of the CPU
three roles (`cpu_role_container_env`; a literal value matching a sensitive keyword
refuses capture). When rendering the rollback, the engine writes that snapshot into the transaction's temporary directory and hands it to the renderer via
`GPU_FAULT_ROLE_SPLIT_CONTAINER_ENV_FILE`; the rendered old Deployment container environment is exactly identical to the captured content --
neither names the old image does not know nor missing names it needs; an incomplete snapshot structure, or one pointing at
a Deployment/container that cannot be rendered, stops with ReleaseError before any mutation. The forward upgrade path
is unaffected and still renders from the current template. Transactions opened before this capture went live lack the key, and rollback falls back to the old behaviour
(rendering from the current template), records `current-template` in `rollback_timing.phases.cpu_restore.details.cpu_container_env`
and prints a `rollback-cpu-container-env` log line, to explain a possible later
`rollback-failed`.

The two validations on the rollback path also use that snapshot as the reference, rather than judging the old release's shape by the current release's rules:
when `GPU_FAULT_ROLE_SPLIT_CONTAINER_ENV_FILE` exists, the apply script adds
`--container-env-snapshot <file>` to `config_cli validate`, and `verify_control_plane_role_split.py` reads
the same variable directly from the environment; both enter "snapshot mode". In snapshot mode, `config_cli validate` skips
`validate_environment_inventory`, `validate_role`, `validate_shutdown`,
`validate_operation_allowlists` and `validate_connections` (the old release validated this environment with its own rules when it was deployed;
old names the current code does not know are expected in a rollback, not errors) and prints one explanatory line;
it still checks that the manifests parse, that all three role Deployments are rendered, that the snapshot and the rendered result cover the same set of
Deployments/containers, and that each container's `env` (compared after sorting by name, order-independent) and `envFrom` (order-sensitive;
the latter overrides the former) equal the snapshot item by item; the first difference exits non-zero naming the Deployment/container/variable. The verifier
skips current-contract assertions such as role, port, uvicorn process count, connection-pool arithmetic, spool limits and lazy pool configuration,
and still checks that the three role Deployments exist, that each container's live `env`/`envFrom` equal the snapshot (same normalisation
rules, provided uniformly by `gpu_fault.container_env_snapshot`), that the container image equals
`GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_IMAGE`, and that all three have `readyReplicas == replicas` with `replicas > 0`. When the snapshot file
is unreadable or structurally invalid, both fail outright. When the variable is unset (a forward upgrade, or a transaction predating the container-environment capture)
both validations behave exactly as before.

When any step fails, `auto_rollback=true` restores the previous required pins, CPU/GPU wheels,
Reconciler bundle/template, Node Agent artifact and the Runtime Profile version referenced by the CPU/Collector/Installer.
Already registered profile versions are kept as immutable rollback targets and are not deleted.
Before the CPU rollout it also saves the old registry as
`previous-clusters.json` inside the `gpu-fault-regional-clusters` Secret. The previous canonical JSON is gzipped and written into
immutable chunked ConfigMaps `gpu-fault-release-previous-<digest>-<part>`; the main release state keeps only
the digest and chunk references; the non-sensitive role ConfigMap data actually referenced by the CPU three roles lives in that snapshot. Read,
resume, rollback, status and management-plane alignment all transparently hydrate and verify chunk by chunk, stopping immediately on a missing chunk or digest mismatch.
Rollback restores these configurations first and then starts the old wheel; tokens still exist only in Secrets and never enter ordinary ConfigMaps, logs
or release state. A successful release deletes the temporary Secret backups and cleans up old snapshot chunks no longer referenced by the current state.
When the control plane synchronises the registry at startup it first reads the raw list of cluster IDs, deletes old IDs no longer declared, then checks and overwrites the old payload of the same ID by the Region
field, and only then reads with the new model. Therefore, when adding required fields, old
payloads must not be required to pass the new model first; the declared configuration must provide the complete new fields, and the final object after migration still fails closed.
On a cluster-local failure the site enters `PAUSED`: completed clusters stay `CONVERGED`, the failed cluster keeps its current
checkpoint, and subsequent clusters stay `PENDING`. After the fix, rerunning the same four-parameter deploy resumes automatically.
`resume` accepts only the `failed` or upgrade-checkpoint state of the same uncommitted release, reads the completed phases,
clusters and Fleet waves and skips completed work. It rebuilds and validates from persistent state the retry diff, the previous snapshot
digest, the CPU required/compatible window, the cluster identities and the node sets, and must not fall back to the default `FULL`; the low-level command
must also use the scripts inside the candidate's isolated checkout and the configuration generated by the same checkout, and must not let the relative
`dist/current-release.json` fall back to the development main repository. Rollback is trimmed by actual component progress, compensating only
components that have started mutation, and restores serially in reverse order of the actual mutations; clusters verified as matching are not replayed.
`T_safe` is recorded when restoration completes, and `T_full` after the complete cross-plane verify; the safety,
Reconciler and Agent convergence durations per cluster and per Fleet wave are saved in `rollback_timing`. The original upgrade error and the rollback error are saved separately.
Every new non-resume transaction and `sync-state` clears the previous release's rollback phase,
cluster checkpoints and result; only an explicit resume of the same failed or checkpointed release keeps the current
checkpoint.
Before a Profile change finalises, it stops if non-terminal workflows or active workloads of the old Profile still exist.

**Replacing a stopped fail-forward transaction with another candidate (`--supersede-failed-transaction`).**
After a fail-forward transaction (`spec.autoRollback: false`, an accepted schema change, or a cluster-local failure entering
`partial-convergence`) stops at `failed`/`partial-convergence`, production is a mixed state of "failed candidate + previous committed
release". Fixing the defect produces a **new** release id, and rerunning deploy directly is then refused:
`release <failed id> stopped in phase failed and the candidate <new id> is a different release;
a failed fail-forward transaction only resumes the release that failed. To open a new
transaction for the candidate on the last committed baseline, rerun the same deploy with
--supersede-failed-transaction`. Rerunning the same release id is still an ordinary resume with unchanged behaviour. With that parameter
(the CLI passes it to the engine as the environment variable `GPU_FAULT_RELEASE_SUPERSEDE_FAILED_TRANSACTION=1`, the same mechanism as
`--accept-schema-change`) the engine opens a **new transaction** for the new candidate instead of resuming. The `status` exit code the deployment driver reads in this mixed state
is 1 (the executor compatibility window left by the failed transaction fails the `control_api` check); the driver treats it as a valid report rather than
a read failure; and as long as the live transaction is not a committed `complete` (failed / rolled-back / mid-way), regardless of whether the source has changed relative to the last successful deployment,
it is judged APPLICATION_RELEASE and handed to the engine, rather than being classified DEPLOY_HOST_ONLY by source identity and doing nothing:

- **Baseline inheritance**: the new transaction's `previous` (the landing point of automatic rollback) directly inherits the failed transaction's `previous`, i.e. the snapshot of the previous committed
  release and the Secret backups it made at the time, without re-capturing from the mixed state; the engine first checks the failed transaction's recorded
  `previous_snapshot_sha256`, that the Secret backups exist and that cluster membership is unchanged, refusing and touching nothing on any mismatch. The new transaction
  recomputes its own `previous_snapshot_sha256` and does not pin the failed transaction's `approved_manifest_sha256` (the new candidate
  is precisely the fix to deploy; M-23's digest is produced by this transaction itself).
- **Diff union**: `release_diff.changed` = the new candidate's changes relative to the failed candidate ∪ the `release_diff.changed` recorded by the failed transaction
  (plus a comparison of the physical wheel/bundle names in previous), with kind promoted along with the union. Components the failed candidate already rolled are re-rolled to the new candidate even when the new candidate
  equals the baseline, otherwise half of a never-committed release would remain online.
- **Audit record**: the new state writes `superseded_transaction` (failed release id, phase, `updated_at_epoch`,
  `original_failure`, the original `release_diff`, `superseded_at`, and the original `schema_change_acceptance`),
  visible in `gpu-fault-admin status`; with the parameter, `next_deploy` of `release-diff` reports
  `resume: false` and `supersedes_release_id`.
- **Schema acceptance carried over**: when the schema version already accepted and migrated by the failed transaction matches the new candidate, the acceptance record (including the snapshot id)
  is carried into the new transaction with no need to pass `--accept-schema-change` again; if the new candidate moves the schema further, that is a new schema change,
  refused as usual until accepted by this command.
- **Rollback policy**: the new transaction reads the site's `spec.autoRollback` like any new transaction and does not inherit the failed transaction's fail-forward.

The parameter is valid only in the states above: when the state is `complete`, a mid-way checkpoint (`cpu-staged` etc.), any `rollback-*`,
`rolled-back`, a bootstrap phase, or the candidate is the failed release itself, the parameter is always refused with a reason, to avoid
the habit of adding it reflexively. The low-level `resume` gives the same hint on encountering a different candidate, but supersede only goes through `deploy`.

##### REG-8.1.5 Joining a Second GPU Cluster to an Existing Control Plane

The formal entry point is to give `deploy` all managed GPU ARNs plus the new cluster (a superset of the managed set);
`join-cluster` is kept as an alias, accepting one or several repeated new GPU EKS/HyperPod ARNs:

```bash
gpu-fault-admin deploy \
  --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault \
  --gpu-cluster-arn arn:aws:eks:us-west-2:123456789012:cluster/gpu-prod-a \
  --gpu-cluster-arn arn:aws:eks:us-west-2:123456789012:cluster/gpu-prod-b

gpu-fault-admin join-cluster \
  --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault \
  --gpu-cluster-arn \
    arn:aws:eks:us-west-2:123456789012:cluster/gpu-prod-b \
  --gpu-cluster-arn \
    arn:aws:eks:us-west-2:123456789012:cluster/gpu-prod-c
```

The command does not rebuild Aurora, the CPU control plane, the NLB or the GPU clusters. When the first deploy is given several GPU ARNs at once,
the remaining clusters automatically enter batch join after the first cluster completes the baseline; the number of clusters rolled simultaneously comes from the site's
`spec.release.upgradeMaxParallelClusters` (default 1, maximum 8), sharing the same policy with deploy;
batch join no longer has its own concurrency or node installation budget. It:

1. Auto-discovers the EKS/HyperPod, VPC, NAT EIPs and nodes, and validates account, Region and `NodeRecovery`;
2. Updates the dedicated GPU kubeconfig, creates the token, Executor IAM/OIDC and node Action keys;
3. Allows the added NAT EIPs and associates the new VPC with the existing private hosted zone;
4. Runs read-only discovery and prerequisite resource preparation of each target in parallel; all targets share one candidate preflight; a separate baseline verify of the
   existing site is no longer done, since the candidate verify in step 6 already covers all clusters;
5. Waits for the Executor, Watcher, Collector, Reconciler and all Agents to become ready;
6. Runs exactly one final complete verify for the whole batch; the evidence is bound to the candidate site SHA, the live release-state
   identity, the complete registry cluster state/generation/content, the target cluster IDs and the time,
   expiring after 15 minutes -- expiry only means clearing `VERIFIED` and verifying again, and never rolls back an already joined data plane;
   each join reads only three runtime snapshots (before verify, after verify, and the final snapshot after activation);
7. Writes the EKS, HyperPod, Executor IAM and Route53 association into the Aurora resource registry.

State and evidence live under `join-cluster/<arn-hash>/`; the new cluster's token is written into the site's `secure/` (the same place as the first token written by bootstrap;
tokens already written under `join-cluster/<arn-hash>/secure/` on old sites continue to be read by the path in
site.yaml). The batch summary (joined/already_managed/failed with each cluster's record path) is printed directly
in the command result, and on partial failure it is output with the error message; no separate batch state file is written any more. Each cluster independently goes through
`PENDING -> ACTIVE/FAILED -> ROLLED_BACK`; the formal site, release state and Aurora registry
are merged incrementally inside the site operation lock, and one failed cluster does not undo the successful clusters of the same batch.
When several join CLIs start concurrently on the same site, only one obtains the site operation lock; the rest fail immediately reporting the pid and command of the
lock holder (deploy, join, remove, uninstall, config and workflow-reconcile share this one
lock and this one policy), never waiting silently. Parallelism only happens inside a single batch.
The Secret disaster-recovery copy uses `resourceVersion` CAS, re-reading and merging after a conflict. The site, release state and
Aurora are committed first, then `ACTIVATION_STARTED` is written before activation; after that point it is fail-forward only. Each cluster's
transaction state is fixed at `join-cluster/<arn-hash>/state.json` and does not occupy the site root `state.json`.

Agent artifact/config/protocol and the Executor protocol are global policies of the control plane. A new cluster must
use the current required release; if an upgrade is needed, all registered GPU clusters must be put into the same
`upgrade` configuration; you cannot upgrade only one and finalise immediately.

##### REG-8.1.6 Removing a GPU Cluster

```bash
gpu-fault-admin remove-cluster \
  --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault \
  --gpu-cluster-arn \
    arn:aws:eks:us-west-2:123456789012:cluster/gpu-prod-b \
  --confirm REMOVE_GPU_CLUSTER
```

The high-level command first cleans up the target data plane and node services. After the Agent/Executor stop, it asynchronously requests namespace deletion,
deregisters the CPU registry/keys in parallel and cleans up IAM/network in waves by installation-resource dependency; finally it waits for the
namespace to disappear and verifies in parallel the target's absence, the registry, EKS/HyperPod preservation and the health of the remaining site. CPU
join and remove share the same site transaction lock. When disassociating Route53 it first waits for ChangeInfo to reach `INSYNC`, then
confirms the VPC association has disappeared from the hosted zone; it must not disassociate while remaining clusters still reference the same VPC.
The control plane, the other GPU clusters and the target GPU EKS/HyperPod are all kept.

Deleting the last registry entry is allowed; regional mode uses the explicit empty list `[]` and the CPU control plane keeps running.
The Runtime Profile registration anchor is not required to belong to the current GPU set. Removal acceptance includes at least:

```bash
# After gpu-fault-admin remove-cluster returns, use the old token from a temporary probe on the removed data plane:
# expect HTTP 403 with detail regional cluster is not registered.

# Execution-token query on the CPU side:
cp_get /v1/regional/clusters
# The output must no longer contain the removed cluster_id.
```
#### REG-8.2 Manual Release Object Inventory and Three-Tier Rollback

Any manual release that does not use the unified orchestrator must first save the current declarative references. Record only object names,
digests and replica counts; do not export Secret plaintext:

```bash
export RELEASE_AUDIT_DIR="$(
  mktemp -d "${SECURE_DIR}/release-audit.XXXXXX"
)"
chmod 0700 "${RELEASE_AUDIT_DIR}"

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get configmap gpu-fault-release-metadata -o yaml \
  > "${RELEASE_AUDIT_DIR}/release-metadata.yaml"

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment \
  gpu-fault-api-ha \
  gpu-fault-control-worker \
  gpu-fault-telemetry-spool-worker \
  -o json |
jq '[
  .items[] | {
    name: .metadata.name,
    replicas: .spec.replicas,
    wheel: (
      .spec.template.spec.volumes[] |
      select(.name == "artifact") |
      .configMap.name
    ),
    artifact_sha256:
      .spec.template.metadata.annotations["gpu-fault.io/artifact-sha256"]
  }
]' > "${RELEASE_AUDIT_DIR}/cpu-deployments.json"

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment \
  gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  gpu-fault-completion-watcher \
  gpu-fault-kubernetes-node-resource-collector \
  gpu-fault-node-installer-reconciler \
  -o yaml > "${RELEASE_AUDIT_DIR}/gpu-deployments.yaml"

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" get nodes \
  -l "sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name=${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
  -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,UID:.metadata.uid,STATE:.metadata.annotations.gpu-fault\.io/installer-state,ARTIFACT:.metadata.annotations.gpu-fault\.io/installer-artifact-sha256,CONFIG:.metadata.annotations.gpu-fault\.io/installer-config-digest' \
  > "${RELEASE_AUDIT_DIR}/node-release.txt"
```

A manual release may finalize only after all of the following objects point at the same release:

1. The CPU content-addressed wheel ConfigMap and `gpu-fault-release-metadata`;
2. All ingress, control-worker and spool-worker with `replicas > 0`;
3. The Executor, Completion Watcher and Resource Collector wheels of every GPU cluster;
4. The installer bundle, Reconciler wheel and Job template of every GPU cluster;
5. The installer artifact/config annotations and Agent heartbeat of every current Node UID;
6. Only at the end promote the candidate pin to required and clear the compatible fields.

Recommended rollback entry point (the rollback target is always the previous committed version in the release state; no id needed):

```bash
gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault --rollback
gpu-fault-admin status --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault --full
```

The orchestrator reads the previous reference and component progress from `gpu-fault-regional-release-state`,
transparently loads the content-addressed snapshot, and compensates only components that actually started mutation. `STARTED/FAILED` is persisted immediately,
several components within the same GPU action are merged into one checkpoint; `COMPLETED` is written merged with the next phase or cluster
checkpoint. GPU clusters are restored serially in reverse of the actual upgrade order --
Executor/Watcher/Resource Collector, Reconciler bundle/template and Node Agent; afterwards the CPU is restored only when
the CPU pin, Profile, registry or CPU roles actually changed. Candidate Installer Jobs and Agent
FleetDeployments are cancelled only within the corresponding cluster/component scope, and A/B slots and immutable artefacts are kept until the
rollback window ends. Any step failure stays fail closed; you must not just run
`rollout undo` on ingress: a Deployment rollback does not restore release metadata, registry, Secrets, installer
bundle or node host artefacts. After the complete verify passes it also aligns the formal site, administrator capacity configuration and release
state, so that subsequent `gpu-fault-admin status` judges health by the rolled-back online version.

Release duration is dominated by the number of `kubectl` calls: each `kubectl` on the deployment host (exec-plugin auth plus API
round trip) takes about 1.2 s. The engine therefore batches reads of many objects of the same kind instead of calling per object: the
18 role ConfigMaps of the `previous` snapshot (`gpu-fault-{api-ha,control-worker,telemetry-spool-worker}-config-*`)
are read with one `kubectl get configmap <name>… --ignore-not-found -o json`, and a missing name still stops immediately with
ReleaseError (the rule refusing capture of sensitive keys is unchanged); the
`verify_control_plane_role_split.py` run after every role apply likewise issues one `get deployment` for the three role Deployments and
one `get configmap` for all ConfigMaps they reference (`optional: true` semantics consistent with kubelet,
unchanged); the `gpu-fault-release-history` audit document is lazily read once per engine process at the first checkpoint,
appended in memory afterwards, with only one `kubectl apply` per checkpoint -- the site operation lock guarantees only
one mutating engine process at a time, dry-run neither reads nor writes, and the truncation rule keeping the latest 200 entries is unchanged.

#### REG-9. Cluster Token Rotation

The formal entry point is one command, replacing the former 8 steps / about 14 manual commands (`kubectl exec` to read the generation,
`openssl rand`, `jq` rewriting the registry Secret, rolling the CPU roles, patching the GPU Secret,
`rollout restart`, the REG-10 DaemonSet rewriting node env, two log greps, file cleanup).
The 2026-09-04 release incident happened precisely because the manual procedure did not include the deployment host's own
`secure/<cluster-id>.token`, leaving an expired file behind.

```bash
gpu-fault-admin rotate-token \
  --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault \
  --gpu-cluster-arn arn:aws:eks:us-west-2:123456789012:cluster/gpu-prod-a \
  --reference CHG-12345
```

1. **Applicability**: the target cluster has no `PENDING`/`LEASED`/`WAITING` remote commands and no
   unfinished release transaction (any intermediate phase of `deploy`/`resume`/`rollback`, or a `complete` not yet
   committed). If either fails, the command refuses outright and modifies nothing. It is not necessary to wait until there are no non-terminal
   workflows: old and new tokens are both valid during the overlap window (design in Administrator Operations §6.1).
2. **Scope of impact**: only the GPU cluster pointed to by `--gpu-cluster-arn`. The CPU side only publishes two extra registry
   revisions (overlap first, then finalisation); that cluster's Executor, Completion Watcher and Resource Collector
   Deployments each roll once; every node reinstalls the Agent once via the same fleet wave as a release (Agent lease / open command
   safety gate -> Reconciler wave ConfigMap handover -> `installer-state=Retrying` triggering the installer reinstall ->
   installer state and heartbeat convergence), and the node's Agent is briefly unavailable during the reinstall. Other GPU
   clusters, the execution token and Aurora are all unchanged.
3. **Read-only checks**: `gpu-fault-admin status --state-dir ...` healthy;
   `<state-dir>/rotate-token/<cluster-id>/state.json` absent, or its `status` is
   `COMPLETED`/`ROLLED_BACK` (`IN_PROGRESS` means the last run did not finish; rerunning the same command resumes);
   the Reconciler's wave ConfigMap `allowed-nodes` is `*` (otherwise a release is rolling nodes and
   the command refuses).
4. **Run the command**: as above. Optional parameters: `--window-minutes` (how long the old token keeps being accepted, default
   120, allowed from 10 minutes to 7 days, set in minutes or hours, not days), `--quiet-seconds` (how long the control-plane logs
   must be continuously free of `authenticated with the retiring token` before the data plane counts as switched, default 180),
   `--acceptance-timeout-seconds` (default 1200), `--keep-window` (do not delete the retiring
   digest immediately at finalisation; leave it until the window expires naturally), `--rollback` (return an unfinished rotation to the old token). The new token is generated by the deployment
   host and exists only as a 0600 file at `<state-dir>/rotate-token/<cluster-id>/pending.token`;
   it is not printed, does not enter the command line, and does not enter `state.json` (which records only the sha256). State machine:
   `PREPARED → REGISTRY_OVERLAP_PUBLISHED → CONNECTION_SECRET_UPDATED →
   DATA_PLANE_ROLLED → NODES_ROLLED → DATA_PLANE_ACCEPTED → TOKEN_FILE_WRITTEN →
   RETIRING_TOKEN_DROPPED`. After any step fails, rerun with exactly the same parameters to resume from the first unfinished step;
   on resume, if the remaining window is under 10 minutes it first republishes the overlap revision to extend the window and then continues, rather than relying on expiry to finalise.
5. **Success criteria**: `status` in the output JSON is `COMPLETED`, all eight `steps` have timestamps,
   `old_token_sha256` and `new_token_sha256` differ; the sha256 of the `token_file` content equals
   `new_token_sha256`, and `<file>.retired-<time>` appears next to it; in `GET /v1/regional/clusters`
   that cluster has `retiring_token_sha256_present=false` (without `--keep-window`); `warnings`
   is empty -- if `remote command terminal counters grew` appears, a remote command was terminated during the rollout;
   investigate per REG-14. Looking only at Pod Ready does not count as success: before `TOKEN_FILE_WRITTEN` the deployment
   host still holds the old token, and the next release would republish the old token.
6. **Rollback**: any failure before `DATA_PLANE_ACCEPTED` can be
   `gpu-fault-admin rotate-token ... --rollback`: it writes the old token back into the GPU Secret, rolls the data-plane
   Deployments, reinstalls only the nodes that already received the new token, and finally publishes a registry
   revision containing only the old digest; both tokens are accepted throughout, with no interruption window. If publishing the overlap revision itself fails, the command
   automatically republishes the old digest with no human intervention. After `TOKEN_FILE_WRITTEN` the old token has been retired and
   `--rollback` refuses -- rolling back at that point is equivalent to doing another rotation.
7. **Evidence**: `<state-dir>/rotate-token/<cluster-id>/state.json` (archived to
   `history/<rotation_id>.json` when the next rotation starts) records the operator ARN, `--reference`, the two digests, the completion time and registry generation of every step,
   the reinstalled nodes and the remote command baseline; the command's output JSON is the
   change-ticket attachment. None of these contains the token in plaintext.

Correspondence to the manual procedure: the registry overlap revision (former step 3), the GPU Secret (step 4), the data-plane restart
(step 5), the node credentials (step 6, no longer using the REG-10 DaemonSet), the retirement log confirmation (step 7),
and the finalisation publish and file replacement (step 8) are all done by the command; the
`gpu-fault-regional-clusters` Secret used by bootstrap is updated with the new token at finalisation. REG-10 is now used only for endpoint and CA
migration, no longer for token rotation.

#### REG-10. Bulk Migration of Node Endpoints and Credentials

`deploy/migrations/gpu-node-collector-endpoint-migration.yaml` is a one-shot
DaemonSet for switching existing GPU nodes that already have the collector/Node Agent installed to the regional control
plane in bulk, or for rewriting node credentials in bulk after a token rotation. It does four things: installs the CA at
`/etc/gpu-fault/control-plane-ca.crt`, reinstalls the host wheel, rewrites `collector.env` and `node-agent.env` with `upsert_env`,
disables `gpu-fault-log-collector` and restarts the other five
services.

It runs `chroot /host` directly with `privileged: true`, should only run inside a maintenance window, and must first be validated on a single node
(limit the scope with a `nodeSelector` plus a temporary label). Brand-new nodes do not need it -- they go through
`install-gpu-fault-collector.sh` or the installer reconciler.

All placeholders in the manifest must be replaced; applying it directly fails or writes wrong values:

| Line | Placeholder | Replace with |
|---|---|---|
| `nodeSelector` | `REPLACE_WITH_CLUSTER_ID` | `${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}` (the value of `sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name`; although the placeholder name contains CLUSTER_ID, the stable `CLUSTER_ID` must not be filled in) |
| `node-agent.env` write | `REPLACE_WITH_ARTIFACT_SHA256` | The sha256 of this release's Node Runtime wheel; must match the CPU required Agent artifact |
| `node-agent.env` write | `REPLACE_WITH_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST` | `components.node_runtime.module_digest` of the release manifest |
| `volumes.artifact` | `REPLACE_WITH_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP` | The content-addressed wheel ConfigMap name in the GPU EKS |

Render and execute:

```bash
export NODE_WHEEL="$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py node-runtime-wheel
)"
export NODE_WHEEL_SHA="$(sha256sum "${NODE_WHEEL}" | cut -d' ' -f1)"
export NODE_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="$(
  jq -er '.components.node_runtime.module_digest' dist/current-release.json
)"
export NODE_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP="gpu-fault-node-runtime-wheel-0100-${NODE_WHEEL_SHA:0:12}"

if ! kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get configmap "${NODE_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    create configmap "${NODE_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}" \
    --from-file="${NODE_WHEEL}"
fi

sed \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_CLUSTER_ID/${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_ARTIFACT_SHA256/${NODE_WHEEL_SHA}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST/${NODE_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP/${NODE_WHEEL_CONFIG_MAP}/" \
  deploy/migrations/gpu-node-collector-endpoint-migration.yaml \
  > /tmp/gpu-fault-node-endpoint-migration.yaml

grep -c REPLACE_WITH /tmp/gpu-fault-node-endpoint-migration.yaml
```

`grep -c` must print `0`. After confirming, apply, check the output node by node, then delete the DaemonSet (the trailing
`sleep 3600` in the container only keeps the logs around; not deleting it leaves one privileged Pod per node for a long time):

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  apply -f /tmp/gpu-fault-node-endpoint-migration.yaml
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status daemonset/gpu-fault-node-endpoint-migration \
  --timeout=900s

for pod in $(
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pod -l app=gpu-fault-node-endpoint-migration \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[*].metadata.name}'
); do
  echo "== ${pod}"
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    logs "${pod}" | tail -20
done

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  delete -f /tmp/gpu-fault-node-endpoint-migration.yaml
rm -f /tmp/gpu-fault-node-endpoint-migration.yaml
```

Every Pod's log must contain `endpoint=`, `ca_sha256=`, `artifact_sha256=`, all five services
`active`, and `node_log=` either `disabled` or empty. If any node fails this, do not continue: the host
env files have been rewritten but the services may not be up.

#### REG-10A. Component Artefact Consistency Check (Mandatory Before Every Deployment and Troubleshooting)

The wheel file name and `importlib.metadata.version` both reflect only the
`0.10.0` in `pyproject.toml`, **not the content**. So when the source changed but was not rebuilt, or the Deployment still points at an old
content-addressed ConfigMap, the Pod runs old code while every version identifier looks normal.

This failure mode can be verified by a reverse drill: make the `module_digest()` of the package running in the Pod differ from the artefact to be released
while keeping the version string the same; the gate must refuse to continue the rollout.

**Before asserting that any endpoint or metric is missing**, run this check first:

```bash
SRC_DIGEST="$(
  PYTHONPATH=src:. python3.12 -c \
    'from scripts.component_wheels import component_source_digest; print(component_source_digest("control_plane"))'
)"
# /v1/version is not a public endpoint: it also exposes release fingerprints such as
```
