```bash
# required_agent_artifact_sha256, so it uses operator credentials (default-deny in regional mode). Omitting this header
# yields 403, not "endpoint does not exist".
TOKEN="$(kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get secret gpu-fault-control-plane-active \
    -o jsonpath='{.data.execution-token}' | base64 -d)"
for pod in $(kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pod -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
    --field-selector status.phase=Running \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[*].metadata.name}'); do
  echo -n "${pod} "
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" exec "${pod}" -- \
    env TOK="${TOKEN}" python3 -c '
import json, os, urllib.request
request = urllib.request.Request(
    "http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/version",
    headers={"X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token": os.environ["TOK"]},
)
print(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(request, timeout=10))["module_digest"])'
done
echo "src=${SRC_DIGEST}"
```

`module_digest` is the SHA-256 of all behavioural files inside a component wheel (ignoring
`__pycache__`). Every control-plane Pod must equal the Control Plane source closure and the manifest's
`components.control_plane.module_digest`. The Executor and Node Runtime use their own
component digests; the three values are different by nature and cannot be compared across components. On a mismatch go back to §4 and rerun
the complete build and gate; do not rebuild ad hoc in this section.

CPU and GPU Pods may share the same OCI image digest, but not the same Python component environment.
The system Python must not import `gpu_fault`; the CPU uses `/opt/gpu-fault/control-plane` and the GPU data plane uses
`/opt/gpu-fault/executor`. The wheel SHA/module digest in the runtime image descriptor must exactly match the corresponding component in the
Manifest.

`gpu-fault-control-worker` runs the same ASGI application too (`--workers 4`, port
**8081**, not 8080), so you can probe it with the same `/v1/version` or compute directly inside
the container -- the latter needs neither port nor credentials and is more convenient when troubleshooting:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" exec \
  "${WORKER_POD}" -- python3 -c 'import gpu_fault; print(gpu_fault.module_digest())'
```

Periodic GPU inventory, anomaly-free GPU metrics health-summaries and Host telemetry
health-summaries can be consumed by the third tier `gpu-fault-telemetry-spool-worker`. Current production
keeps `GPU_FAULT_TELEMETRY_SPOOL=false` by default with that Deployment at 0 replicas, because
the 32-cluster stress test still does not simultaneously satisfy full acceptance and a 60-second drain. For explicit experiments scale to 3 replicas; each
Pod is fixed at a single uvicorn process (port **8082**; when switching spool from enabled to disabled the role rollout script
reads that value from the Pod's `http` container port to probe the drain, and a Pod not yet answering just keeps polling), 8 replay
threads; when all three backlog classes exist they are scheduled inventory/GPU metrics/Host telemetry
`1:2:1`, and when one class is empty the others may borrow its slots. It does not start the processor main queue,
the workflow dispatcher or the managed observer.

The spool-worker calls the in-process telemetry batch core functions directly; it no longer opens a loopback TCP connection per batch
or re-enters the ASGI Store I/O queue. When a priority 0 backlog
exists, each Pod automatically drops to 1 replay worker. On the ingress side, 64 consecutive submissions in order are assigned
to the same spool admission stripe, then rotated across the 8 stripes; each stripe is serial internally,
but one batch can span several clusters. The ingress per-process PostgreSQL pool has max 40 and min 2,
with connections growing on demand. Store I/O threads are configured independently as general 28, evidence 4, fault 8, spool admission 8,
sharing the same connection pool; enabling spool does not subtract 8 from the general thread count, nor does it mean every thread holds a connection.
Check the connection budget, the runtime demand ratio and checkout waits separately; do not treat the thread cap as a connection partition.
`threshold:*`, `sustained:*`, candidate, counter, EFA changes and any
`collection_errors` still enter the priority 50 main queue, and XID/SXID remain priority 0.

The release order when experimenting again must be:

1. First apply and wait for all `gpu-fault-telemetry-spool-worker` Pods to be Ready, confirming
   `gpu_fault_telemetry_spool_consumer_running=1`.
2. Then roll ingress so that `GPU_FAULT_TELEMETRY_SPOOL=true` starts writing to the spool.
3. Verify that depth/bytes can fall back and `dropped/rejected/errors` do not grow before widening the stress test.
Rollback is in the reverse order: first disable ingress admission, wait until
`gpu_fault_telemetry_spool_depth` and `leased_bytes` are both 0, then scale down or delete the
spool-worker. Do not stop the consumer first, or ingress keeps accepting with nobody consuming.

The component digests are also the criterion of `make artifact-check`: it deletes `dist/`, rebuilds,
then asserts that the three wheels each equal their own source dependency closure and that the wheel inside the bundle equals the Node
Runtime wheel. The manifest annotation
`gpu-fault.io/artifact-sha256` records **intent** and proves nothing about what the process loaded --
both control-plane Deployments have carried annotations inconsistent with the wheel ConfigMap actually mounted.

Two mandatory checks before rolling the control plane:

1. **In-flight commands must be 0.** Only when both `PENDING` and `LEASED` of `remote_command_total`
   are `0` may you roll. When the old wheel does not export that metric, query the store directly:

   ```bash
   kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
     exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c "
   from gpu_fault.app import ApplicationContext
   from collections import Counter
   ctx = ApplicationContext.from_environment()
   items = ctx.store._list('remote_command')
   print(Counter(getattr(i.status,'value',i.status) for i in items))
   "
   ```

2. **Literal values left by `kubectl set env` make the apply fail outright.** Production once used
   `set env` to set `GPU_FAULT_EMAIL_SENDER`/`RECIPIENTS` to literal addresses while the manifest uses
   `secretKeyRef`. After the strategic merge by `name`, the same variable carries both `value`
   and `valueFrom`, and the API server refuses:

   ```text
   spec.template.spec.containers[0].env[67].valueFrom: Invalid value: "":
   may not be specified when `value` is not empty
   ```

   First remove the manual value with `kubectl set env deployment/gpu-fault-api-ha VAR-` and then apply.
   Before removing, you **must** check that the value in the Secret equals the manual value, otherwise the recipients silently change:

   ```bash
   kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
     get secret gpu-fault-email -o json |
   python3 -c "import base64,json,sys; d=json.load(sys.stdin)['data']; [print(k,'=',base64.b64decode(v).decode()) for k,v in sorted(d.items())]"
   ```

Expected side effect after the rollout: the regional periodic thread cleans up terminal
`remote_command` records according to `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_COMPLETED_RETENTION_SECONDS` (default 600 seconds).
If the previously running wheel lacked this cleanup path, the historical terminal records are cleared in one go after the rollout and every state of
`remote_command_total` drops to zero. This is expected behaviour, not data loss.

#### REG-11. Cluster Action Executor Upgrade and Rollback

Normal upgrade and rollback use the bound `gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir` /
`deploy --rollback`; the release orchestrator saves previous, renders images and pins and verifies convergence.
This section keeps only a reviewed break-glass reference; the shared-image example below does not apply to schema-v4,
and must not be used as grounds to relax the separate image, offline dependency or required/compatible pin checks.

The executor is stateless: all command state lives in the CPU control plane's Aurora
(`GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_REMOTE_STATE=true`), and the executor only claims/executes/reports.
So an upgrade is just replacing the wheel ConfigMap and rolling the Deployment, with no data migration.

The risk lies in **commands currently executing**: a rolling restart makes already LEASED commands lose their executor. During execution
the executor renews the lease from a background thread every `lease_seconds / 3` (capped at 30 seconds)
(`CommandLifecycle.renew_lease` in `gpu_fault.cluster_executor.lease`), so a long command is not stolen by another
executor because of a timeout; but once the Pod is deleted the renewal stops, and the command is re-claimed after
`GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_LEASE_SECONDS` (default 120 seconds). If the original Pod
already produced side effects on the target cluster (cordon, delete pod, HyperPod reboot), re-execution relies on each
adapter's idempotency keys. Therefore you must confirm there are no non-terminal commands before upgrading.

A batch claim sets the lease on the whole batch at the same moment, yet the executor still executes the batch sequentially.
The configuration must therefore satisfy:

```text
GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_BATCH_SIZE × worst-case duration of one command
    < GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_LEASE_SECONDS
```

Production should reserve at least 20% jitter margin. Measured on 2026-08-20 with 25 commands, about 5 seconds each and a 120-second lease,
the first 23 succeeded and the 24th/25th had expired before their turn and returned 409; under that timing model a batch of at most
20 is recommended. The current default `BATCH_SIZE=5` satisfies the constraint. Before increasing the batch you must recompute with the slowest
NodeAction/HyperPod operation in the target environment; do not estimate from empty claim latency alone.

Step 1: check in-flight commands:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import urllib.request; print("".join(l for l in urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8080/metrics", timeout=10).read().decode().splitlines(True) if "remote_command_total" in l))'
```

Both `PENDING` and `LEASED` must be `0`. When non-zero, wait for convergence; do not force the upgrade.

Step 2: record the current version for rollback:

```bash
export EXECUTOR_ROLLBACK_CONFIG_MAP="$(
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
    -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.volumes[?(@.name=="artifact")].configMap.name}'
)"
echo "rollback to: ${EXECUTOR_ROLLBACK_CONFIG_MAP}"
```

Content-addressed ConfigMaps are never overwritten, so the old version stays available; **do not** clean up old wheel ConfigMaps,
or you lose the rollback target.

Step 3: upload the new wheel and render the new manifest. The wheel ConfigMap is still used for the physical artefact proof and pin, but the process code
comes from the pre-installed OCI image declared by the signed Manifest; runtime `pip install` must not be restored:

While rolling the control plane, the release metadata must keep the old stable executor protocol as
`required` and place the new protocol only in
`compatible-regional-executor-protocol-versions`. The old control plane does not know the compatible
field; if the new protocol is set as required prematurely, the old Pods created by `rollout undo` read the
new pin, and rollback becomes unusable instead. Only after the control plane has fully upgraded does this section's data-plane rollout begin.

```bash
export NEW_WHEEL="$(
  python3.12 scripts/release-artifact-path.py executor-wheel
)"
test -s "${NEW_WHEEL}"
export NEW_WHEEL_SHA="$(sha256sum "${NEW_WHEEL}" | cut -d' ' -f1)"
export NEW_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST="$(
  jq -er '.components.executor.module_digest' dist/current-release.json
)"
export NEW_CONFIG_MAP="gpu-fault-executor-wheel-0100-${NEW_WHEEL_SHA:0:12}"

# This ConfigMap is only a pin/identity artefact: the runtime image has the executor built in (PATH points at
# /opt/gpu-fault/executor/bin) and the Pod does not install from /artifact. When creating by hand stay consistent with the release
# engine: store the xz-compressed wheel under the key <wheel>.whl.xz.
if ! kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get configmap "${NEW_CONFIG_MAP}" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  # The identity artefact matches the releaser, using .whl.xz; the running code still comes from the signature-verified image.
  xz -9e -c "${NEW_WHEEL}" > "/tmp/$(basename "${NEW_WHEEL}").xz"
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    create configmap "${NEW_CONFIG_MAP}" \
    --from-file="$(basename "${NEW_WHEEL}").xz=/tmp/$(basename "${NEW_WHEEL}").xz"
fi

# The IRSA role annotation must be replaced together. The manifest holds a placeholder, and **missing the replacement is worse than not writing it**:
# apply would overwrite the correct eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn on the ServiceAccount with
# REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN, the newly created Pods would then get no credentials,
# and an executor with the HyperPod adapter enabled fails and exits straight away at startup.
# Use the value already in the cluster to avoid transcription errors:
export EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN="$(
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" get sa \
    gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
    -o jsonpath='{.metadata.annotations.eks\.amazonaws\.com/role-arn}'
)"
test -n "${EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN}"

sed \
  -e "s/gpu-fault-executor-wheel-0100$/${NEW_CONFIG_MAP}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_ARTIFACT_SHA256/${NEW_WHEEL_SHA}/" \
  -e "s/REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST/${NEW_COMPATIBILITY_DIGEST}/" \
  -e "s#public.ecr.aws/docker/library/python:3.12-slim#${GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_IMAGE}#g" \
  -e "s#REPLACE_WITH_EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN#${EXECUTOR_IRSA_ROLE_ARN}#" \
  deploy/dataplane/cluster-action-executor.yaml \
  > /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml

grep -n 'configMap:' -A1 /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml
# Not a single placeholder may remain (grep exits 1 when there is no match, which is exactly the expected value here)
! grep -n REPLACE_WITH /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml
```

Step 4: roll the upgrade and verify functional readiness, not just Ready:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  apply -f /tmp/gpu-fault-cluster-action-executor.yaml
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor --timeout=600s

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor --since=3m |
  grep -E 'ERROR|Traceback|401|403|CERTIFICATE_VERIFY_FAILED' &&
  exit 1 || true

# Clean logs != usable data plane. This step checks the items that do not appear in the logs: the IRSA annotation and
# the token actually injected into the Pod, the wheel ConfigMap versus the control plane's required Executor pin,
# the three ENABLE_* switches, and the Secret keys needed by the enabled adapters.
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  GPU_FAULT_KUBE_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  python3 deploy/dataplane/tools/verify_dataplane_executor.py
```

When the data plane is rolled, the control plane has usually already finished rolling, so this step also blocks "the data plane lagging one version
behind": the script preferentially reads
`required-regional-executor-artifact-sha256` from `gpu-fault-release-metadata` to derive the target Executor ConfigMap. Only when the old
metadata has no separate pin does it fall back to the control-plane Deployment's legacy wheel. You can also give the baseline explicitly with
`GPU_FAULT_EXPECTED_WHEEL_CONFIGMAP`; when no baseline can be obtained the script errors out
rather than silently skipping.

Step 5: rollback. While the compatibility window has not yet closed, the old stable protocol is still required; you can replay the `sed` + `apply` of steps 3 and 4 with the ConfigMap name
recorded in step 2, or simply run
`kubectl rollout undo`. The Deployment's old ReplicaSet still references the old content-addressed wheel; do not
delete a ConfigMap referenced by a ReplicaSet.

If the new protocol has already been promoted to required and the compatibility window cleared, you must first restore the old protocol as
required and put the new protocol back into compatible, then roll back the Pods. Otherwise the old executor is refused with 503 before
claiming. `rollout undo` only reverts the Deployment spec, not the Secret, runtime
profile or release metadata:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout undo deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor --timeout=600s
```

#### REG-12. GPU Cluster Deregistration

Deregistration runs in the reverse order of registration: stop the data plane first, then delete the entry from the control-plane registry. Doing it the other way round leaves the executor
retrying with 401 continuously after its credential is revoked, flooding the logs.

The administrator's normal path runs only:

```bash
gpu-fault-admin remove-cluster \
  --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault \
  --gpu-cluster-arn "${GPU_CLUSTER_ARN}" \
  --confirm REMOVE_GPU_CLUSTER
```

The command state lives at `remove-cluster/<cluster-id>/state.json` (the cluster-id is resolved from that ARN's record in the site) and can be resumed with the original parameters. The step-by-step commands below are only for
inspecting old versions or repairing an interruption of the high-level state machine; they are no longer the normal operating path.

Step 1: use the unified cleanup entry point to generate a **single GPU cluster** plan. It covers the Executor, Completion
Watcher, Kubernetes node resource collector, Installer/Reconciler, GPU/DCGM
DaemonSets, historical HMA components and all node systemd units; do not stop just two of them by hand any more:

```bash
: "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG:?must point to this Region's release JSON}"

deploy/control-plane/regional/prepare-clean-redeploy.sh \
  --config "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG}" \
  --scope gpu \
  --cluster-id "${CLUSTER_ID}" \
  --mode clean \
  --node-mode uninstall
```

Step 2: execute with a new state file. The script first confirms the cluster has no active workflows/remote commands,
stops the reporting sources, waits for the cluster's processor/spool rows to reach zero, and only then stops the Executor:

```bash
export GPU_UNREGISTER_STATE="$(
  printf '%s/unregister-%s-%s.json' \
    "${SECURE_DIR}" "${CLUSTER_ID}" "$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ)"
)"

deploy/control-plane/regional/prepare-clean-redeploy.sh \
  --config "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG}" \
  --scope gpu \
  --cluster-id "${CLUSTER_ID}" \
  --mode clean \
  --node-mode uninstall \
  --state-file "${GPU_UNREGISTER_STATE}" \
  --execute

python3 deploy/control-plane/tools/cleanup_state.py verify \
  --path "${GPU_UNREGISTER_STATE}"
test "$(jq -r .phase "${GPU_UNREGISTER_STATE}")" = CLEANUP_COMPLETED
test "$(jq -r .status "${GPU_UNREGISTER_STATE}")" = COMPLETED
```

Step 3: delete the entry from the control-plane registry. The current version allows an explicit empty registry `[]`; an old version not yet
upgraded to a release supporting an empty registry must be upgraded before removing the last cluster:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get secret gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
  -o jsonpath='{.data.clusters\.json}' |
  base64 -d > "${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.current.json"

jq --arg cluster_id "${CLUSTER_ID}" \
  'map(select(.cluster_id != $cluster_id))' \
  "${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.current.json" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.json"

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create secret generic gpu-fault-regional-clusters \
  --from-file=clusters.json="${SECURE_DIR}/clusters.json" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" apply -f -

KUBECONFIG="${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE="${NAMESPACE}" \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/restart-regional-control-plane.sh
```

Step 4: delete the GPU-side credentials the script deliberately kept, and clean up the NLB whitelist:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete secret gpu-fault-regional-connection --ignore-not-found
```

At the same time, following the reverse of step 7 of this chapter, remove that cluster's NAT EIPs from the NLB Security Group inbound rules
and `shred` the local `${SECURE_DIR}`. Historical request/workflow records are kept in Aurora
for audit; deregistration does not delete them.

Step 5: acceptance: the registry query for that `cluster_id` returns empty; calling claim with the old token returns
401/403:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- env TOKEN="${EXECUTION_TOKEN}" python -c \
  'import json,os,sys,urllib.request; r=urllib.request.Request("http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/regional/clusters", headers={"X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token": os.environ["TOKEN"]}); print([c["cluster_id"] for c in json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(r, timeout=10))])'
```
#### REG-13. Permanent Decommissioning of the Regional Solution (Delete the CPU Cluster, Keep the GPU Clusters)

Run this only when the solution will no longer be used. The final state must be: the CPU EKS/HyperPod, Aurora, NLB,
solution IAM/monitoring/Secrets all absent; every GPU EKS/HyperPod cluster still present with only this
solution's components removed. **No step of this procedure may call `delete-cluster` on a GPU cluster.**

The administrator's normal path runs only:

```bash
gpu-fault-admin uninstall \
  --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault \
  --cpu-cluster delete \
  --aurora-final-snapshot retain \
  --confirm DELETE_CPU_CONTROL_PLANE
```

The steps below are only for old deployments, item-by-item regulatory audit, or break-glass when the unified CLI cannot run. They must
meet the same standards of registry export, GPU preservation and residue verification as the unified command.

Step 1: record the CPU/NLB/Aurora and every GPU cluster identity, then dry-run. Do not run a
manual Aurora deletion first; complete the registry and audit data export first:

```bash
: "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG:?must contain all GPU clusters}"
regional_require \
  AWS_REGION CPU_EKS_NAME CPU_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER \
  CPU_KUBECONFIG NAMESPACE SECURE_DIR
: "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID:?}"
: "${NLB_NAME:?}"
: "${NLB_SG_ID:?}"
: "${TLS_CERTIFICATE_ARN:?}"
: "${PKI_SECRET_ID:?}"

aws eks describe-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" --name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/cpu-eks-before-retire.json"
aws sagemaker describe-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" --cluster-name "${CPU_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER}" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/cpu-hyperpod-before-retire.json"
aws rds describe-db-clusters \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
  > "${SECURE_DIR}/aurora-before-retire.json"
chmod 0600 "${SECURE_DIR}"/*-before-retire.json

deploy/control-plane/regional/prepare-clean-redeploy.sh \
  --config "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG}" \
  --scope all \
  --mode clean \
  --node-mode uninstall
```

Step 2: safely stop and uninstall the CPU/GPU components, but keep the Aurora data to make the final snapshot:

```bash
export REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE="$(
  printf '%s/regional-retire-%s.json' \
    "${SECURE_DIR}" "$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ)"
)"
test ! -e "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}"

deploy/control-plane/regional/prepare-clean-redeploy.sh \
  --config "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG}" \
  --scope all \
  --mode clean \
  --node-mode uninstall \
  --state-file "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}" \
  --execute

python3 deploy/control-plane/tools/cleanup_state.py verify \
  --path "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}"
test "$(jq -r .phase "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}")" = CLEANUP_COMPLETED
test "$(jq -r .status "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}")" = COMPLETED
```

Step 3: delete the NLB Service, wait for the Controller to delete the NLB/listener/target group, then delete the NLB SG, ACM certificate, PKI Secret and Load Balancer
Controller/IAM per §0.3 steps 5 and 6. The command order cannot be swapped:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete service gpu-fault-api-nlb \
  --ignore-not-found --wait=true --timeout=10m

for _ in {1..120}; do
  if ! NLB_ERROR="$(
    aws elbv2 describe-load-balancers \
      --region "${AWS_REGION}" --names "${NLB_NAME}" 2>&1
  )"; then
    [[ "${NLB_ERROR}" == *LoadBalancerNotFound* ]] && break
    printf '%s\n' "${NLB_ERROR}" >&2
    false
  fi
  sleep 5
done
[[ "${NLB_ERROR:-}" == *LoadBalancerNotFound* ]]
```

Step 4: before deleting Aurora, delete the CPU/GPU solution namespaces and advance the external state to the only
gate that permits database deletion. Even if the runtime ConfigMaps disappear at this point, resume is unaffected, because the complete
inventory, original replica counts, phase history and SHA-256 are already in `${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}`:

```bash
python3 - "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG}" <<'PY' |
import json
import sys

for cluster in json.load(open(sys.argv[1], encoding="utf-8"))["clusters"]:
    print(cluster["cluster_id"], cluster["context"])
PY
while read -r cluster_id context; do
  kubectl --context "${context}" \
    delete namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
    --ignore-not-found --wait=true --timeout=10m
  kubectl --context "${context}" get --raw=/readyz
  printf 'GPU_CLUSTER_PRESERVED=%s\n' "${cluster_id}"
done

kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" \
  delete namespace "${NAMESPACE}" \
  --ignore-not-found --wait=true --timeout=10m

python3 deploy/control-plane/tools/cleanup_state.py transition \
  --path "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}" \
  --phase READY_TO_DELETE_AURORA \
  --status COMPLETED \
  --message "Kubernetes, node, NLB, and namespace cleanup verified"
python3 deploy/control-plane/tools/cleanup_state.py verify \
  --path "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}"
```

Step 5: decide on the Aurora final snapshot. The final snapshot is kept by default; switch to `--skip-final-snapshot` only when regulations explicitly require immediate physical destruction and
approval has been completed. The external gate must be verified again before the delete command:

```bash
test "$(jq -r .phase "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}")" = READY_TO_DELETE_AURORA
test "$(jq -r .status "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}")" = COMPLETED

export FINAL_AURORA_SNAPSHOT="$(
  printf '%s-final-%s' \
    "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" "$(date -u +%Y%m%d%H%M%S)"
)"

aws rds modify-db-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
  --no-deletion-protection \
  --apply-immediately

mapfile -t AURORA_INSTANCE_IDS < <(
  aws rds describe-db-instances \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --query "DBInstances[?DBClusterIdentifier=='${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}'].DBInstanceIdentifier" \
    --output text |
  tr '\t' '\n'
)
for instance_id in "${AURORA_INSTANCE_IDS[@]}"; do
  test -n "${instance_id}" || continue
  aws rds delete-db-instance \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --db-instance-identifier "${instance_id}" \
    --skip-final-snapshot \
    --delete-automated-backups
done
for instance_id in "${AURORA_INSTANCE_IDS[@]}"; do
  test -n "${instance_id}" || continue
  aws rds wait db-instance-deleted \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --db-instance-identifier "${instance_id}"
done

aws rds delete-db-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" \
  --final-db-snapshot-identifier "${FINAL_AURORA_SNAPSHOT}" \
  --delete-automated-backups
aws rds wait db-cluster-deleted \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}"
aws rds describe-db-cluster-snapshots \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-snapshot-identifier "${FINAL_AURORA_SNAPSHOT}" \
  --query 'DBClusterSnapshots[0].Status' --output text |
  grep -x available
```

After confirming the cluster is deleted, delete the Aurora-specific subnet group and SG:

```bash
aws rds delete-db-subnet-group \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-subnet-group-name "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}"
aws ec2 delete-security-group \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --group-id "${AURORA_SG_ID}"

python3 deploy/control-plane/tools/cleanup_state.py transition \
  --path "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}" \
  --phase AURORA_DELETED \
  --status COMPLETED \
  --message "Aurora cluster verified deleted"
python3 deploy/control-plane/tools/cleanup_state.py verify \
  --path "${REGIONAL_RETIRE_STATE}"
```

Step 6: delete the CPU-side Pod Identity associations and the solution-specific IAM/AMP/SNS and other resources.
When the CPU EKS is a cluster dedicated to this solution, the deletion must be performed through the same infrastructure lifecycle entry point that created it;
the repository cannot infer from the cluster which subnets/NAT/route tables/SGs are safe to delete. Keep the real IaC commands in
controlled variables and generate a plan first:

```bash
: "${CPU_INFRA_PLAN_COMMAND:?a read-only infrastructure deletion plan command must be provided}"
: "${CPU_INFRA_DESTROY_COMMAND:?an approved infrastructure deletion command must be provided}"
bash -lc "${CPU_INFRA_PLAN_COMMAND}"
```

The plan must contain only the CPU control plane and its VPC/subnets/NAT/route tables/SGs/EIPs, the CPU HyperPod/EKS
nodes, the solution IAM/Pod Identity, AMP/SNS and other dedicated resources; it must not contain any GPU EKS/HyperPod
cluster. After reviewing the plan, execute:

```bash
bash -lc "${CPU_INFRA_DESTROY_COMMAND}"
```

Environments without IaC must delete the CPU HyperPod first, then the CPU EKS; before deletion the
nodegroups, Fargate profiles and Pod Identity associations still have to be cleaned up one by one:

```bash
aws sagemaker delete-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --cluster-name "${CPU_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER}"

until ! aws sagemaker describe-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --cluster-name "${CPU_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER}" >/dev/null 2>&1; do
  sleep 30
done

mapfile -t CPU_NODEGROUPS < <(
  aws eks list-nodegroups \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --query 'nodegroups[]' --output text |
  tr '\t' '\n'
)
for nodegroup in "${CPU_NODEGROUPS[@]}"; do
  aws eks delete-nodegroup \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --nodegroup-name "${nodegroup}"
  aws eks wait nodegroup-deleted \
    --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
    --cluster-name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" \
    --nodegroup-name "${nodegroup}"
done

aws eks delete-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" --name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}"
aws eks wait cluster-deleted \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" --name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}"
```
Step 7: final acceptance. The CPU EKS/Aurora/NLB queries must return not-found; every GPU context must still succeed at
`/readyz`:

```bash
! aws eks describe-cluster \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" --name "${CPU_EKS_NAME}" >/dev/null 2>&1
! aws rds describe-db-clusters \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" \
  --db-cluster-identifier "${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}" >/dev/null 2>&1
! aws elbv2 describe-load-balancers \
  --region "${AWS_REGION}" --names "${NLB_NAME}" >/dev/null 2>&1

python3 - "${REGIONAL_RELEASE_CONFIG}" <<'PY' |
import json
import sys

for cluster in json.load(open(sys.argv[1], encoding="utf-8"))["clusters"]:
    print(cluster["context"])
PY
while IFS= read -r context; do
  kubectl --context "${context}" get --raw=/readyz
done
```

#### REG-14. Regional Mode Troubleshooting

The failure modes of regional mode differ from single-cluster: the control plane does not operate on any GPU cluster directly; every action is handed to the executor via remote
command. So "it looks like nothing is happening" usually means the command was not claimed, not that execution failed. Locate the problem in the following
order.

##### REG-14.1 CPU API Pod CrashLoopBackOff

The regional-mode startup guard exits directly with `RuntimeError` on a configuration contradiction; this is intended behaviour, and must not be bypassed by increasing
`initialDelaySeconds`. Look at the error string first:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha --tail=40 --previous |
  grep -E 'RuntimeError|regional'
```

| Log fragment | Cause | Handling |
|---|---|---|
| `regional mode requires GPU_FAULT_REGIONAL_CLUSTERS_JSON` | The Secret `gpu-fault-regional-clusters` does not exist or the key name is not `clusters.json` | Create per step 2 of the registration chapter; the key must be `clusters.json` |
| `GPU_FAULT_REGIONAL_CLUSTERS_JSON is invalid` | JSON syntax error, commonly a newline introduced when concatenating with `jq -r`, or the token written as a bare string | Validate locally with `jq empty` before writing the Secret |
| `regional cluster registry must be a list` | The registry JSON is not an array | Change to an array of objects; an unloaded control plane uses an explicit `[]` |
| `regional mode uses the cluster registry; GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER must be unset` | Environment variables copied from a single-cluster deployment | Remove `GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER` from the regional patch |
| `regional control plane must not enable the in-cluster KubernetesWorkflowAdapter` | `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_KUBERNETES_ADAPTER=true` | Change to `false`; scheduling actions go through remote commands |
| `regional control plane must delegate HyperPod mutations to the target cluster executor` | `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER=true` (this is a data-plane switch, not a control-plane switch) | Set `false` on the control plane and enable it in the executor manifest |
| `regional mode requires remote execution owners` | `GPU_FAULT_REMOTE_EXECUTION_OWNERS` was set to an empty string | Remove the variable to use the default, or fill in the complete owner list |
| `HyperPod managed recovery observer requires GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_AGENT_REGISTRY=true` | The agent registry was disabled while the managed recovery observer was kept | Enable the registry, or set `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_MANAGED_OBSERVER` to `false` |

Validate the combination of these switches in the manifests without waiting for a Pod crash:

```bash
ROLE_SPLIT_CHECK_DIR="$(mktemp -d)"
GPU_FAULT_ROLE_SPLIT_OUT_DIR="${ROLE_SPLIT_CHECK_DIR}" \
  deploy/control-plane/tools/render-control-plane-role-split.sh
PYTHONPATH=src python3.12 -m gpu_fault.config_cli validate \
  "${ROLE_SPLIT_CHECK_DIR}"
rm -rf "${ROLE_SPLIT_CHECK_DIR}"
```

##### REG-14.2 Commands Stay PENDING (Most Common)

`PENDING` means the command has been written to the control plane but no executor has claimed it. No component reports an error for this, so you must
check proactively. Look at the alerts and metrics first:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import urllib.request; print("".join(l for l in urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8080/metrics", timeout=10).read().decode().splitlines(True) if "remote_command" in l))'
```

`gpu_fault_remote_command_oldest_unclaimed_seconds{cluster_id="..."}` above 600 seconds
triggers the `GpuFaultRemoteCommandUnclaimed` alert. Rule out causes in this order:

1. **The executor is not claiming at all.** `kubectl -n gpu-fault-system logs -l
   app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor --since=5m`. With no claim logs at all, check the OCI image digest,
   entrypoint and `startupProbe`; the production Manifest performs no runtime `pip install`.
2. **TLS or token failure.** `CERTIFICATE_VERIFY_FAILED` in the logs => `ca.crt` not mounted
   or `SSL_CERT_FILE` missing; `401`/`403` => the tokens on the two sides differ; re-sync per the token rotation sub-section.
   Note that the readinessProbe only verifies `/healthz` is reachable; both failures still leave the Pod
   Ready.
3. **`cluster_id` mismatch.** The GPU Secret's `cluster-id` must be exactly identical to the registry entry
   (case and leading/trailing whitespace count). Claiming with the wrong ID returns 200 but `commands` is always empty:
   ```bash
   kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
     get secret gpu-fault-regional-connection \
     -o jsonpath='{.data.cluster-id}' | base64 -d | od -c | tail -3
   ```
   The output must not end with `\n`.
4. **Owner not advertised (the most hidden).** The executor reports to the claim endpoint only the owners of the adapters it
   **actually constructed**, and the control plane silently skips commands whose owner is not advertised: neither side reports an error. Setting
   `nodeReboot`/`nodeReplace` to OWN in the runtime profile (owner
   `gpu-fault-hyperpod-adapter`) while the executor has
   `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER=false` is exactly this case. Cross-check:
   ```bash
   kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
     get deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
     -o jsonpath='{range .spec.template.spec.containers[0].env[*]}{.name}={.value}{"\n"}{end}' |
     grep -E 'ENABLE_(NODE_ACTION|HYPERPOD)_ADAPTER|ALLOW_HYPERPOD'

   python3.12 -c '
   import sys, yaml
   profile = yaml.safe_load(open(sys.argv[1]))
   for claim in profile["claims"]:
       if claim["mode"] in {"OWN", "DELEGATE"}:
           print(claim["capability"], claim["owner"])
   ' config/runtime-profile.regional-hyperpod-safe.example.yaml
   ```
   Every owner in the output must be covered by an enabled adapter:
   `gpu-fault-kubernetes-adapter` is enabled by default; `gpu-fault-node-agent` needs
   `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_NODE_ACTION_ADAPTER=true`; `gpu-fault-hyperpod-adapter` needs
   `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER=true`, and reboot/replace are additionally controlled by
   `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REBOOT` / `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REPLACE` respectively.
   Capabilities whose owner is `gpu-fault-control-plane`, `gpu-fault-validation-adapter` or
   `gpu-fault-support-escalation` execute locally on the control plane and produce no remote command.
5. **The command belongs to a deregistered or disabled cluster.** When the registry entry has `enabled: false`, claims are
   refused and commands pile up.

Unclaimed commands have a timeout and dead-lettering: the control plane's periodic cleanup sets commands still `PENDING` whose creation time exceeds
`GPU_FAULT_REMOTE_COMMAND_CLAIM_DEADLINE_SECONDS` (default 900 seconds)
to `FAILED` with `status_source=unclaimed-deadline-exceeded`, and the error states that no executor announced the
`execution_owner`. The workflow therefore **fails** and enters the alerting path instead of staying in WAITING
forever; setting 0 disables the timeout, but that reverts to the old behaviour of "nobody takes the order and nobody knows".
The count is on `gpu_fault_remote_command_unclaimed_expired` in `/metrics`;
the `GpuFaultRemoteCommandUnclaimed` alert remains the earlier means of discovery -- do not turn it off.

##### REG-14.3 Commands Stuck in LEASED

The executor claimed the command but did not report a result. During execution the executor renews the lease automatically
(`lease_seconds / 3`, capped at 30 seconds), so a persistent LEASED usually means the Pod has disappeared or renewal is
failing (`regional command lease renewal failed` in the logs); after the lease expires at
`GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_LEASE_SECONDS` (default 120 seconds) the command can be re-claimed.
If it is still LEASED after more than 2 lease periods, check:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get pod -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor --since=10m |
  grep -E 'could not report result|Traceback'
```

`regional cluster executor could not report result` means the action was executed on the GPU cluster but reporting the result
failed (network jitter or a control-plane rollout). The command is then re-claimed and executed once more, relying on the adapter's
idempotency keys for deduplication. If a real action (such as a HyperPod replace) really executed twice, record it and evaluate, based on the duration of
`gpu_fault_remote_command_total{status="LEASED"}`, whether
`LEASE_SECONDS` needs lowering.

Duplicate submission of HyperPod reboot/replace is blocked by the submission record persisted in the control plane, not by executor
process memory -- see REG-14.7.

##### REG-14.4 A Step FAILED with an Unclear Cause

The result carries a `status_source` field distinguishing the failure origin:

| `status_source` | Meaning | Handling |
|---|---|---|
| Missing or an adapter's own value | The adapter executed normally and judged failure | Handle according to the specific adapter's failure reason |
| `executor-rejected` | The executor actively refused (credentials, namespace allowlist, switch not enabled) | Look at `details`; usually a configuration problem |
| `executor-internal-error` | **An exception in the executor's own code**; `details.exception_type` gives the exception type | A defect, not a configuration problem; the retained count is shown by the `gpu_fault_remote_command_executor_internal_errors_total` gauge, `gpu_fault_remote_command_executor_internal_error_last_seen_timestamp_seconds` records the latest event and triggers `GpuFaultRemoteCommandExecutorInternalError` within a 900-second window; capture the full executor stack trace |

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  logs -l app=gpu-fault-cluster-executor --since=30m |
  grep -A30 'Traceback'
```

##### REG-14.5 All Nodes Become Non-ACTIVE

Node credentials and executor credentials are two separate distribution paths: the executor reads the Kubernetes Secret, while the collectors and
Node Agent read the host `/etc/gpu-fault/*.env`. Updating only the Secret leaves the executor working while all
nodes lose contact after `GPU_FAULT_AGENT_MAX_HEARTBEAT_AGE_SECONDS` (default 90 seconds).
Use the endpoint migration DaemonSet of sub-section 10 to synchronise the host env files.

Another cause is an artifact compatibility refusal: when the CPU side's
`GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256` / `GPU_FAULT_REQUIRED_AGENT_CONFIG_DIGEST`
differ from the nodes' actual values, registration is refused. Read the actual values before setting them; do not guess in advance:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- env TOKEN="${EXECUTION_TOKEN}" python -c \
  'import json,os,sys,urllib.parse,urllib.request; c=urllib.parse.quote(sys.argv[1], safe=""); r=urllib.request.Request("http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/fleet/agents?cluster_id="+c, headers={"X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token": os.environ["TOKEN"]}); d=json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(r, timeout=15)); print(json.dumps(d, indent=2)[:4000])' \
  "${CLUSTER_ID}"
```

##### REG-14.6 Receiving 403 "regional mode requires a valid X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token"

Regional-mode authorisation is default-deny: any request that is neither on the public whitelist (`/healthz`, `/metrics`, `/openapi.json`,
`/docs`, `/redoc`) nor a data-plane path requires the operator execution token.
`/v1/fleet/agents` and `/v1/fleet/readiness` accept two kinds of credentials -- with
`X-GPU-Fault-Cluster-ID` they validate the data-plane token and enforce scope; without it they require the execution
token. So operator calls must explicitly carry the execution token and must not carry the cluster ID
header at the same time.

##### REG-14.7 HyperPod Reboot Reports "unknown outcome" or "already reserved"

The idempotency record of a provider reboot **is stored in the CPU control-plane database**, not in executor process memory.
The reason is that a reboot often restarts the very executor Pod that submitted it: if the record lived only in memory, a replay after the restart would send the provider
another `BatchRebootClusterNodes`. Provider replace is always disabled; although the compatibility model
can still read historical replace records, the current runtime path must not create new replace submissions. The executor defaults to
`GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_REMOTE_STATE=true` and has no database of its own, so it reads and writes the control-plane records through three data-plane
paths (authenticated with the per-cluster cluster token like the other `/v1/regional/executors/` paths, and enforcing that the payload's `cluster_id` and `record.cluster_name` match the registered entry):

| Path | Method | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `/v1/regional/executors/hyperpod-submissions/reserve` | POST | Registers `INTENDED` **before** calling the provider; only the party whose registration succeeded (`reserved=true`) may submit |
| `/v1/regional/executors/hyperpod-submissions` | GET | Replays an existing record by `cluster_name` + `idempotency_key` |
| `/v1/regional/executors/hyperpod-submissions/outcome` | POST | Writes `SUBMITTED` or `UNKNOWN` after the call returns |

This directly distinguishes three failures:

| Step error | Record state | Meaning and handling |
|---|---|---|
| `...has an unknown outcome; an operator must confirm the node state before retrying` | `UNKNOWN` | The provider call raised an exception and **whether the submission took effect is unknown**. Not retrying automatically is deliberate: first confirm the node's actual state with CloudTrail and `describe-cluster-node`, and replay the workflow manually only after confirming it did not take effect |
| `a HyperPod submission for this idempotency key is already reserved and has no recorded outcome` | `INTENDED` | The submission is in progress on another replica, or the executor was killed mid-submission. Re-check after one lease period; still `INTENDED` means the process is dead; confirm manually as in the previous row |
| `idempotency key was already used for a different HyperPod request` | Any | The same key was used for a different action or node set; a defect or a hand-crafted request; do not overwrite the record |

To view the record of a step (over loopback from inside the CPU API Pod, with that cluster's cluster token):

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- env \
  TOKEN="${CLUSTER_TOKEN}" CID="${CLUSTER_ID}" \
  HP="${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" KEY="${IDEMPOTENCY_KEY}" python -c \
  'import json,os,urllib.parse,urllib.request; q=urllib.parse.urlencode({"cluster_name":os.environ["HP"],"idempotency_key":os.environ["KEY"]}); r=urllib.request.Request("http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/regional/executors/hyperpod-submissions?"+q, headers={"Authorization":"Bearer "+os.environ["TOKEN"],"X-GPU-Fault-Cluster-ID":os.environ["CID"]}); print(json.dumps(json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(r, timeout=10)), indent=2))'
```

`IDEMPOTENCY_KEY` must be the HyperPod provider submission key. Preferably read it from the remote command
receipt's `result_details.submission_idempotency_key`; for old receipts or while the command is still
`LEASED`, derive it with
`gpu_fault.hyperpod.hyperpod_submission_idempotency_key(request_id, step_index,
operation)`. The current format is
`<request_id>/<operation>/<step_index>`, which differs from the remote command's own
`<request_id>/<step_index>/<operation>`; the latter cannot simply be copied.

Before force-restarting the executor or performing a duplicate replay, you must first read, by that exact key, a record with
`state=SUBMITTED`, a non-empty `result`, and an action/node set consistent with the command. If any condition
is unmet, stop immediately; do not "probe" whether the record exists by calling the adapter, because a non-existent key is
treated as a new provider submission.

In addition, the executor's `confirm_cluster_name` second confirmation is taken only from the separate
`GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CONFIRM_CLUSTER` (the connection Secret's
`hyperpod-confirm-cluster-name`). The actual provider target still comes from
`GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_CLUSTER`. The two values must be supplied by different configuration items and explicitly compared; the command or
provider configuration must not confirm itself. Therefore:

- When the variable is missing, every HyperPod reboot/replace step FAILs directly, reporting
  `confirm_cluster_name` missing. For an executor that does not own HyperPod changes, leaving it missing is the correct configuration.
- When the confirmation variable differs from the provider's `hyperpod_cluster_name`, the step fails at the adapter's confirmation
  gate. This is precisely the mechanism preventing one executor from mis-operating another HyperPod cluster; do not bypass it by changing code.

<a id="5a4-区域数据面训练任务提交与验收"></a>
### 5.4 Regional Data-Plane Training Job Submission and Acceptance

This chapter belongs to the data-plane delivery and acceptance flow of the regionally separated architecture. Training workloads run on the GPU EKS, and
the Completion Watcher and Cluster Executor are also deployed on that GPU EKS; the CPU control plane only receives
observations, terminal events and fault events.

#### TRAIN-1. Using the Unified Training Submission Command

Installing the project generates `gpu-training-submit`. The command is run once on a management host,
login node or CI/CD Runner that can reach the Kubernetes API, not on every GPU node. It currently supports a single
`Job`, `PyTorchJob` or `JobSet` YAML.

The system supports two formal submission methods:

1. **Recommended: `gpu-training-submit`.** Automatically generates or validates the job/attempt IDs, injects all
   Completion Watcher metadata, then directly calls `kubectl apply` once.
2. **`gpu-fault-workload-annotate` followed by a separate `kubectl apply`.** Uses exactly the same validation and injection logic
   as the first method but only generates the managed YAML; suited to scenarios that still need
   a server-side dry-run, policy scan, approval or GitOps commit before submission.

Both methods must reference an **already registered** profile; do not guess the version from an example file name. Before submitting in regional mode
run the following gate:

```bash
STATE_DIR="${GPU_FAULT_STATE_DIR:?set GPU_FAULT_STATE_DIR}"
gpu-fault-admin status --full --state-dir "${STATE_DIR}"
```

Do not submit when this command fails. `status --full` runs the complete health report (without `--full` it checks only control-plane
workloads and the control API), and must confirm the Profile is registered, warnings are empty and the content has not drifted.
The training tools read the current Profile version from the site; `profile.cluster_id` is the registration anchor of the regionally shared profile
and is not required to equal the target cluster of each training run. The target cluster identity is still bound independently by the cluster token, workload observations
and the regional registry.

On the first submission you can let the command generate the IDs:

```bash
gpu-training-submit pytorchjob.yaml \
  --site "${SITE_FILE}" \
  --restart-budget 2
```

The command prints `job-id` and `attempt-id` to standard error and submits the metadata-injected workload to the Kubernetes API.
The caller must save the printed `job-id`; when the same logical job restarts, reuse that value and increment the attempt:

```bash
gpu-training-submit pytorchjob.yaml \
  --site "${SITE_FILE}" \
  --job-id train-0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef \
  --attempt-number 2 \
  --restart-budget 2
```

An external training platform can also provide both IDs:

```bash
gpu-training-submit pytorchjob.yaml \
  --site "${SITE_FILE}" \
  --job-id customer42-llama70b \
  --attempt-id customer42-llama70b-a002
```

Review the final YAML before submitting:

```bash
gpu-training-submit pytorchjob.yaml \
  --site "${SITE_FILE}" \
  --job-id customer42-llama70b \
  --attempt-number 1 \
  --dry-run > /tmp/pytorchjob.managed.yaml
```

Parameter description:

| Parameter | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `--job-id` | Auto-generated `train-<uuid>` | Logical job ID; must stay unchanged on restart |
| `--attempt-id` | `<job-id>-aNNN` | ID of this run |
| `--attempt-number` | `1` | Used when no attempt ID is given explicitly |
| `--site` | `GPU_FAULT_SITE_FILE` | Reads the current Runtime Profile version from the site source of truth |
| `--runtime-profile-version` | Site version, otherwise `GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_PROFILE` or `hyperpod-v1` | Explicit override; must agree when given together with `--site` |
| `--expected-critical-ranks` | Derived from the workload | Number of critical Pod/rank units; an explicit value must equal the template-derived value |
| `--training-container` | Auto-derived for single-container Pods | Multi-container Pods must specify the training container |
| `--restart-budget` | Not injected; the Watcher default applies | Maximum automatic restarts of the same job |
| `--namespace` | kubectl current namespace | Also overrides `metadata.namespace` of the rendered manifest and is passed to `kubectl` |
| `--context` | kubectl current context | Target HyperPod EKS context |
| `--kubeconfig` | kubectl default path | kubeconfig file |
| `--dry-run` | false | Only outputs the injected YAML without calling kubectl |

The command:

1. Injects managed, job, attempt and role labels on the workload and all Pod Templates.
2. Injects expected ranks, the training container, the Runtime Profile and the optional restart budget.
3. Injects the global rank offset for PyTorchJob and JobSet.
4. Validates that multi-rank `Job`s and JobSet child Jobs use `completionMode: Indexed`.
5. Calls `kubectl apply -f -` once via standard input.

The command does not query or auto-increment historical attempts in the cluster. The training platform or operator must save the job ID and
supply an unused attempt number/ID for restarts; the control plane's idempotency and restart budget still prevent duplicate recovery.

##### TRAIN-1a. Inject the YAML First, Then Submit Separately

This entry point does not access the Kubernetes API. It only reads one `Job`, `PyTorchJob` or `JobSet`,
generates the managed YAML, and prints the final job/attempt IDs to stderr:

```bash
gpu-fault-workload-annotate \
  examples/hyperpod/three-node-pytorchjob.yaml \
  --job-id customer42-llama70b \
  --attempt-number 1 \
  --runtime-profile-version "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" \
  --restart-budget 1 \
  --output /tmp/three-node.managed.yaml

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  apply --dry-run=server -f /tmp/three-node.managed.yaml
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  apply -f /tmp/three-node.managed.yaml
```

It can also write to stdout for feeding into signing, scanning or GitOps flows:

```bash
gpu-fault-workload-annotate training.yaml \
  --job-id customer42-llama70b \
  --attempt-id customer42-llama70b-a002 \
  > /tmp/training.managed.yaml
```

An external training platform may generate an equivalent complete managed YAML itself and `kubectl apply` it directly, but it must
satisfy the same contract. The generated YAML contains, on the workload and all Pod Templates:

- `gpu-fault.io/managed`
- `gpu-fault.io/job-id`
- `gpu-fault.io/attempt-id`
- `gpu-fault.io/role` and `gpu-fault.io/critical`
- `gpu-fault.io/expected-critical-ranks`
- `gpu-fault.io/training-container`
- `gpu-fault.io/runtime-profile-version`
- `gpu-fault.io/rank-offset`
- `gpu-fault.io/restart-budget`

The Completion Watcher discovers these Pods automatically and calls `POST /v1/workload-observations`. The production flow
should no longer have administrators assembling Observations by hand; manual POSTs are only for isolated test fixtures.

##### TRAIN-1b. Customer-Defined Training Images

`public.ecr.aws/deep-learning-containers/pytorch-training@sha256:ff2c928a2e7b3b290b7c3e353085a373b3cfc7f6b165e338b9be4df439feae38` is used only for this repository's
NCCL example and is not a control-plane dependency. Both `gpu-training-submit` and
`gpu-fault-workload-annotate` preserve the original `image`, `command`, `args`,
volume and resource configuration of the customer YAML and inject only fault-management metadata.

That digest corresponds to the AWS PyTorch Training DLC
`2.9.0-gpu-py312-cu130-ubuntu22.04-ec2-v1.6`. On 2026-08-23 it was validated in the current
H200/P5en environment with PyTorch 2.9, CUDA 13.0, NCCL 2.27.7, aws-ofi-nccl,
Libfabric/EFA direct, 24-rank `all_reduce=300.0` and three-step DDP training.

When the customer already has a final `Job`, `PyTorchJob` or `JobSet`, use that YAML directly as the input of both commands.
If you reuse the repository's three-node sample, first replace Master/Worker simultaneously with structured YAML processing; do not just
`sed` the first image occurrence:

```bash
export TRAINING_IMAGE='<registry>/<repository>@sha256:<image-digest>'
export TRAINING_CONTAINER=pytorch
export IMAGE_PULL_SECRET=''  # fill in the Kubernetes Secret name for non-ECR private registries
export TRAINING_MANIFEST=/tmp/customer-three-node-pytorchjob.yaml

SOURCE_MANIFEST=examples/hyperpod/three-node-pytorchjob.yaml \
TRAINING_IMAGE="${TRAINING_IMAGE}" \
TRAINING_CONTAINER="${TRAINING_CONTAINER}" \
IMAGE_PULL_SECRET="${IMAGE_PULL_SECRET}" \
python3.12 - <<'PY' > "${TRAINING_MANIFEST}"
import os
from pathlib import Path

import yaml

document = yaml.safe_load(
    Path(os.environ["SOURCE_MANIFEST"]).read_text(encoding="utf-8")
)
container_name = os.environ["TRAINING_CONTAINER"]
pull_secret = os.environ["IMAGE_PULL_SECRET"].strip()
for role, replica in document["spec"]["pytorchReplicaSpecs"].items():
    pod = replica["template"]["spec"]
    matches = [
        container
        for container in pod["containers"]
        if container["name"] == container_name
    ]
    if len(matches) != 1:
        raise SystemExit(
            f"{role} must contain exactly one {container_name} container"
        )
    matches[0]["image"] = os.environ["TRAINING_IMAGE"]
    matches[0]["imagePullPolicy"] = "IfNotPresent"
    if pull_secret:
        pod["imagePullSecrets"] = [{"name": pull_secret}]
print(yaml.safe_dump(document, sort_keys=False))
PY
```

Image requirements:

- An immutable `repository@sha256:digest` is recommended, so that different nodes do not run different content after a tag is overwritten;
  the pre-pull and the real workload must use exactly the same image string.
- Amazon ECR private images use the full registry/repository reference. The GPU node IAM role must have
  ECR pull permission; cross-account repositories additionally need a repository policy. The actual permission is ultimately verified by the pre-pull
  DaemonSet below.
- For other private registries, first create a
  `kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson` Secret in the training namespace and put its name in
  `${IMAGE_PULL_SECRET}`. Credentials must not be written into ordinary ConfigMaps or workload annotations.
- The image must contain the programs the customer YAML's `command/args` depend on. The repository sample requires
  `/bin/bash`, Python, PyTorch and `torchrun`, and must be compatible with the node NVIDIA driver,
  CUDA and NCCL/EFA runtime; customers may use a completely different entry point in their own YAML.
- Multi-container Pods must pass the training container name to
  `--training-container "${TRAINING_CONTAINER}"`. GPU/EFA requests, `/dev/shm`,
  volumes, checkpoints and security context remain defined by the customer workload.

Secret example for a non-ECR private registry:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create secret generic training-registry-credentials \
  --type=kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson \
  --from-file=.dockerconfigjson="${HOME}/.docker/config.json" \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" apply -f -
export IMAGE_PULL_SECRET=training-registry-credentials
```

##### TRAIN-1c. Three-Node 24 GPU / 48 EFA Example

The repository provides
`examples/hyperpod/three-node-pytorchjob.yaml`, configured as one Master Pod and two Worker
Pods. Pod anti-affinity forces the three onto different `kubernetes.io/hostname`s; each Pod requests
8 GPUs and 16 EFA devices, and starts a total of 24 processes via `torchrun --nproc-per-node=8`.
So `gpu-fault.io/expected-critical-ranks` of the managed YAML is `3` (three critical Pods),
while the PyTorch `world_size` in the training logs is `24`; the two are not the same count.

Before submitting, confirm that all three nodes have at least 8 GPUs and 16 EFA allocatable:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" get nodes \
  -o custom-columns='NAME:.metadata.name,GPU:.status.allocatable.nvidia\.com/gpu,EFA:.status.allocatable.vpc\.amazonaws\.com/efa'
```

The current example training image has about 8.81 GB of compressed layers. `imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent` starts quickly only when the target node already has a complete
cache; when the cache is missing or has been reclaimed by kubelet image GC, each of the three nodes pulling separately can take several minutes. Before submitting, pre-pull in parallel on all target GPU nodes with
a temporary DaemonSet:

```bash
export TRAINING_IMAGE="${TRAINING_IMAGE:-public.ecr.aws/deep-learning-containers/pytorch-training@sha256:ff2c928a2e7b3b290b7c3e353085a373b3cfc7f6b165e338b9be4df439feae38}"

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete serviceaccount gpu-fault-training-image-puller \
  --ignore-not-found
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  create serviceaccount gpu-fault-training-image-puller
if [[ -n "${IMAGE_PULL_SECRET:-}" ]]; then
  kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    patch serviceaccount gpu-fault-training-image-puller \
    --type=merge \
    -p "{\"imagePullSecrets\":[{\"name\":\"${IMAGE_PULL_SECRET}\"}]}"
fi

cat <<EOF | kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" apply -f -
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: DaemonSet
metadata:
  name: gpu-fault-training-image-prepull
  namespace: ${NAMESPACE}
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: gpu-fault-training-image-prepull
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: gpu-fault-training-image-prepull
    spec:
      nodeSelector:
        sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name: ${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}
      serviceAccountName: gpu-fault-training-image-puller
      tolerations:
        - operator: Exists
      containers:
        - name: pull
          image: ${TRAINING_IMAGE}
          imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent
          command: ["/bin/sh", "-c"]
          args: ["echo image-ready; sleep 3600"]
          resources:
            requests:
              cpu: 10m
              memory: 32Mi
            limits:
              cpu: 100m
              memory: 128Mi
EOF

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status daemonset/gpu-fault-training-image-prepull \
  --timeout=20m

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get pod -l app=gpu-fault-training-image-prepull -o wide

kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete daemonset gpu-fault-training-image-prepull --wait=true
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  delete serviceaccount gpu-fault-training-image-puller
```

The DaemonSet's desired count must equal the number of target GPU nodes, all Pods must have been Ready, and the events must contain no
`ErrImagePull`/`ImagePullBackOff`. Submit the training job immediately after deleting it, so the image is not
GC'd again while idle. The pre-pull requests no GPUs and does not change training capacity.

Submit:

```bash
gpu-training-submit \
  examples/hyperpod/three-node-pytorchjob.yaml \
  --job-id hp-three-node-24gpu-demo \
  --attempt-number 1 \
  --runtime-profile-version "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}" \
  --restart-budget 1
```

The cluster's Kubeflow Training Operator requires the main training container to be named `pytorch`. 8-GPU NCCL
also needs enough shared memory; the example mounts an 8 GiB memory-backed `emptyDir` at `/dev/shm`. Without that mount
you may see `Error while creating shared memory segment ... No space left on device`.

Verify the three-node resources and identity:

```bash
kubectl get pods -n gpu-fault-system \
  -l gpu-fault.io/attempt-id=hp-three-node-24gpu-demo-a001 \
  -o wide

kubectl wait --for=condition=Succeeded \
  pytorchjob/gpu-fault-three-node-pytorch \
  -n gpu-fault-system --timeout=180s
```

A successful log must satisfy all of:

- All 24 ranks print `SUCCESS ... all_reduce=300.0`;
- NCCL prints `NET/Libfabric/.../GDRDMA`, proving EFA RDMA is used across nodes;
- The Completion Watcher prints `status=SUCCEEDED` for the corresponding attempt.

<a id="5a5-区域数据面节点-systemd-安装详解"></a>
### 5.5 Regional Data-Plane Node systemd Installation in Detail

This chapter is not a separate deployment architecture but one of the node-side installation methods of REG-6. It applies to GPU data nodes that cannot use
the Kubernetes privileged Installer Job, need to be installed by a configuration management system, or need the host
systemd services maintained directly. The nodes still connect to the regional CPU control plane and use the same cluster token,
CA, wheel, Runtime Profile and node-specific action key.

#### NODE-1. Recommended Installation Command

With an existing DCGM Exporter:

```bash
sudo deploy/node/install-gpu-fault-collector.sh \
  --control-plane-url https://gpu-fault.example.internal \
  --ca-certificate /secure/path/control-plane-ca.crt \
  --cluster-id gpu-prod-01 \
  --runtime-profile-version hyperpod-active-v1 \
  --token-file /secure/cluster-token \
  --wheel-sha256 "$(cat /secure/node-runtime-wheel.sha256)" \
  --node-id "$(hostname -f)" \
  --driver-branch 575 \
  --cuda-version 12.9 \
  --metrics-mode dcgm \
  --dcgm-exporter existing \
  --dcgm-metrics-url http://127.0.0.1:9400/metrics \
  --required-interfaces efa0 \
  --training-log-paths '/var/log/pods/*/*/*.log,/opt/ml/output/**/*.log' \
  --python-command /usr/bin/python3.12 \
  --enable-node-agent \
  --node-action-secret-file /secure/node-action-secret \
  --allow-gpu-reset \
  --allow-fabric-reset \
  --allow-service-quiesce \
  --allow-fabric-manager-restart \
  --node-agent-advertise-url "https://$(hostname -I | awk '{print $1}'):9099" \
  --node-instance-id '<stable instance ID or Kubernetes Node UID>'
```

Regional production installation enables reset/quiesce by default. The bare CLI default of the generic installer is still off, so a manual
installation must pass the four parameters above explicitly to ensure the config digest in the node heartbeat matches the control-plane pin.
The production RESET_GPU and RESET_ALL_GPUS_NVSWITCHES workflows do not allow bypassing the service quiesce,
the no-GPU-client check, fencing or the control-plane allowlist.

Without a DCGM Exporter, the current solution does not start the `NvidiaSmiMetricsCollector`; the deployment should fail closed and the DCGM Exporter
completed first. Only after additional validation in an isolated environment may it be enabled explicitly:

```bash
sudo deploy/node/install-gpu-fault-collector.sh \
  --control-plane-url https://gpu-fault.example.internal \
  --cluster-id gpu-prod-01 \
  --runtime-profile-version eks-v1 \
  --metrics-mode nvidia-smi \
  --dcgm-exporter disabled \
  --enable-nvidia-smi-metrics-collector
```

#### NODE-2. Installer Parameters

Only the commonly used parameters are listed here; the complete interface and defaults are given by `deploy/node/install-gpu-fault-collector.sh --help`
(the four inventory-related parameters are also in the parameter table of section 3 of "Configuring EFA/RDMA traffic and hung diagnostics");
these parameters do not replace the managed release, node identity or maintenance-window gates.

| Parameter | Required | Default / constraint | Description |
|---|---|---|---|
| `--control-plane-url` | Yes | URL | Control-plane address |
| `--ca-certificate` | Required with a private CA | Absolute path | PEM CA certificate of the HTTPS control plane |
| `--certificate-min-validity-seconds` | No | 2592000 | Refuse installation when the remaining validity of the CA/leaf certificate is below this many seconds; the same value is used by `gpu-fault-certificate-check.timer` for periodic re-checks |
| `--cluster-id` | Yes | Non-empty | Stable cluster ID |
| `--runtime-profile-version` | Yes | Non-empty | A registered profile |
| `--node-id` | No | Host FQDN | Must match the control-plane/cluster node name |
| `--node-instance-type` | No | Read automatically on HyperPod | EC2/HyperPod instance type, written into metrics and emails |
| `--expected-gpu-count` | No | By instance type | Expected number of visible GPU UUIDs |
| `--expected-efa-device-count` | No | Not checked when unset | Expected number of ACTIVE EFA devices |
| `--inventory-mismatch-samples` | No | 2 | How many consecutive inventory mismatch rounds trigger an action |
| `--token` | No | Empty | Collector bearer token; written directly on argv, visible to processes on the same machine; production uses `--token-file` instead |
| `--token-file` | No | Empty | Reads the bearer token from a 0600 absolute-path file; mutually exclusive with `--token` |
| `--gpu-product` | No | Empty | Fallback when auto-discovery is unavailable, e.g. H200 |
| `--gpu-product-discovery` | No | `auto` | `auto/required/disabled` |
| `--driver-branch` | No | Empty | Integer branch, e.g. 575 |
| `--cuda-version` | No | Empty | e.g. 12.9 |
| `--metrics-mode` | No | `auto` | `auto/dcgm/nvidia-smi` |
| `--metrics-interval` | No | 15 seconds | Positive number |
| `--host-interval` | No | 15 seconds | Positive number |
| `--log-interval` | No | 10 seconds | Positive number |
| `--fabric-manager-log-interval` | No | 5 seconds | Fabric Manager journal/file polling period |
| `--disable-fabric-manager-journal` | No | false | Disable journald and use only the configured files |
| `--fabric-manager-identifiers` | No | NVIDIA FM default identifiers | journald `SYSLOG_IDENTIFIER/_COMM/unit` allowlist |
| `--fabric-manager-log-paths` | No | Auto-discovered | Absolute globs of Fabric Manager file logs, comma-separated; an explicit value takes precedence over the NVIDIA configuration |
| `--filesystems` | No | `/,/var,/tmp` | Comma-separated |
| `--required-interfaces` | No | Empty | Critical EFA/RDMA/NICs |
| `--nvswitch-topology-command` | No | Empty | Site NVSwitch topology query command that outputs canonical JSON |
| `--training-log-paths` | No | Empty | Absolute globs, comma-separated |
| `--dcgm-exporter` | No | `existing` | `existing/docker/disabled` |
| `--dcgm-metrics-url` | No | localhost:9400 | Prometheus URL |
| `--dcgm-exporter-image` | Conditional | None | Required in docker mode, with a pinned version/digest |
| `--dcgm-exporter-interval-ms` | No | Empty | In `existing` mode tells the collector the external exporter's collection period (decimal milliseconds >= 1000, no leading zeros); the HyperPod installation Job has the reconciler pass 15000 automatically |
| `--wheel` | No | Latest wheel in dist | Production should specify explicitly |
| `--wheel-sha256` | Yes | 64 hex digits | Expected wheel SHA-256 from the pinned release record (from the release artefacts, not the installer package); verifies the wheel to be installed and the release manifest, failing closed on mismatch |
| `--wheelhouse` | No | Empty | Offline dependency directory; dependencies are installed only from the hash lock with `--require-hashes`, never resolving unpinned packages online |
| `--dependency-lock` | No | `requirements/node-runtime.lock` | The hash lock corresponding to the candidate bundle; dependencies of another release must not be mixed in |
| `--python-command` | No | `python3` | Must be >=3.12 |
| `--disable-kernel` | No | false | Use only when an alternative XID source exists |
| `--enable-node-log-collector` | No | false | Enables the NodeLogCollector; current production deployments keep it disabled |
| `--enable-nvidia-smi-metrics-collector` | No | false | Allows the not fully validated nvidia-smi metrics fallback; production keeps it disabled |
| `--enable-node-agent` | No | false | Installs the signed-action Agent |
| `--node-action-secret[-file]` | Conditional | >=32 characters | One of the two when the Agent is enabled |
| `--derive-node-action-secret` | No | false | Derives the node-specific key by `cluster_id/node_id` from the supplied fleet secret (the node does not keep the fleet master after installation); the key version is fixed at 2 when deriving; a legacy-path compatibility parameter -- regional production provisions node-level v2 keys on the CPU and does not distribute the fleet master to GPU nodes |
| `--node-action-key-version` | No | 1 when not deriving | Declares the version of the supplied key (1 or 2); managed regional installation provides node-level keys explicitly and sets 2; 1 is only for legacy migration, and production must ultimately be 2 |
| `--node-agent-host` | No | Node ID | Agent bind address |
| `--allow-node-agent-plaintext` | No | false | Compatibility parameter explicitly allowing HTTP without a server certificate; forbidden in production |
| `--allow-gpu-reset` | No | false | Dangerous switch |
| `--allow-fabric-reset` | No | false | Allows node-local all-GPU/NVSwitch reset; requires service quiesce |
| `--allow-service-quiesce` | No | false | Allows stopping/starting GPU base services; requires reset to be enabled at the same time |
| `--allow-fabric-manager-restart` | No | false | Allows fenced Fabric Manager restart |
| `--diagnostic-output-dir` | No | `/var/lib/gpu-fault/diagnostics` | Local diagnostic bundle directory |
| `--diagnostic-s3-uri` | No | Empty | Optional S3 target for persisting diagnostic bundles |
| `--diagnostic-retention-seconds` | No | 604800 | Local diagnostic bundle retention time |
| `--diagnostic-max-archives` | No | 20 | Cap on the number of local diagnostic bundles |
| `--allow-field-diagnostic` | No | false | Enables the pinned NVIDIA Field Diagnostic; must be given together with `--field-diagnostic-command` (absolute path; `{link_id}`/`{gpu_uuid}`/`{pci_bdf}` may be used) and `--field-diagnostic-sha256` |
| `--field-diagnostic-timeout` | No | 1800, range 60-7200 | Field Diagnostic timeout (seconds) |
| `--memory-field-diagnostic-command` / `-sha256` | No | Empty | Whole-card memory diagnostic command used for row-remap failure and its SHA-256 |
| `--allow-driver-remediation` | No | false | Allows explicitly mapped SXID driver remediation; must be given together with `--driver-remediation-command` (`{target}` may be used), `--driver-remediation-sha256`, `--target-driver-branch` |
| `--allow-efa-driver-remediation` | No | true (the installer default is already on; the parameter is only an explicit confirmation) | Allows rebinding the EFA PCI driver after workloads stop; the actual action still requires the workload, Profile and signing gates |
| `--disable-efa-driver-remediation` | No | false | Explicitly disables EFA PCI driver rebinding and removes `REMEDIATE_EFA_DRIVER` from the node allowlist |
| `--allow-firmware-update` | No | false | Allows explicitly mapped SXID firmware updates; must be given together with `--firmware-update-command`/`-sha256`, `--target-firmware-version`, `--firmware-verify-command`/`-sha256` |
| `--quiesce-services` | No | `nvidia-fabricmanager,nvidia-dcgm,nvidia-persistenced,gpu-fault-gpu-persistence,gpu-fault-metrics-collector,gpu-fault-host-collector,kubelet` (7 items) | Restored in this order, stopped in reverse; the complete default is also in §7.5 |
| `--quiesce-processes` | No | NVIDIA/DCGM base processes | Sends TERM by exact process name |
| `--quiesce-containers` | No | `aws-hyperpod/health-monitoring-agent,gpu-fault-system/exporter,kube-system/nvidia-device-plugin-ctr` | Kubernetes `namespace/container` selectors stopped during quiesce |
| `--container-stop-timeout` | No | 30 seconds | Container graceful-stop timeout |
| `--container-restore-timeout` | No | 180 seconds | Timeout waiting for containers to recover |
| `--quiesce-failsafe-seconds` | No | 420, range 30-3600 | Delay of the first automatic systemd recovery |
| `--quiesce-retry-seconds` | No | 60, range 10-600 | Retry period after automatic recovery fails |
| `--quiesce-settle-seconds` | No | 2 | Settle wait after stopping services and sending TERM |
| `--node-agent-port` | No | 9099 | 1-65535 |
| `--node-agent-advertise-url` | No | `https://node:9099` | The installer auto-generates the node TLS cert/key by default; production must use HTTPS |
| `--node-agent-tls-cert` | No | Auto-generated | When given explicitly, must be given together with the key |
| `--node-agent-tls-key` | No | Auto-generated | Certificate private key |
| `--node-agent-tls-client-ca` | No | Empty | Requires client certificates and verifies them with this CA |
| `--node-heartbeat-interval` | No | 30 seconds | Minimum 5 seconds |
| `--node-inflight-wait-timeout` | No | 2100 seconds | Cap on how long a duplicate command waits for the in-flight original to finish |
| `--node-action-retention-seconds` | No | 2592000 seconds (30 days), minimum 600 | Node action ledger retention time |
| `--node-action-max-results` | No | 10000 | Cap on ledger rows |
| `--node-instance-id` | Conditional | Auto-discovered | Passing the Node UID/instance ID explicitly is recommended |
| `--no-start` | No | false | Install and enable only |
| `--print-config-digest-environment` | No | Does not install | Prints the deterministic Agent config digest inputs and exits; used to cross-check the fleet digest |

The Installer Job's `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_NODE_EFA_DRIVER_REMEDIATION` accepts only `true` / `false`,
generating the explicit allow / disable parameter respectively; a misspelling refuses to render and cannot silently fall back to the enabled default.
Enabling the capability by default does not change the guard that keeps the Node Agent off by default in a collector-only installation.

#### NODE-3. Generated Files

- `/opt/gpu-fault/releases/<artifact-sha>/venv`
- `/opt/gpu-fault/current` (atomic symlink)
- `/opt/gpu-fault/venv` (legacy rollback slot or compatibility symlink for new installations)
- `/etc/gpu-fault/collector.env`
- `/etc/gpu-fault/node-agent.env`
- `/etc/gpu-fault/dcgm-counters.csv`
- `/var/lib/gpu-fault/node-actions.db`
- `/var/lib/gpu-fault/quiesce/` (holds recovery state only during a quiesce)
- `/var/lib/gpu-fault/log-collector-state.json` (generated only after the NodeLogCollector is enabled)
- `/opt/gpu-fault/verify`
- `/opt/gpu-fault/uninstall`
- `/opt/gpu-fault/verify-certificate-bundle`, `/opt/gpu-fault/check-control-plane-certificate` (certificate check entry points, used with `gpu-fault-certificate-check.service/.timer`)
- `/opt/gpu-fault/runtime-artifact-sha256` (marker of the wheel SHA-256 of the currently active runtime)
- `/opt/gpu-fault/installed-units.txt` (inventory of the systemd units actually installed; the uninstaller works from it)
- `/opt/gpu-fault/tools/py-spy-<version>-<sha12>/` (only when the Node Agent is enabled; the py-spy venv for hung diagnostics)
- `/etc/gpu-fault/dcgm-exporter.env` (only in `--dcgm-exporter docker` mode)
- `/var/lib/gpu-fault/installer-degraded-gpu.json` (written only when the installer detects a degraded GPU; see NODE-5)

Before an upgrade or uninstall, the script checks the quiesce state file and first calls the fail-safe recovery entry point. If any service cannot
be recovered the operation aborts immediately and does not delete the recovery program or the state file.

Configuration files have mode `0600`.

#### NODE-4. NodeLogCollector Deployment Policy

`NodeLogCollector` is currently not planned for further production implementation and validation. The installer still keeps the program and systemd unit,
but by default runs `disable --now gpu-fault-log-collector.service`, and the node self-check shows it as
`SKIP`. This does not turn off kernel XID/SXID, Fabric Manager SXID, GPU metrics, host
telemetry or the Node Agent's hung diagnostics.
