  production deployment under any circumstances. Otherwise, when the separate reboot switch is absent, it falls back per
  `src/gpu_fault/hyperpod.py::HyperPodAdapterConfig.from_environment` -- only turning reboot on
  (reboot inherits the old switch when absent); replace is always false in every configuration, setting
  `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REPLACE=true` makes the process refuse to start, and the old switch cannot enable provider replace either.
- Every submission must provide the cluster second confirmation, isolated nodes, workflow/incident fencing token and idempotency key.
- Start from one test node; the batch maximum of 25 is not a recommended concurrency.

The actual change command for the regional Executor is as follows; the trailing `-` means deleting the old master switch, not setting it to
an empty string:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  set env deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_MUTATION- \
  GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER=true \
  GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REBOOT=true \
  GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REPLACE=false
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  rollout status deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  --timeout=600s
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  -o jsonpath='{range .spec.template.spec.containers[0].env[*]}{.name}={.value}{"\n"}{end}' |
  grep -E 'GPU_FAULT_(ENABLE_HYPERPOD_ADAPTER|ALLOW_HYPERPOD_)'
```

`GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_MUTATION` must not appear in the output, and the three separate values must be
`true`, `true`, `false` respectively. The same values must be written back into the manifests or configuration used by the next release, otherwise
a later `kubectl apply` overwrites this activation.

#### Phase D.2: warm-spare nodeReplace

warm-spare replacement is not HyperPod provider node replacement. It executes
`REPLACE_NODE` + `replacement_strategy=HEALTHY_WARM_SPARE_ONLY`, completing local reservation, activation and workload rebinding from the pool of spare nodes the customer already holds and
manages; the faulty node stays isolated and
is not destroyed, and `BatchReplaceClusterNodes` must not be called at any stage.

Before go-live all of the following must be proven:

1. HyperPod `NodeRecovery=None`, the Runtime Profile's `nodeReplace` owner is
   `gpu-fault-hyperpod-adapter`, and the observed version is `warm-spare-v1`.
2. The Executor keeps
   `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_SPARE_FAILOVER=true`,
   `GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_REMOTE_STATE=true` and
   `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REPLACE=false`. The spare failover switch is a safety lock forcing the local path and
   refusing provider fallback, not an independent action authorisation.
3. Every candidate spare carries `gpu-fault.io/spare=true`, belongs to the same instance
   group/type as the faulty node, and simultaneously satisfies HyperPod `Running`, provider
   `Schedulable`, Kubernetes `Ready`, Agent fleet-ready, no active faults and not reserved by another
   incident.
4. The number of healthy spares can cover all faulty nodes of this round. Allocation and activation must complete atomically; when insufficient the whole
   `REPLACE_NODE` fails, with no partial replacement.
5. The request contains the cluster second confirmation, verified node isolation, workflow/incident fencing token
   and idempotency key.
6. The complete order passes acceptance:
   `MARK_UNSCHEDULABLE/QUARANTINE -> STOP_WORKLOADS -> REPLACE_NODE ->
   VALIDATE_GPU -> VALIDATE_FABRIC -> RESTORE_SCHEDULING -> RESTART_WORKLOAD`.

Check the regional Executor:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  get deployment/gpu-fault-cluster-executor \
  -o jsonpath='{range .spec.template.spec.containers[0].env[*]}{.name}={.value}{"\n"}{end}' |
  grep -E 'GPU_FAULT_(ENABLE_HYPERPOD_SPARE_FAILOVER|CLUSTER_EXECUTOR_REMOTE_STATE|ALLOW_HYPERPOD_REPLACE)'
```

The three values must be `true`, `true`, `false` respectively. With no healthy spare, health still being confirmed,
provider recovery `Automatic`, or any version/fencing/validation gate failing, the workflow
must stay waiting/failed, and the faulty node stays isolated and enters the administrator alert or support flow.

<a id="15-示例清单使用警告"></a>
<a id="13-示例清单使用警告"></a>
## 11. Warning on Using the Sample Manifests

The following files contain test-environment hard-coding or placeholders and cannot be used in production as is:

- `deploy/dataplane/completion-watcher.yaml`
- `scripts/e2e/regional/manifests/hyperpod-canary.yaml`
- `deploy/dataplane/hyperpod-dcgm-exporter.yaml`
- `deploy/migrations/sqlite-to-postgres-migration.yaml`
- `deploy/node/run-hyperpod-installer-job.sh`

Key search:

```bash
rg -n 'REPLACE_WITH|hp-cluster|hyperpod-i-|gpu-fault-api-canary|wheel-0100' \
  deploy
```

Test manifests in `scripts/e2e/regional/manifests/` carrying `REPLACE_WITH_WHEEL_CONFIGMAP` must be rendered to `/tmp` with
`scripts/e2e/render_manifest.py` before applying; manifests needing a specific node
select it via the temporary label `gpu-fault.io/e2e-target=true` and do not accept a hard-coded nodeName.

HyperPod regional sites render resources from the same trusted release via `gpu-fault-admin deploy`;
`deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh` is kept only as the old single-cluster compatibility entry point of Appendix A.
## Configuring EFA/RDMA Traffic and Hung Diagnostics

Nodes need no additional EFA metrics exporter. EFA instances such as p5en are read directly from sysfs counters by
`gpu-fault-host-collector`. Before enabling the Node Agent the
diagnostic commands must be installed:

```bash
sudo dnf install -y strace iproute procps-ng coreutils rdma-core ethtool
command -v strace ss ps timeout rdma ethtool
```

Ubuntu nodes use:

```bash
sudo apt-get update
sudo apt-get install -y strace iproute2 procps coreutils rdma-core ethtool
```

The Node Installer only allows installing the binary wheel `py-spy==0.4.1` into a separate tool slot, then verifies
the pinned SHA-256 of `/opt/gpu-fault/tools/py-spy-0.4.1-<sha>/venv/bin/py-spy`. The installation Job
must be able to reach the configured Python
package index; a version drift, a source package or a binary hash mismatch all fail the installation. Verify after deployment:

```bash
sudo "$(sed -n 's/^GPU_FAULT_PYTHON_STACK_TOOL=\"\\(.*\\)\"$/\\1/p' \
  /etc/gpu-fault/node-agent.env)" --version
sudo systemctl show gpu-fault-node-agent.service -p User -p Group
cat /proc/sys/kernel/yama/ptrace_scope
```

The Agent should run as root. `ptrace_scope` or SELinux may still refuse to attach; in that case the
manifest must show an explicit error, and an empty Python stack file must not be treated as success.

Configure the control-plane thresholds before deployment:

```bash
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_MIN_ACTIVE_BPS=1048576
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_SPIKE_RATIO=4
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_DROP_RATIO=0.25
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_ZERO_BPS=1
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_ZERO_WARNING_SECONDS=60
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_ZERO_HUNG_SECONDS=600
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_PROGRESS_SUPPRESSION_ENABLED=true
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_RANK_LIVENESS_ENABLED=true
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_PROGRESS_SUPPRESSION_MAX_SECONDS=1800
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_BASELINE_ALPHA=0.2
export GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_STARTUP_GRACE_SECONDS=120
```

| Parameter | Default | Meaning |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_MIN_ACTIVE_BPS` | 1048576 | Only above this value does the attempt prove it once had effective EFA traffic |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_SPIKE_RATIO` | 4 | Reports a spike when the current throughput reaches this multiple of the EWMA baseline |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_DROP_RATIO` | 0.25 | Reports a drop when the current throughput falls below this fraction of the baseline |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_ZERO_BPS` | 1 | At or below this value counts as zero traffic |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_ZERO_WARNING_SECONDS` | 60 | Continuous zero-traffic warning time |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_ZERO_HUNG_SECONDS` | 600 | Continuous zero-traffic suspected-hung time |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_PROGRESS_SUPPRESSION_ENABLED` | true | While training progress is still advancing (step growing or a checkpoint being written), resets the zero-traffic timer to the last progress instant. Depends on application instrumentation; without instrumentation it degrades automatically to no suppression |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_RANK_LIVENESS_ENABLED` | true | Adopts the node-reported per-rank liveness (`training_rank_*`) to reset the zero-traffic timer. Depends on no application instrumentation |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_PROGRESS_SUPPRESSION_MAX_SECONDS` | 1800 | Suppression budget: from the first zero traffic the timer can be pushed back by at most this long; beyond it, escalation proceeds as usual regardless of "progress" |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_BASELINE_ALPHA` | 0.2 | EWMA new-sample weight, range `(0,1]` |
| `GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_STARTUP_GRACE_SECONDS` | 120 | No zero-traffic judgement in the attempt's initial phase |

`ZERO_HUNG_SECONDS` must be greater than or equal to `ZERO_WARNING_SECONDS`. For communication-sparse models,
raise both durations according to the longest communication-free period of a normal step; do not lower them to a few seconds
in pursuit of faster recovery.

### Per-Rank Liveness Decision Before Escalation (Independent of Application Instrumentation)

Zero traffic alone cannot distinguish a wedged collective from checkpoint writing or graph compilation, and
`TrainingProgressCollector` requires the training script itself to write the progress file -- in multi-node torchrun jobs
usually only individual ranks write logs, so it cannot be the sole per-node/per-rank basis. The Host
Collector therefore reads three procfs files for every GPU compute process on this node in every collection period (default 15 s),
and the node side directly answers "how long ago was rank progress last seen":

| Parameter | Default | Meaning |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `GPU_FAULT_RANK_LIVENESS_ENABLED` | true | Node-side per-rank probe switch |
| `GPU_FAULT_RANK_PROGRESS_MIN_WRITE_BPS` | 1048576 | A `max(write_bytes, wchar)` rate reaching this value counts as the rank advancing |
| `GPU_FAULT_RANK_PROGRESS_MIN_CPU_CORES` | 0.5 | While the GPU is idle, host CPU usage reaching this many cores counts as advancing |
| `GPU_FAULT_RANK_PROGRESS_GPU_IDLE_PERCENT` | 5 | The GPU counts as idle only when the node's maximum GPU utilisation does not exceed this value |

Basis of the criteria:

- **checkpoint**: `write_bytes` counts only block device writes; writing to FSx / streaming upload to S3 shows only in
  `wchar`, so the larger of the two is taken. Log flushing is orders of magnitude below 1 MiB/s and is not misjudged.
- **graph compilation / dataloader stall**: host CPU saturated while the GPU is idle. A stuck collective cannot
  look like this -- its spin kernel occupies the SMs and GPU utilisation is non-zero.
- **CPU busy alone does not count as progress**: NCCL waiting itself spins, so the CPU criterion must hold
  together with GPU idle.
- The reported `training_rank_write_syscalls_delta` and
  `training_rank_voluntary_ctxt_switches_delta` serve only as evidence and take no part in the decision: during a hang
  watchdog logging drives them up too.

The control plane converts the node-reported `training_rank_seconds_since_progress` into a measured progress instant
and resets the zero-traffic timer from it; the
`diagnostic_parameters.rank_liveness.status` of a `HUNG_SUSPECTED` finding records the decision source of this escalation:

| status | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `stalled` | The node confirmed no rank is advancing; the escalation was confirmed by the probe |
| `advancing` | Some rank is advancing (usually no escalation at this point, unless the suppression budget is exhausted) |
| `unavailable` | The node reported nothing (no GPU process / no nvidia-smi / no baseline in the first collection period); **escalates as usual**, never silenced by a missing probe |
| `disabled` | Turned off with `RANK_LIVENESS_ENABLED=false` |

Suppression must have a budget: `PROGRESS_SUPPRESSION_MAX_SECONDS` guarantees that even if some signal "looks like
progress" for a long time (for example a rank keeps spinning while the GPU is idle), escalation happens at the latest at
`first zero traffic + 1800 s + ZERO_HUNG_SECONDS`, so the detector is never permanently muted.

## Configuring Host Resource Persistent Hazard Monitoring

The Host Collector needs no additional exporter. Configure the control-plane thresholds before deployment:

```bash
export GPU_FAULT_LOW_UTILIZATION_THRESHOLD_PERCENT=5
export GPU_FAULT_LOW_UTILIZATION_DURATION_SECONDS=300
export GPU_FAULT_MEMORY_AVAILABLE_WARNING_PERCENT=5
export GPU_FAULT_MEMORY_PRESSURE_DURATION_SECONDS=60
export GPU_FAULT_PAGE_CACHE_WARNING_PERCENT=90
export GPU_FAULT_PAGE_CACHE_DURATION_SECONDS=300
export GPU_FAULT_LOCAL_FILESYSTEM_WARNING_PERCENT=90
export GPU_FAULT_LOCAL_FILESYSTEM_DURATION_SECONDS=60
```

| Parameter | Default | Meaning |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `GPU_FAULT_LOW_UTILIZATION_THRESHOLD_PERCENT` | 5 | Upper bound of low CPU or single-GPU utilisation on an ACTIVE training node |
| `GPU_FAULT_LOW_UTILIZATION_DURATION_SECONDS` | 300 | Continuous low-utilisation duration |
| `GPU_FAULT_HOST_NOTIFICATION_COOLDOWN_SECONDS` | 3600 | Email cooldown for the same node, job and signal |
| `GPU_FAULT_MEMORY_AVAILABLE_WARNING_PERCENT` | 5 | Lower alert bound of `MemAvailable/MemTotal` |
| `GPU_FAULT_MEMORY_PRESSURE_DURATION_SECONDS` | 60 | Continuous low-available-memory duration |
| `GPU_FAULT_PAGE_CACHE_WARNING_PERCENT` | 90 | Lower alert bound of page cache as a share of physical memory |
| `GPU_FAULT_PAGE_CACHE_DURATION_SECONDS` | 300 | Continuous high page cache duration |
| `GPU_FAULT_LOCAL_FILESYSTEM_WARNING_PERCENT` | 90 | Lower alert bound of local filesystem usage |
| `GPU_FAULT_LOCAL_FILESYSTEM_DURATION_SECONDS` | 60 | Continuous high disk usage duration |

Percentages allow `0..100`, durations at least 15 seconds. Regional sites write these values into the source Manifests,
render them via the renderer into the role ConfigMaps (`regional/generated/gpu-fault-*-config-core.yaml`) and release them with the checked
release; the values are validated during rendering and by the startup guard; do not override production values with the deployment shell environment:

```bash
make PYTHON=.venv/bin/python deployment-contracts-update
# Build, verify signatures and release per section 4.
gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault
```

The old single-cluster path writes the same set of variables into the control-plane Deployment with `deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh deploy` (Appendix A).

Low CPU/GPU triggers only when the node is associated with an ACTIVE training job. Low memory, high page cache and high local disk
usage do not require a training job. The event action is fixed as `RUN_DIAGNOSTICS`, and neither node nor job is restarted automatically;
the administrator email uses the `host-resource-event-zh-v1` fixed template. Findings of several GPUs of the same node in the same HostTelemetry batch
are aggregated into one email, deduplicated by cluster, workload, node, signal and
cooldown window; findings and evidence are still kept per device, without mass-sending per GPU.

Verify the Host Collector's raw reports:

```bash
sudo journalctl -u gpu-fault-host-collector.service -n 100 --no-pager
nvidia-smi --query-gpu=uuid,utilization.gpu --format=csv,noheader,nounits
awk '/MemTotal|MemFree|MemAvailable|Cached|SReclaimable|Shmem/ {print}' \
  /proc/meminfo
df -PT / /var /tmp
```

After the administrator investigates a SPIKE, if it is only confirmed as a transient traffic burst, without changing the baseline:

```bash
curl -fsS -X POST \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "X-GPU-Fault-Execution-Token: ${GPU_FAULT_EXECUTION_TOKEN}" \
  "${GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_URL}/v1/efa-traffic/admin-actions" \
  -d '{
    "cluster_id": "<cluster-id>",
    "node_id": "<node-id>",
    "job_id": "<job-id>",
    "attempt_id": "<attempt-id>",
    "event_id": "<event-id-from-email>",
    "action": "ACKNOWLEDGE_TRANSIENT",
    "operator": "<administrator>",
    "reason": "<investigation-result>"
  }'
```

If the sustained high traffic is a new normal operating phase, clear the SPIKE and adopt the latest sample as the new baseline
by changing only `action` to:

```json
{"action": "ACCEPT_NEW_BASELINE"}
```

You cannot just set the state to `NORMAL` while keeping the old baseline, or the next sample triggers SPIKE again.
The endpoint requires the Event ID in the email to still be the active SPIKE of that job/attempt on that node; an old Event ID,
wrong node or old attempt returns `409`. The endpoint uses the Event ID and action to guarantee idempotency, and saves the administrator,
reason, old/new baseline and the accepted traffic sample as `ADMIN_ACTION` evidence.

Regional sites release these parameters via the source Manifests and the role-aware renderer (rendered into
ConfigMaps such as `regional/generated/gpu-fault-api-ha-config-telemetry.yaml`):

```bash
make PYTHON=.venv/bin/python deployment-contracts-update
# Build, verify signatures and release per section 4.
gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault
```

The old single-cluster path uses `RUN_E2E=false deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh deploy` (Appendix A).

When verifying configuration and collection, check the thresholds from the actual role ConfigMap: the thresholds of the regional ingress are injected via the six
ConfigMaps of `envFrom`, the Deployment's `.env` holds only Secret/valueFrom items, so do not guess values from the Deployment's Secret
references:

```bash
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n gpu-fault-system \
  get configmap gpu-fault-api-ha-config-telemetry -o yaml |
  grep GPU_FAULT_EFA_TRAFFIC_

sudo systemctl status gpu-fault-host-collector.service
sudo journalctl -u gpu-fault-host-collector.service -n 100 --no-pager

for f in /sys/class/infiniband/*/ports/1/hw_counters/{rx_bytes,tx_bytes}; do
  printf '%s=' "$f"
  cat "$f"
done
```

If a training job needs fast NCCL hang localisation, the training container must inject:

```yaml
env:
- name: TORCH_NCCL_TRACE_BUFFER_SIZE
  value: "20000"
- name: TORCH_NCCL_DEBUG_INFO_PIPE_FILE
  value: /tmp/torch_nccl_debug_pipe_
- name: TORCH_NCCL_DUMP_ON_TIMEOUT
  value: "1"
```

`scripts/e2e/regional/manifests/training/three-node-managed-pytorchjob.yaml`
already contains this configuration, but the Runtime Profile is a placeholder; it must first be rendered with
`scripts/e2e/render_manifest.py --runtime-profile "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION}"`
before applying. Without these variables,
`COLLECT_HUNG_TRIAGE` does not fail but degrades from the flight recorder rules to a single round of py-spy and
`/proc` behaviour judgement. The Node Agent allowlist must contain `COLLECT_HUNG_TRIAGE`; that operation is
read-only and does not require enabling GPU reset, driver remediation or firmware update.

The `HUNG_SUSPECTED` workflow is fixed as:

```text
FREEZE_EVIDENCE
  └─ COLLECT_HUNG_TRIAGE (all attempt nodes, <=10s)
       ├─ COLLECT_DIAGNOSTIC_BUNDLE (culprit nodes/PIDs only)
       └─ VALIDATE_FABRIC (all attempt nodes)
```

When inspecting the workflow you must confirm:

- `dag_enabled=true`, and `dag_revision=1` when the triage is created;
- Both the bundle and fabric validation depend on the triage, and fabric validation does not depend on the bundle;
- `dag_revision` increments after the triage completes;
- The bundle parameters contain an exact `target_pids_by_node`, with
  `expand_python_cgroup_processes=false`;
- Unreachable nodes are recorded in `undetermined_nodes` and do not block the triage completing as a whole.

Diagnostic bundles live by default in `/var/lib/gpu-fault/diagnostics`; after configuring
`GPU_FAULT_DIAGNOSTIC_S3_URI=s3://<bucket>/<prefix>` they are uploaded to S3. Check that
`fault_context.diagnostic_reason` in `manifest.json` is
`EFA_TRAFFIC_HUNG_SUSPECTED`, and confirm the presence of `gpu_processes`,
`proc-*-stack-sample-NN.txt`, `python-stack-*-sample-NN.txt` and
`strace-*-sample-NN*`. Every associated Python PID should have three Python stacks `sample-01/02/03`;
Python launchers such as `torchrun` that hold no CUDA context are associated through the same
container cgroup as the GPU PID. When `py-spy` or `strace` fails on ptrace permission, the manifest records the
error and the workflow
still keeps the NVIDIA, DCGM, kernel and network evidence.

Every GPU compute process is sampled 3 times by default, rather than one 10-second collection:

```bash
export GPU_FAULT_HUNG_STRACE_SAMPLE_COUNT=3
export GPU_FAULT_HUNG_STRACE_SAMPLE_DURATION_SECONDS=3
export GPU_FAULT_HUNG_STRACE_SAMPLE_INTERVAL_SECONDS=2
export GPU_FAULT_HUNG_PYSPY_SAMPLE_COUNT=3
export GPU_FAULT_HUNG_PYSPY_SAMPLE_INTERVAL_SECONDS=2
export GPU_FAULT_HUNG_PYSPY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=10
```

The allowed ranges of the three `strace` parameters are `2..5`, `1..30` seconds and `0..30` seconds respectively; the three `py-spy`
parameters allow `3..5`, `0..30` seconds and `1..60` seconds respectively. `PYSPY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`
is only the anti-hang cap of a single command and does not make a normal `py-spy dump` wait 10 seconds. Regional sites write the values
into the source Manifests and release them via `make deployment-contracts-update` -> build release ->
`gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir` into the recovery ConfigMap
(`regional/generated/gpu-fault-*-config-recovery.yaml`); rendering and the startup guard validate the ranges;
the old single-cluster path has `deploy/hyperpod/deploy.sh deploy` validate and write them into the control-plane Deployment (Appendix A).
The control plane bakes the parameters into the signed diagnostic command, and the Node Agent additionally clamps the received values to the same ranges.

The Node Agent runs the Python stack phase first: each round samples all associated Python PIDs in parallel, running
3 rounds by default with only 2 seconds between rounds. Only afterwards does it run the `/proc/<pid>/{stack,wchan}` and `strace`
phases. Python stacks no longer share a loop with the per-PID serial 3-second `strace`. Check
`python_stack_sampling.mode=round_parallel` in `manifest.json`, and confirm that every round
includes all associated Python PIDs.

When distributed training hits `HUNG_SUSPECTED`, the control plane first collects a lightweight triage, then generates deep S3 archives only on the culprit nodes
and the node of one control rank:

```text
s3://<bucket>/<prefix>/<node-id>/gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar.gz
```

The node count and concurrency of the triage phase are controlled by two parameters:

```bash
export GPU_FAULT_HUNG_TRIAGE_MAX_NODES=8
export GPU_FAULT_NODE_ACTION_MAX_PARALLEL=16
```

| Parameter | Default | Meaning |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `GPU_FAULT_HUNG_TRIAGE_MAX_NODES` | 8 | Maximum number of nodes attached in one hung triage; `0` means unlimited |
| `GPU_FAULT_NODE_ACTION_MAX_PARALLEL` | 16 | Node-level concurrency cap of `COLLECT_HUNG_TRIAGE` / `COLLECT_DIAGNOSTIC_BUNDLE` |

The triage reads the flight recorder on every node and takes one py-spy sample per rank, briefly
pausing the training processes, hence the node cap. Sampling **always** keeps the nodes that reported zero traffic and the node with the lowest global
rank (rank 0 drives most collectives and holds the coordinator); the remaining slots are taken evenly by stride across the other nodes,
avoiding all landing in the same rack.

`MAX_NODES` is **not** for making the triage cheap: identifying the culprit is inherently a cross-rank comparison
(a minority of ranks lagging behind the majority's collective sequence number); sampling too few gives neither a comparable majority nor
protection against excluding the culprit outright. So the cap serves only as a blast-radius constraint, and the default of 8 nodes (H200/P5
at 8 cards per node is 64 ranks) is enough to support the comparison. Unsampled nodes are recorded in
`not_sampled_nodes` of the triage details, separate from the genuinely unreachable `undetermined_nodes`; the classification conclusion also
carries that list, to avoid reading "not located in this sample" as "the whole fabric is broken".

The two collect operations are read-only and tolerate single-node failures, so they are dispatched concurrently up to `MAX_PARALLEL`;
the other operations that change node state remain strictly serial -- when the first node fails, nothing may yet have been sent to the following nodes.
### Downloading and Extracting Diagnostic Bundles on Windows

A diagnostic bundle is a gzip-compressed tar archive, not a ZIP. Windows Explorer or tools that perform only one layer of extraction
may not expand it directly. The current archive may also keep the temporary creation-time file name
`gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar.gz.partial` in the gzip header; that metadata does not affect gzip/tar integrity, but some
Windows tools output a `.partial` file by the embedded name and stop extracting further because the extension is no longer
`.tar`.

Prefer the Windows built-in `tar` in PowerShell to complete both layers of unpacking at once:

```powershell
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force .\gpu-diagnostic | Out-Null
tar -xzf .\gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar.gz -C .\gpu-diagnostic
tar -tf .\gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar.gz
```

You can also extract twice with 7-Zip. If the first layer yields
`gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar.gz.partial`, rename it to `.tar` and extract again:

```powershell
Rename-Item `
  .\gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar.gz.partial `
  .\gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar
tar -xf .\gpu-diagnostic-<bundle-key>.tar -C .\gpu-diagnostic
```

When investigating an incomplete Windows download, first check the S3 object size and SHA-256. The verified
`gpu-diagnostic-6912996569bf09eb8a735545.tar.gz` example should be `11594199` bytes,
with SHA-256
`0a213550331e315658e1f489603f6daea0391a72a7eced2a9a5bdfa1a311c399`:

```powershell
(Get-Item .\gpu-diagnostic-6912996569bf09eb8a735545.tar.gz).Length
Get-FileHash `
  .\gpu-diagnostic-6912996569bf09eb8a735545.tar.gz `
  -Algorithm SHA256
```

`diagnostics/nvidia-bug-report.log.gz` inside the outer archive is a separate inner gzip file and needs to be
extracted again as needed. Do not judge that a bundle is a hung bundle from the `gpu-diagnostic-*` file name alone; check
`diagnostics/manifest.json`. A real hung-process diagnostic must contain
`fault_context.diagnostic_reason=EFA_TRAFFIC_HUNG_SUSPECTED`, plus
`python-stack-*-sample-NN.txt`, `proc-*-stack-sample-NN.txt` and
`strace-*-sample-NN*`. The `691299...` example above is a base archive format validation bundle and contains no
`py-spy/strace` process samples.

On each node, process state and command line are collected once only for the compute processes corresponding to the GPU UUIDs allocated to that attempt,
and the repeated sampling above is run for `/proc/<pid>/stack`, `wchan` and time-limited `strace`. Check
`target_gpu_uuids` and `gpu_processes` in
`manifest.json`. When collection fails on any node,
the Adapter still continues requesting the other nodes; `node_results`, `failed_nodes` and
`waiting_nodes` in the result record per-node state. Temporarily unreachable nodes keep the workflow `WAITING`, and retries do not repeat
collection already completed by the Node Agent command ledger.

The archive must also contain:

```text
rdma-link-show.txt
infiniband-counters.json
ethtool-<interface>-statistics.txt
```

`infiniband-counters.json` saves per device/port the `state`, `phys_state`,
`counters/*`, `hw_counters/*` and the associated network interface. The default sysfs root is
`/sys/class/infiniband`; non-standard test environments can set
`GPU_FAULT_INFINIBAND_ROOT`. Nodes need `rdma` and `ethtool` installed; a missing command, timeout or
permission error is written into `manifest.json` without dropping the other diagnostic files.

## Configuring Heterogeneous Node GPU/EFA ACTIVE Inventory Checks

### 1. Understand the Configuration Scope

The expected GPU/EFA counts are node-level configuration, not global control-plane parameters. The installation Job is pinned to one
Kubernetes Node, reads that node's `node.kubernetes.io/instance-type`, then writes the instance type and
expected counts into that node's `/etc/gpu-fault/collector.env`. So one control plane can handle at the same time different training jobs
running on P5, P5e, P5en, P6-B200 and P6-B300.

The current AWS mapping comes from EC2 `DescribeInstanceTypes`:

| Kubernetes/EC2 instance type | GPU | Expected ACTIVE EFA count |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| `p5.4xlarge` / `ml.p5.4xlarge` | 1 | 1 |
| `p5.48xlarge` / `ml.p5.48xlarge` | 8 | 32 |
| `p5e.48xlarge` / `ml.p5e.48xlarge` | 8 | 32 |
| `p5en.48xlarge` / `ml.p5en.48xlarge` | 8 | 16 |
| `p6-b200.48xlarge` / `ml.p6-b200.48xlarge` | 8 | 8 |
| `p6-b300.48xlarge` / `ml.p6-b300.48xlarge` | 8 | 16 |

P6-B300's AWS `NetworkCards` is 17, but `MaximumEfaInterfaces` is 16; do not count the
dedicated ENA card into the `/sys/class/infiniband` EFA expectation. For an unknown instance type without both GPU and EFA values given explicitly,
the HyperPod node installation fails rather than silently turning off the check.

### 2. Check Node Labels Before Deployment

```bash
kubectl get nodes \
  -L node.kubernetes.io/instance-type \
  -L sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name
```

Spot-check one healthy node per instance type:

```bash
NODE='<kubernetes-node-name>'
kubectl debug "node/${NODE}" -it --image=public.ecr.aws/amazonlinux/amazonlinux:2023 \
  -- chroot /host bash

nvidia-smi --query-gpu=uuid,name --format=csv,noheader
find /sys/class/infiniband -mindepth 1 -maxdepth 1 -printf '%f\n'
for port in /sys/class/infiniband/*/ports/*; do
  printf '%s state=' "$port"
  cat "$port/state"
  printf '%s phys_state=' "$port"
  cat "$port/phys_state"
done
```

The Collector also requires `/sys/class/infiniband/<device>/device/driver` to point at the `efa` driver,
or `device/uevent` to contain `DRIVER=efa`. Other RDMA NICs such as mlx5 are not counted. Once confirmed as EFA,
at least one port must be both `4: ACTIVE` and `5: LinkUp` to count as ACTIVE. Exit the debug
shell before running the installation.

### 3. Configuration Parameters

| Environment variable | Node installer parameter | Default | Meaning |
| --- | --- | ---: | --- |
| `GPU_FAULT_NODE_INSTANCE_TYPE` | `--node-instance-type` | Read automatically on HyperPod | Instance type written into metrics and emails |
| `GPU_FAULT_EXPECTED_GPU_COUNT` | `--expected-gpu-count` | Per the table above | Expected number of visible GPU UUIDs |
| `GPU_FAULT_EXPECTED_EFA_DEVICE_COUNT` | `--expected-efa-device-count` | Per the table above | Expected number of ACTIVE EFA devices |
| `GPU_FAULT_INVENTORY_MISMATCH_CONSECUTIVE_SAMPLES` | `--inventory-mismatch-samples` | 2 | How many consecutive abnormal rounds trigger |
| `GPU_FAULT_HOST_INTERVAL_SECONDS` | `--host-interval` | 15 | Host Collector period (seconds) |

By default it triggers only after about 30 seconds of continuous mismatch. Production may raise the consecutive
round count according to driver initialisation and EFA link convergence time; setting it to 1 is not recommended.

### 4. Deploying for Heterogeneous HyperPod Nodes

Do not export one global set of `GPU_FAULT_EXPECTED_*` on a heterogeneous cluster and reuse it for all nodes. Let the installation script
read the labels node by node and choose the mapping:

```bash
for node in $(kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" get nodes \
  -l sagemaker.amazonaws.com/cluster-name="${HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME}" \
  -o name | cut -d/ -f2); do
  GPU_FAULT_KUBECTL_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  GPU_FAULT_CONNECTION_MODE=regional \
  GPU_FAULT_REGIONAL_CONNECTION_SECRET=gpu-fault-regional-connection \
  GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" \
  GPU_FAULT_INVENTORY_MISMATCH_CONSECUTIVE_SAMPLES=2 \
    deploy/node/run-hyperpod-installer-job.sh --node "$node"
done
```

`regional` mode does not look up the decommissioned `gpu-fault-api-canary`
ClusterIP in the GPU EKS. The installation Job reads the NLB URL,
per-cluster token, private CA, cluster ID and node action secret only through the `gpu-fault-regional-connection` Secret; Secret values are not written into the generated
Job YAML.

Control-plane liveness uses `GET /livez`: it asserts only process-local state (the event loop is answering,
the spool-worker's consumer thread is still alive, the worker role's `gpu-fault-processor-inbox` consumer loop is still alive --
when the coordinator's `processor_consumer_running` has exited, or the age of the latest round exceeds
`max(30 s, 6 × GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_NOTIFICATION_FALLBACK_SECONDS)`, it lists
`processor-consumer` in `dead_threads` and returns 503; before the first round it is treated as alive), does not read registry freshness,
and makes no Aurora round trip;
readiness continues to use `/healthz` (including registry freshness, `GPU_FAULT_REGISTRY_STALE_SECONDS`
production value 90 s, covering one Aurora writer failover). So during a failover the Pod is briefly NotReady,
but is not restarted into a process that again needs Aurora to start; at startup the registry bootstrap also retries with exponential backoff within the
`GPU_FAULT_STARTUP_STORE_RETRY_SECONDS` (default 120 s) budget.
Although the synchronous Store calls of business endpoints have been isolated from the event loop,
Store I/O saturation or downstream timeouts should not cause several NLB targets to be killed by liveness at the same time.
Because the container needs to install dependencies from the wheel at startup, a TCP `startupProbe` must also be configured (currently waiting up to
10 minutes); before startup succeeds Kubernetes runs no readiness/liveness, avoiding repeatedly restarting a container still
installing dependencies.
A normal role-split release does not passively wait the full 10 minutes: the total rollout window per role is 5 minutes, during which the candidate release Pod logs are checked every 5 seconds.
An unknown `GPU_FAULT_*` variable, a regional registry lacking Agent CIDRs or
a registry schema incompatible with the target wheel immediately terminates that role's rollout and enters the formal rollback; only a slow installation without a deterministic
startup error keeps using the probe budget.

The current generated three-tier topology uses ingress `3 × 4` uvicorn processes, control-worker
`6 × 4` processes, and the spool-worker at 0 replicas by default with 1 process per Pod. ADOT scrapes Pods, and
the `/metrics` of a multi-process Pod is aggregated by the application inside the Pod: every process writes complete samples to
`/dev/shm/gpu-fault-process-metrics/<POD_UID>/`, the process answering the scrape merges the samples of all live processes and
outputs them per the policy table of `gpu_fault.app.metric_aggregation` (SUM/MAX/MIN/ANY/PER_PROCESS);
`gpu_fault_metrics_aggregation_degraded 1` means that Pod has degraded to single-process readings
(Detailed Design v2 §/metrics). All business endpoints that may access the synchronous
Aurora/store, hold locks or perform blocking external I/O are executed by a separate bounded store I/O
thread pool; when the pool saturates it returns `503 Retry-After` (`detail` is `store I/O capacity exceeded`
or `request deadline exceeded`, and `Retry-After` is taken from
`GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_RETRY_AFTER_SECONDS` like 429). Requests whose JSON body contains a `\u0000` escape are rejected with 422 at decode time
and counted in `gpu_fault_ingress_decode_rejections_total{reason="nul"}` -- PostgreSQL `jsonb`
cannot hold NUL, and putting it into the spool would drag down the other clusters of the same stripe; the collector sink treats 422 as final and
dead-letters instead of replaying. The lease owner of the processor and XID coordinator
uses `POD_UID:PID`.

The current resource configuration is: ingress `requests 4 CPU/4Gi, limits 8 CPU/8Gi`, control-worker
`requests 3 CPU/4Gi, limits 4 CPU/6Gi`, spool-worker `requests 1 CPU/2Gi,
limits 4 CPU/4Gi`. These values come from
`deploy/control-plane/regional/generated/`; when adjusting you must re-render and validate with
`config_cli validate` and the role-split verifier. `config_cli validate` also checks every `GPU_FAULT_*` resolved from each Deployment against the runtime
environment variable inventory (`src/gpu_fault/data/env-inventory.json`): names not in the inventory, empty values of boolean switches or invalid tokens all fail at render time --
these are exactly the errors the Pod startup validation would refuse and which would otherwise surface only as a crash loop mid-rollout; a new environment variable
must have the inventory regenerated before the manifests are rendered. After go-live keep observing the working set,
OOM restart count, event-loop lag and queue backlog; do not mask unbounded growth by adding processes or memory.

The root cause of the single-worker OOM above was not the normal uvicorn baseline but the old PostgreSQL store's
processor hot path repeatedly reading and deserialising all historical requests, including completed large GPU telemetry
payloads. The current implementation uses a dedicated `gpu_fault_processor_queue` table with normalised state, cluster, ordering key, priority, lease and time
columns; claims read only the actually claimed requests via indexes,
`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` and in-database lane deduplication. During a mixed old/new rollout
it also writes the old JSONB objects and reconciles by `updated_at`; after the upgrade completes, the Pod holding the separate
`processor-cleanup` periodic task lease periodically cleans a bounded number of
`COMPLETED` records older than 10 minutes, no longer scanning the history table on every completion.

Collector requests return `202 Accepted` immediately after passing authentication, admission and durable enqueue, no longer polling Aurora
every 100 ms for the processing result; control requests such as XID/SXID and workflow that need response content
still wait synchronously. When a collector receives 429/5xx it honours both `Retry-After` and exponential backoff, and uses
full jitter to avoid many nodes retrying at the same instant.

Every **uvicorn process** uses a separate PostgreSQL connection pool; store I/O threads check out connections from the pool when executing transactions,
and all cursors of the same transaction are pinned to the same connection. The connection cap is
`POOL_MAX_SIZE × --workers × replicas`, not `POOL_MAX_SIZE × replicas`.
The current steady-state formula is:

```text
ingress pool       = 40 × 4 × 3 = 480
control-worker pool= 24 × 4 × 6 = 576
worker LISTEN      =  1 × 4 × 6 =  24
spool-worker       =  0
steady ceiling                    1080
```

Comparing pools alone gives `1056`; the capacity gate must also include the separate PostgreSQL
LISTEN connection of every worker process. When rolling ingress, `maxSurge: 1` adds another `40 × 4 = 160`, so the current worst-case transient
cap is `1240`; when the spool-worker is enabled its pool and listener must be added too. Before go-live query the real database cap from a running Pod
instead of freezing an old value of some environment in the document:

```bash
CPU_API_POD="$(
  kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
    get pod -l app=gpu-fault-api-ha \
    -o jsonpath='{.items[0].metadata.name}'
)"
kubectl --kubeconfig "${CPU_KUBECONFIG}" -n "${NAMESPACE}" \
  exec "${CPU_API_POD}" -- python -c \
  'import os,psycopg; c=psycopg.connect(os.environ["GPU_FAULT_STORE_URL"]); print(c.execute("SHOW max_connections").fetchone()[0]); c.close()'
```

The result must cover the steady ceiling, rolling surge, schema/admin connections and the operations reserve.
`GPU_FAULT_POSTGRES_FLEET_CONNECTION_BUDGET` is a deployment-side protective budget and does not equal the database's actual
`max_connections`. Pools grow on demand, and a measured connection count below the ceiling cannot replace the upper-bound accounting.

**The processor's thread pools and `PROCESSOR_WORKERS` exist only in the tier that
consumes the queue** (`SERVICE_ROLE=all|worker`): only `run_processor` creates them,
and with `SERVICE_ROLE=ingress` that thread never starts, so do not add these thread counts
into the ingress thread or connection budget, and `generated/gpu-fault-api-ha-ingress.yaml` no longer carries
these five variables (`verify_control_plane_role_split.py` fails the deployment when they reappear on ingress).
`POOL_MAX_SIZE` should not be less than
`GPU_FAULT_STORE_IO_WORKERS`, otherwise the concurrency cap lands on checkout, presenting as
`PoolTimeout` rather than readable backpressure; a warning is printed at startup. After the control-plane review of 2026-09-08
(F-E5 / G-7) this estimate is computed by **the thread families that actually exist for that role**: each Store I/O pool + the processor claim loop
and each sub-pool worker (by the number `ProcessorPoolSettings` actually starts, not the `PROCESSOR_WORKERS` budget) +
dispatcher workers + 1 each for periodic services, XID correlation, notification dispatch, collector snapshot, regional registry refresh + spool replay
threads, requiring `demand + 2 ≤ POOL_MAX_SIZE` (the two spare for registry refresh, `/metrics` and per-request renewal threads);
LISTEN connections are outside the pool and counted separately. The ratio is exported as `gpu_fault_postgres_pool_oversubscription_ratio`;
it is a ratio of configuration constants with no alert -- read only on the Control-plane capacity dashboard (together with the per-consumer
demand breakdown), and one WARNING is logged at startup; actual queueing is seen in
`GpuFaultPostgresPoolCheckoutQueueing` (`requests_waiting`), lost slots in
`GpuFaultPostgresPoolConnectionErrors`.
`GPU_FAULT_POSTGRES_POOL_MAX_IDLE_SECONDS` lets connections grown during a stress test fall back to
`POOL_MIN_SIZE`, and `..._MAX_LIFETIME_SECONDS` also reclaims connections still pointing at the old
writer after an Aurora failover.

After the old hot-path fix was deployed, the three real control-plane Pods ran continuously for over 36 minutes with no restarts, and the working
set dropped from about 4.9--5.7Gi to 126--189Mi. 4Gi/8Gi is still kept as traffic peak and diagnostic
headroom; the limit should not be shrunk immediately based on that idle baseline. The dedicated queue table, connection pool and 202 path must also pass
capacity tests against the target Aurora and NLB before large-scale production validation can be declared complete.

The Aurora isolated-schema smoke should verify concurrent claims, same-cluster lane serialisation, cross-cluster parallelism,
fencing, statistics and cleanup, and clean up its dedicated schema. But the current
`scripts/e2e/regional/manifests/postgres-processor-queue-smoke.yaml` still depends on an uncompressed wheel, runs
`pip install` at startup, and lacks the RDS CA mount required by `verify-full`, so it cannot be applied directly to the current release.
Signed OCI, schema ensure and dependency pins must not be bypassed by ad hoc decompression with online dependency installation or relaxed TLS.
Until this old Job is adapted to the same release's pre-installed CPU image, CA, separate schema initialisation and the bound cleanup flow,
and passes review, it should be recorded as `NOT_RUN`; a local isolated PostgreSQL test cannot replace real Aurora acceptance.
The following only demonstrates the existing render entry point and creates no Job:

```bash
python3 scripts/e2e/render_manifest.py \
  scripts/e2e/regional/manifests/postgres-processor-queue-smoke.yaml \
  --wheel-configmap gpu-fault-control-plane-wheel-review-only \
  -o /tmp/gpu-fault-postgres-processor-queue-smoke.yaml
```

A valid smoke run under approval must have complete proof of success and cleanup; it must not be released on an output string alone.
The read-only capacity verification of each GPU EKS must also be approved separately, using the capacity
probe Job below; it uses the NLB URL and private CA from `gpu-fault-regional-connection` and reaches `/metrics` from the real GPU
VPC/NAT egress. That path passes through the NLB, the API bounded executor, the PostgreSQL
pool and queries the Aurora processor queue, creating no incident:

```bash
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n gpu-fault-system \
  create configmap gpu-fault-control-plane-capacity-probe \
  --from-file=control_plane_capacity_probe.py=scripts/perf/control_plane_capacity_probe.py \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml |
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" apply -f -
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n gpu-fault-system \
  delete job gpu-fault-control-plane-capacity-probe \
  --ignore-not-found
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
  apply -f scripts/e2e/regional/manifests/control-plane-capacity-probe.yaml
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n gpu-fault-system \
  wait --for=condition=complete \
  job/gpu-fault-control-plane-capacity-probe --timeout=10m
kubectl --context "${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" -n gpu-fault-system \
  logs job/gpu-fault-control-plane-capacity-probe
```

Run `concurrency=1,8,16,32,64` level by level, each level for at least 5 minutes; observe the output
requests/second, p50/p95/p99, and at the same time check `gpu_fault_store_io_*`,
`gpu_fault_processor_*`, Aurora ACU, connection count, CPU, memory and NLB target response time.
Any 429/503, transport error, p95 exceeding the site SLO, continuously growing queue or Aurora connection exhaustion
counts as a failure. The production capacity conclusion must record the test date, control-plane version, GPU cluster/node scale, concurrency,
request mix and Aurora min/max ACU; it must not keep only one idle `/metrics` read-pressure result.

For a new instance type not yet in the built-in table, both explicit values must be given:

```bash
GPU_FAULT_EXPECTED_GPU_COUNT='<gpu-count>' \
GPU_FAULT_EXPECTED_EFA_DEVICE_COUNT='<maximum-efa-interfaces>' \
GPU_FAULT_INVENTORY_MISMATCH_CONSECUTIVE_SAMPLES=3 \
GPU_FAULT_KUBECTL_CONTEXT="${GPU_EKS_CONTEXT}" \
GPU_FAULT_CONNECTION_MODE=regional \
GPU_FAULT_REGIONAL_CONNECTION_SECRET=gpu-fault-regional-connection \
GPU_FAULT_INSTALLER_ARTIFACT_SHA256="${REQUIRED_AGENT_ARTIFACT_SHA256}" \
deploy/node/run-hyperpod-installer-job.sh --node <node-name>
```

When installing directly on the node:

```bash
sudo deploy/node/install-gpu-fault-collector.sh \
  --control-plane-url "${GPU_FAULT_CONTROL_PLANE_URL}" \
  --cluster-id "${GPU_FAULT_CLUSTER_ID}" \
  --runtime-profile-version "${RUNTIME_PROFILE_VERSION:?}" \
  --node-instance-type <instance-type> \
  --expected-gpu-count <gpu-count> \
  --expected-efa-device-count <maximum-efa-interfaces> \
  --inventory-mismatch-samples 2
```

### 5. Verify the Final Configuration of Every Node

```bash
sudo grep -E \
  'GPU_FAULT_NODE_INSTANCE_TYPE|GPU_FAULT_EXPECTED_(GPU|EFA_DEVICE)_COUNT|GPU_FAULT_INVENTORY_MISMATCH' \
  /etc/gpu-fault/collector.env
sudo systemctl restart gpu-fault-host-collector.service
sudo systemctl status gpu-fault-host-collector.service
sudo journalctl -u gpu-fault-host-collector.service -n 100 --no-pager
```

A healthy node keeps reporting expected, active, missing, excess and mismatch. If the
`nvidia-smi` query fails, the batch's `collection_errors` records the error, but the GPU count is not
fabricated as 0; the EFA inventory is still collected independently.

### 6. Control-Plane Behaviour After an Anomaly

1. A lost GPU reports `gpu_inventory_mismatch=1`. EFA separately reports the PCI discovered,
   driver bound and ACTIVE/LinkUp counts and `failure_mode`; the cluster-level
   `KubernetesNodeResourceCollector` additionally monitors `vpc.amazonaws.com/efa` and
   `nvidia.com/gpu` allocatable.
2. The control plane isolates state by cluster, node and metric, generating a CRITICAL finding and incident.
3. SES sends the email with the fixed `hardware-inventory-mismatch-zh-v1` template. The body contains the instance
   type, expected, ACTIVE, discovered, missing/excess, node, workload and workflow.
4. A missing EFA PCI executes `RESTART_NODE`; an unbound driver executes
   `STOP_WORKLOADS -> REMEDIATE_EFA_DRIVER -> VALIDATE_FABRIC`; a port merely inactive first
   diagnoses automatically and reboots only if validation still fails; a missing Kubernetes allocatable alone restarts the target node's
   EFA device-plugin Pod.
5. `STOP_WORKLOADS` and `RESTART_WORKLOAD` appear only when the finding is associated with a workload.
6. After the reboot confirm the new boot/Agent, the node `Running`, and wait the default 60-second settle period; then accept only
   new HostTelemetry from after the reboot completed, comparing GPU ACTIVE with expected GPU and EFA
   ACTIVE with expected EFA at the same time.
7. If any card is still missing, validation fails and escalates automatically to `REPLACE_NODE`. Replacement uses only a healthy
   warm spare of the same specification, verifying again after replacement; when spares are insufficient the workflow is blocked.

Check the layered state:

```bash
NODE='<node>'
kubectl debug "node/${NODE}" -it \
  --image=public.ecr.aws/amazonlinux/amazonlinux:2023 \
  --profile=sysadmin -- chroot /host bash

lspci -Dnnd 1d0f:
find /sys/class/infiniband -mindepth 1 -maxdepth 1 -printf '%f\n'
for d in /sys/class/infiniband/*; do
  printf '%s driver=%s\n' "$d" \
    "$(basename "$(readlink -f "$d/device/driver")")"
  cat "$d"/ports/*/state "$d"/ports/*/phys_state
done
kubectl get node "${NODE}" \
  -o jsonpath='{.status.allocatable["vpc.amazonaws.com/efa"]}{"\n"}'
```

Do not confuse `REMEDIATE_DRIVER` with EFA recovery: the former is NVIDIA GPU driver remediation;
EFA uses the separate `REMEDIATE_EFA_DRIVER`. When the PCI function has disappeared, repeatedly reloading the driver is forbidden;
go straight into the reboot/replace chain.

The post-reboot inventory gate above applies to all `RESTART_NODE` workflows, not only incidents triggered by an
inventory mismatch. The same check runs after a reboot triggered by XID, SXID or DCGM.

Emails depend on the SES sender, recipients, region and identity of section 8. Destructive workflows
also require the Runtime Profile, operation allowlist, HyperPod Adapter, healthy spares and
restart budget to all be satisfied. A successfully sent email does not mean the node replacement necessarily succeeded.

Do not perform acceptance on a production active control plane by deliberately setting a wrong expected count; that really triggers isolation
and replacement. Fault injection should first run on a simulation/canary control plane, then on dedicated nodes in a maintenance window.
## Configuration Management and Pre-Go-Live Validation

Non-sensitive control-plane configuration is no longer inlined directly into Deployments. The role manifests load domain-split ConfigMaps via
`envFrom`:

- `core`
- `postgres`
- `processor`
- `telemetry`
- `notification`
- `recovery`

Secrets, tokens, passwords and dynamic release pins continue to use explicit
`valueFrom` and must not be moved into ConfigMaps. Before changing configuration, confirm whether the target item is currently
`value`, `envFrom` or `valueFrom`; a same-named configuration appearing twice is refused by the validator.

The complete environment variable reference table used by the code is generated by:

```bash
python3 scripts/generate-env-reference.py
```

The generated result is in `docs/环境变量参考.md` (Environment Variables Reference). CI runs `--check` and fails outright when the code adds a
`GPU_FAULT_*` configuration without updating the reference table.

The same generator also maintains the in-package `gpu_fault/data/env-inventory.json`. At startup the API, Node Agent,
Cluster Executor, Collector and Completion Watcher enumerate the `GPU_FAULT_*` in the actual environment;
unknown names fail closed by default, preventing misspellings from silently falling back to defaults.
`GPU_FAULT_UNKNOWN_ENV_POLICY=warn|ignore` is allowed only for a controlled migration window and must be removed in steady state.

Besides names, `value_kinds` in the inventory also records each variable's value type. The types are not maintained by hand:
the generator infers them from the conversion the code actually applies to the variable (`int(...)` -> integer, `float(...)` -> number,
comparison with `0/1/true/false/yes/no/on/off` -> boolean); variables with more than one reading or caught by `except ValueError`
stay untyped and unvalidated, so the inference can only miss, never misjudge a type as unusable in production.
The five processes above validate these values at startup, **unaffected by `GPU_FAULT_UNKNOWN_ENV_POLICY`**:
integers/numbers must parse; switches must be one of the eight words above, and must be an enabling word that switch itself recognises --
a switch that only recognises `true` written as `yes` fails startup instead of silently staying off (this kind of misspelling used to
be discovered only when a recovery action was due and did not execute). `no`/`0`/`off` are always accepted, because for every reading
they mean off. An empty value equals unconfigured, and every reading falls back to its own default.
The error message contains only the variable name, type and expected word list, never echoing the value, because some variables carry tokens.

Before deployment you must run:

```bash
PYTHONPATH=src python3 -m gpu_fault.config_cli validate \
  deploy/control-plane/regional/generated
```

The validator checks:

- Sensitive configuration must use `valueFrom`
- `value` and `valueFrom` must not coexist
- preStop, Uvicorn, lifespan and Pod termination budgets
- processor valid request and exit budgets
- ingress/worker/spool role configuration boundaries
- agent artifact/config pin declarations
- The fleet-wide PostgreSQL connection budget `pool max × workers × replicas`

### Production Manifest Modification and Auto-Generation

**This section is only for developers who modify this solution's source code.** Administrators and customers use the already built, tested and released
version; they neither write solution Manifests, nor modify generated/ or the cleanup inventory, nor apply
resources in `deploy/control-plane/` and `deploy/dataplane/` one by one.

Developers should first read [Developer Deployment Implementation](developer-deployment-implementation.md); this section keeps the complete annotation,
renderer and generated-output details as implementation reference.

| Role | What to maintain | What not to maintain |
|---|---|---|
| Solution developer | Component source code, source Manifests, lifecycle annotations, generators/renderer, tests | The runtime installed-resource ConfigMap |
| Administrator/customer | `site.yaml`, Secrets, IAM/AWS resource inputs and release approval | Solution Deployment/DaemonSet/RBAC/PDB/Service YAML |

"Not maintaining the resource registry" does not mean "not writing Manifests". Developers still fully define the component's image,
command/args, ports, resources, probes, security context, ServiceAccount/RBAC and configuration mounts;
they just no longer copy kind/name into a second registry. For running
Deployments/DaemonSets/CronJobs, developers additionally declare `cleanup-phase` and
`cleanup-order` so the automation knows the dependency order. The resource kind, name and namespace/cluster scope
are derived by the generator from the YAML, and whether it is actually installed is written into the runtime
ConfigMap from the post-deployment `kubectl get` result.

The source files of production Manifests fall into two classes:
- The GPU data plane directly maintains
  `deploy/dataplane/*.yaml` and
  `deploy/dataplane/optional/*.yaml`;
- The CPU control plane modifies
  `deploy/control-plane/base/control-plane-deployment.yaml`,
  `deploy/control-plane/regional/*.yaml` or
  `render_control_plane_role_split.py`. `deploy/control-plane/regional/generated/`
  must not be modified directly.

#### Administrator/Customer Boundary: Do Not Write Solution Manifests

When deploying an existing release, administrators and customers only prepare the existing cluster ARNs, state-dir, administrator email and controlled AWS
identity, then run the unified entry point:

```bash
gpu-fault-admin deploy \
  --cpu-cluster-arn <cpu-arn> \
  --gpu-cluster-arn <gpu-arn> \
  --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault \
  --admin-email <operations-email>
```

The first deployment uses that command; later upgrades need only `gpu-fault-admin deploy --state-dir /secure/gpu-fault`,
which automatically chooses bootstrap or upgrade from the internal site and the regional release state. Resume is rerunning as is, rollback is
the same command plus `--rollback`, joining a cluster is the same command with a new `--gpu-cluster-arn` (or the alias
`join-cluster`), and removal uses `remove-cluster --gpu-cluster-arn`; no path requires the administrator to touch
the low-level release JSON or the regional rollout scripts.

If that command generates a Profile plan and stops, the administrator reruns the same deploy once with
`--approve-profile-plan <plan_sha256> --reference <change ticket>`; the internal
release scripts are not called directly to inject the approval.

Administrators/customers must not perform the following to "patch in a component":

```text
kubectl apply -f deploy/dataplane/<some file>
Editing deploy/control-plane/regional/generated/ directly
Editing deploy/control-plane/regional/cleanup-inventory.json directly
Online kubectl edit of this solution's Deployments/DaemonSets/RBAC
```

These operations bypass the release pin, role split, runtime resource registration and rollback records. When a new solution component is needed,
a developer should submit the source change and publish a new release, and the administrator then deploys it through `upgrade`.

The customer's own training job YAML is an explicit exception. Customers may maintain their own PyTorchJob/Job/JobSet,
and then onboard it to this solution via `gpu-training-submit` or `gpu-fault-workload-annotate`; that YAML
is a business workload and not a "solution production Manifest" in the sense of this section.

#### Example: Adding a GPU PCIe Collector

For example, add the source file `pcie-collector.yaml` under `deploy/dataplane/`. Running
Deployments/DaemonSets/CronJobs must declare the lifecycle in top-level `metadata.annotations`:

```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
  namespace: gpu-fault-system
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRole
metadata:
  name: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
rules:
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["nodes"]
    verbs: ["get", "list"]
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRoleBinding
metadata:
  name: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
roleRef:
  apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
  kind: ClusterRole
  name: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
subjects:
  - kind: ServiceAccount
    name: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
    namespace: gpu-fault-system
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
  namespace: gpu-fault-system
  annotations:
    gpu-fault.io/cleanup-phase: producer
    gpu-fault.io/cleanup-order: "35"
    gpu-fault.io/deploy-mode: regional_rollout
spec:
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: gpu-fault-pcie-collector
      containers:
        - name: collector
          image: public.ecr.aws/docker/library/python:3.12-slim
          command: ["gpu-fault-collector"]
          args: ["pcie"]
          resources:
            requests:
              cpu: 50m
              memory: 128Mi
            limits:
              cpu: 500m
              memory: 512Mi
```

Annotation semantics:

| annotation | Values | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `gpu-fault.io/cleanup-phase` | CPU: `ingress/consumer/auxiliary`; GPU: `producer/executor` | Determines the stop order; required for running resources |
| `gpu-fault.io/cleanup-order` | Non-negative integer | Order within the same phase; the Installer/Reconciler should precede Collectors that report data |
| `gpu-fault.io/cleanup-action` | `delete/namespace/reset` | Defaults to `delete`; objects such as the NLB that are deleted only on reset write `reset` explicitly |
| `gpu-fault.io/deploy-mode` | `regional_rollout/regional_reconciler` | Filled in only for GPU Deployments that need the regional orchestrator to apply them automatically |

ServiceAccount, Role, RoleBinding, ClusterRole, ClusterRoleBinding, PDB and ordinary
Service are derived as `support/delete` by default and need no repeated annotation. ConfigMap, Secret
and Namespace are covered by default by the final namespace deletion; declare the lifecycle explicitly only when early deletion in the `clean` phase is needed.

`regional_rollout` in the example above makes the regional orchestrator automatically read the same source file, replace the wheel/image/
namespace placeholders, apply and wait for the Deployment rollout. If the component is deployed only by a separate switch,
omit `deploy-mode`, but the cleanup registry still includes it automatically.

#### Example: Modifying a CPU Control-Plane Role

To modify the ingress common template, edit:

```text
deploy/control-plane/base/control-plane-deployment.yaml
```

For regional differences edit:

```text
deploy/control-plane/regional/regional-control-plane-patch.yaml
```

For the derivation logic of the control worker/spool-worker edit:

```text
deploy/control-plane/tools/render_control_plane_role_split.py
```

The base Deployment declares `cleanup-phase: ingress`; the renderer automatically writes the
`consumer` phase and a stable order for `gpu-fault-control-worker` and `gpu-fault-telemetry-spool-worker`.
Do not add annotations in the generated files, because the next render overwrites them.

#### Complete Developer Change Steps

When adding, deleting or modifying a solution component, developers proceed in this order; changing only Python code or only online
objects is not acceptable:

1. **Determine the component's ownership and lifecycle.** Decide whether it runs on the CPU or GPU EKS, and whether it is
   ingress, consumer, auxiliary, producer or executor; determine the stop order within the phase,
   namespace/cluster scope, RBAC and rollback method.
2. **Modify the source code and source Manifests.** GPU components add or modify YAML under `deploy/dataplane/`;
   CPU components modify the base, regional patch or renderer. New resources must use a stable
   `metadata.name`; the release SHA must not be written into the resource name.
3. **Complete the runtime constraints.** Deployments/DaemonSets/CronJobs must include resources, probes,
   termination grace, security context, ServiceAccount/RBAC and the
   `gpu-fault.io/cleanup-*` annotations defined in this section. GPU Deployments that need the regional orchestrator to apply them
   additionally declare `deploy-mode`.
4. **Auto-generate the derived files.** From the repository root run:

   ```bash
   make PYTHON=.venv/bin/python deployment-contracts-update
   ```

   The `regional/generated/` or
   `cleanup-inventory.json` produced by that command must not be patched by hand.
5. **Review the generated result.** Check the images, envFrom, replicas, PDBs,
   cleanup phase/order of the generated manifests, and the kind/name/scope/action in the cleanup inventory. When the generator
   reports an unknown Kind, a missing annotation or a conflict, fix the source Manifest; do not bypass it by modifying the generated result.
6. **Run the read-only contracts and the complete quality gate:**

   ```bash
   make PYTHON=.venv/bin/python deployment-contracts-check
   make PYTHON=.venv/bin/python check
   ```

7. **Do a real release and rollback in an approved staging window.** Using staging's `site.yaml` run the bound
   `gpu-fault-admin deploy` (which contains preflight/verify internally), accept with `status --full`, then rehearse rollback with
   `deploy --rollback` or the checked low-level rollback flow; confirm the new component is Ready, enters
   `gpu-fault-installed-resources`, the dry-run cleanup order is correct, and actually verify one rollback.
8. **Commit all artefacts in the same change.** Source code, source Manifests, auto-generated files, tests and documentation must be in
   the same PR; after CI is fully green build one release, and administrators/customers receive only that release, not scattered
   YAML.

#### One Command Completes the Update

After the source Manifest changes are complete, run only:

```bash
make PYTHON=.venv/bin/python deployment-contracts-update
```

The command automatically performs in order:

1. Re-renders `regional/generated/`;
2. Scans the lifecycle annotations of the production Manifests;
3. Rewrites the build-time candidate inventory `cleanup-inventory.json`;
4. Validates the three-role configuration, the generated allowlist and the deploy directory boundary;
5. Runs the renderer and cleanup inventory contract tests.

Before committing you can run the read-only mode to confirm no generated file was forgotten:

```bash
make PYTHON=.venv/bin/python deployment-contracts-check
```

CI runs the same `--check` entry point automatically. It fails when a source Manifest adds a running resource without
`cleanup-phase`, annotation values conflict, generated output is stale, the registry is stale or
`deploy-mode` has no unique source file. Developers no longer hand-edit
`cleanup-inventory.json`; it and `regional/generated/` are both generated artefacts. On the first deployment,
`apply-control-plane-role-split.sh` automatically creates the CPU
`gpu-fault-installed-resources`, and the node Installer/Reconciler deployment entry point automatically creates the GPU
registry; upgrades scan live objects and merge-refresh, without requiring administrators to maintain resource names.
## Hard Constraints on Client Identity and Migration Switches

- All uvicorn processes must explicitly use `--no-proxy-headers`;
  `--proxy-headers`, `--forwarded-allow-ips` and
  `FORWARDED_ALLOW_IPS` are forbidden. Processor replay and the in-Pod metrics bypass trust only
  the direct peer address of the TCP socket.
- `GPU_FAULT_NODE_ACTION_RESULT_SIGNATURE_REQUIRED=false` is allowed only for a short
  migration, and at the same time
  `GPU_FAULT_NODE_ACTION_RESULT_SIGNATURE_MIGRATION_RELEASE_ID` matching the current `GPU_FAULT_RELEASE_ID` must be set, as well as
  a `GPU_FAULT_NODE_ACTION_RESULT_SIGNATURE_MIGRATION_EXPIRES_AT`
  no more than 14 days out.
- By default `/metrics` exposes only the collector aggregates, the fixed Top-N and
  `gpu_fault_collector_metrics_snapshot_age_seconds`. That metric family is exported only by
  `GPU_FAULT_SERVICE_ROLE=all/ingress`; a custom scrape configuration must include ingress,
  and worker replicas do not export the collector gauges.
- `/v1/internal/metrics/collector-silence` provides worst-node details with the same cap as the fixed Top-N,
  must use the execution token, and must not be added to the AMP default scrape.
- A non-regional active executor is only for Canary/transition and must explicitly set
  `GPU_FAULT_ALLOW_SINGLE_CLUSTER=true`; without that confirmation it refuses to start.

<a id="5-旧版-hyperpod-单集群过渡部署非生产目标"></a>
## Appendix A. Legacy HyperPod Single Cluster (Non-Production)

> **Maturity warning: this chapter is not the current recommended production path.** The control plane and the GPU data plane share one EKS,
> lacking regional mode's independent trust boundary, per-cluster tokens, remote Executor and multi-cluster isolation, and it has not
> passed complete capacity, rolling upgrade and fault-domain acceptance at the same level as the regional form. Use it only to maintain historical environments,
> prepare for migration, run a single-cluster Canary or validate code paths. New production environments must use the regionally separated deployment.

> **Uninstall warning: the resources created by this script are not in the installation resource registry.** It does not write `bootstrap-state.json`,
> nor any registry record, while `gpu-fault-admin uninstall` only uninstalls sites established by `gpu-fault-admin deploy`
> with a `site.yaml` under `--state-dir`, so the deletion-protected Aurora cluster
> `${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}`, its writer/reader, the same-named DB subnet group and Security Group and
> the IRSA roles can only be deleted by hand; the Secret `<namespace>/gpu-fault-aurora` records which cluster it is --
> **do not delete this Secret first**. The cluster parameter group `${AURORA_CLUSTER_ID}-pg` belongs to this stack only while it is still the parameter group
> currently attached to the cluster; if the cluster is still on the engine default group, do not touch the same-named group.
> The script prints the same warning to stderr both at the start of `ensure_aurora` and in the final summary.

<a id="51-设置参数"></a>
### A.1 Setting Parameters

```bash
export AWS_REGION='<aws-region>'
export EKS_CLUSTER_NAME='<EKS cluster name>'
export HYPERPOD_CLUSTER_NAME='<HyperPod cluster name>'
export GPU_FAULT_NAMESPACE=gpu-fault-system
export GPU_FAULT_RUNTIME_PROFILE=hyperpod-v1
export RUN_E2E=true
export GPU_FAULT_DEFAULT_RESTART_BUDGET=1
export GPU_FAULT_MULTI_NODE_AGGREGATION_WINDOW_SECONDS=5
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_DRAIN_MAX_WAIT_SECONDS=30
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_EXIT_ON_DEADLINE=true
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_EXIT_GRACE_SECONDS=5
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_WORKERS=24
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_MAX_QUEUE_DEPTH=10000
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_MAX_CLUSTER_QUEUE_DEPTH=1000
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_RETRY_AFTER_SECONDS=2
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_CLEANUP_INTERVAL_SECONDS=60
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_COMPLETED_RETENTION_SECONDS=600
export GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_CLEANUP_BATCH_SIZE=1000
export GPU_FAULT_STORE_IO_WORKERS=8
export GPU_FAULT_STORE_IO_MAX_IN_FLIGHT=64
export GPU_FAULT_STORE_IO_ADMISSION_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=2
export GPU_FAULT_POSTGRES_POOL_MIN_SIZE=1
export GPU_FAULT_POSTGRES_POOL_MAX_SIZE=8
export GPU_FAULT_POSTGRES_POOL_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=2
export GPU_FAULT_DCGM_EXPORTER_MODE=auto
export GPU_FAULT_DCGM_EDGE_FILTER_ENABLED=true
export GPU_FAULT_DCGM_HEALTH_SUMMARY_SECONDS=300
export GPU_FAULT_DCGM_EDGE_CONFIRMATION_SAMPLES=3
export GPU_FAULT_DCGM_HISTORY_MAX_POINTS=20
export GPU_FAULT_HOST_EDGE_FILTER_ENABLED=true
export GPU_FAULT_HOST_HEALTH_SUMMARY_SECONDS=300
export GPU_FAULT_HOST_HISTORY_MAX_POINTS=20
# NVIDIA DCGM Health defaults; may be overridden per site baseline
export GPU_FAULT_PCIE_REPLAY_RATE_WARNING_PER_MINUTE=8
export GPU_FAULT_NVLINK_ERROR_DELTA_CRITICAL=1
# The following fallbacks are used when reading device thresholds fails
export GPU_FAULT_GPU_TEMP_WARNING_C=85
export GPU_FAULT_GPU_TEMP_CRITICAL_C=90
export GPU_FAULT_MEMORY_TEMP_WARNING_C=90
export GPU_FAULT_MEMORY_TEMP_CRITICAL_C=95
export GPU_FAULT_GPU_TEMP_WARNING_MARGIN_C=5
export GPU_FAULT_GPU_TEMP_SHUTDOWN_MARGIN_C=3
export GPU_FAULT_MEMORY_TEMP_WARNING_MARGIN_C=5
export GPU_FAULT_THERMAL_VIOLATION_DRAIN_CONSECUTIVE_SAMPLES=2
# existing mode may use a node address template:
# export GPU_FAULT_DCGM_METRICS_URL='http://{node_ip}:9400/metrics'
export GPU_FAULT_NVSWITCH_TOPOLOGY_MAX_AGE_SECONDS=300
# On platforms such as A100 that may have trunks, configure after installing the site query adapter:
# export GPU_FAULT_NVSWITCH_TOPOLOGY_COMMAND="/path/to/site-topology-query --json"

# Aurora, adjustable by capacity
export AURORA_CLUSTER_ID=gpu-fault-aurora
export AURORA_MIN_ACU=8
export AURORA_MAX_ACU=32
export AURORA_ENGINE_VERSION=16.8

# Managed recovery observation; recommended to keep in production
export GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_MANAGED_OBSERVER=true
export GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_IDENTITY_REFRESH_SECONDS=20
export GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_MANAGED_RECOVERY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS=1800
export GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_POST_REBOOT_STABILIZATION_SECONDS=60

# Automatic spare node switching disabled by default
export GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_SPARE_FAILOVER=false
```

Parameter description:

| Parameter | Default | Configuration advice |
|---|---:|---|
| `RUN_E2E` | `true` | true for the first deployment; may be turned off as needed for change verification |
| `AURORA_MIN_ACU` | `8` | Raise per stress test for high-frequency metrics or concentrated reconnect scenarios |
| `AURORA_MAX_ACU` | `32` | Determined by stress testing against node count, event volume and SLO |
| `AURORA_ENGINE_VERSION` | `16.8` | Must be an Aurora PostgreSQL version supported in the target region |
| `GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_IDENTITY_REFRESH_SECONDS` | `20` | Should be less than the Agent stale window |
| `GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_MANAGED_RECOVERY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` | `1800` | Covers the actual node replacement duration; it is also used as the per-step wait cap of those two actions, so adjusting it adjusts the cap too |
| `GPU_FAULT_HYPERPOD_POST_REBOOT_STABILIZATION_SECONDS` | `60` | After the new boot, Agent and HyperPod Running, keep waiting for kubelet, GPU and EFA device plugin registration to settle before resuming training |
| `GPU_FAULT_ENABLE_HYPERPOD_SPARE_FAILOVER` | `false` | Enable only after spare labels, capacity and drills are ready |
| `GPU_FAULT_DEFAULT_RESTART_BUDGET` | `1` | Cluster default; a per-job annotation may override |
| `GPU_FAULT_MULTI_NODE_AGGREGATION_WINDOW_SECONDS` | `5` | Initial aggregation wait for multi-node recovery of the same job/attempt and cross-type DCGM metric, XID, SXID recovery; `0` disables |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_DRAIN_MAX_WAIT_SECONDS` | `30` | Maximum time to wait for the same GPU cluster's processor queue to drain after the aggregation window expires; released on expiry to avoid workflow starvation by continuous telemetry |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_WORKERS` | deploy.sh `24`; bare application default `4` | Number of parallel lanes per active consumer process; fault/workflow changes are serialised per cluster, periodic telemetry is parallel per cluster, node and endpoint. Also the default source for the four pools `PROCESSOR_FAULT_WORKERS` / `_OBSERVATION_WORKERS` / `_GPU_TELEMETRY_WORKERS` / `_HOST_TELEMETRY_WORKERS` when not set explicitly (each takes `//4`). **These variables take effect only in the worker tier that consumes the queue**: the thread pools are created by `run_processor`, and with `SERVICE_ROLE=ingress` that thread does not start, so setting them on ingress neither takes effect nor should be counted in capacity |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_REQUEST_LEASE_SECONDS` | `120` (deploy.sh does not set it, taking the bare application default; regional manifest ingress/spool 30, worker 150) | Active lane lease cap; the actual lease does not exceed the remaining execution deadline of this request |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_REQUEST_RENEW_SECONDS` | `5` | Renewal interval of an executing active lane; must be less than the request lease |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_REQUEST_MAX_EXECUTION_SECONDS` | `30` (deploy.sh does not set it, taking the bare application default; regional manifest ingress/spool 20, worker 120) | Hard deadline of a single processing; a timed-out result is dropped, claiming new requests stops, and after the lane expires another Pod takes over with fencing |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_RETRYABLE_RESPONSE_MAX_AGE_SECONDS` | `300` | Maximum request age for releasing the request/lane and retrying when internal replay returns 408/425/429/5xx; beyond that age the terminal response is kept, avoiding a permanent poison queue |
| `GPU_FAULT_LIFESPAN_SHUTDOWN_MAX_SECONDS` | ingress `20` / worker `130` / spool-worker `30` | Exit deadline shared by all background threads; the worker value covers a 120-second legitimate processor request, the 5-second deadline exit grace and 5 seconds of wrap-up. The corresponding termination grace is 120/240/130 seconds respectively |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_GPU_INVENTORY_STALE_SECONDS` | `180` | PENDING GPU inventory older than this is explicitly completed as `STALE_SUPERSEDED`, no longer executing policy with an old device mapping |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_HEALTH_SUMMARY_STALE_SECONDS` | `420` | Applies only to GPU/Host health summaries without edges/errors; anomaly, recovery and fault events are not dropped by this TTL |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_OBSERVATION_STALE_SECONDS` | `120` | When a Workload Observation is older than this and the control plane has saved a newer snapshot, the old request terminates as `STALE_SUPERSEDED` |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_TRAINING_PROGRESS_STALE_SECONDS` | `120` | When Training Progress is older than this and the same rank already has a newer heartbeat, the old request terminates as `STALE_SUPERSEDED` |
| `GPU_FAULT_FAULT_ACTION_MAX_AGE_SECONDS` | `900` | Maximum age for automatic execution of XID/SXID destructive actions; when over-age or the source boot differs from the current Agent, only evidence is frozen and the workflow is BLOCKED, and the event is still kept for audit |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_EXIT_ON_DEADLINE` | `true` | After the processor exceeds the hard deadline the current process exits and Kubernetes recreates the Pod |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_EXIT_GRACE_SECONDS` | `5` | Wrap-up time before the hard exit after the deadline fires; still bound by the total budget of the worker lifespan and Kubernetes termination grace |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_MAX_QUEUE_DEPTH` | `10000` | Hard cap on unfinished regional processor requests; new requests return 429 once reached |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_MAX_CLUSTER_QUEUE_DEPTH` | `1000` | Hard cap on unfinished requests of a single GPU cluster, preventing one cluster from filling the regional queue |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_FAULT_RESERVED_QUEUE_DEPTH` | `4000` | Capacity reserved in the global queue for priority=0 fault events; covers a fault storm produced simultaneously by 8 clusters of 2048 cards with margin |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_FAULT_RESERVED_CLUSTER_DEPTH` | `400` | Capacity reserved per cluster for priority=0 fault events; covers simultaneous XIDs on a 256-node cluster with margin |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_RETRY_AFTER_SECONDS` | `2` | `Retry-After` seconds of 429 and 503 (store I/O / decode capacity, request deadline) responses |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_MAX_REQUEST_BYTES` | `16777216` | Hard cap on the processor enqueue request body; over the cap returns 413 without writing Aurora |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_ADMISSION_BATCH_SIZE` | `64` | Server-side admission micro-batch cap Ingress applies to priority=100 periodic Telemetry; Faults do not enter that batch |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_ADMISSION_BATCH_DELAY_SECONDS` | `0.002` | Maximum wait of the Telemetry admission micro-batch; clients are not required to use the batch API |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_CLEANUP_INTERVAL_SECONDS` | `60` | Cleanup period of the completed queue guarded by the separate `processor-cleanup` periodic task lease |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_COMPLETED_RETENTION_SECONDS` | `600` | Retention of completed responses; must exceed the maximum wait of synchronous control requests |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_CLEANUP_BATCH_SIZE` | `1000` | Cap on completed requests deleted per run by `updated_at` |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_RECEIPT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` | `120` | Cap on the Completion Watcher waiting for the final receipt of the asynchronous failure/terminal processor |
| `GPU_FAULT_PROCESSOR_RECEIPT_POLL_SECONDS` | `0.25` | Initial receipt polling interval; the client backs off exponentially up to 2 seconds |
| `GPU_FAULT_WATCHER_RECONCILE_DEBOUNCE_SECONDS` | `0.5` | Debounce window merging Pod watch events per attempt, avoiding O(N²) full Observation resends for large jobs |
| `GPU_FAULT_COLLECTOR_STARTUP_SPREAD_SECONDS` | `15` | Stable stagger window for the first GPU/Host collection after an ordinary Collector starts/restarts; the phase is determined by a cluster/node/channel hash |
| `GPU_FAULT_STORE_IO_WORKERS` | `8` | Bounded number of threads per API process executing synchronous Aurora/store transactions |
